"""Spec compiler — converts legacy YAML/JSON specs to Markdown.

The legacy spec format (v0.1.x) uses a ``sections`` array to describe
document structure.  This module compiles that format into Markdown with
YAML frontmatter so the rest of the pipeline only needs to deal with
Markdown.
"""

import json
import logging
from pathlib import Path

import yaml

logger = logging.getLogger(__name__)


class SpecCompileError(Exception):
    """Raised when a spec cannot be compiled to Markdown."""


def compile_spec(spec: dict) -> str:
    """Compile a legacy spec dictionary to Markdown with YAML frontmatter.

    Args:
        spec: A document specification dict (as loaded from YAML/JSON).

    Returns:
        A Markdown string with YAML frontmatter.
    """
    parts: list[str] = []

    # --- Build frontmatter ---------------------------------------------------
    fm: dict = {}
    for key in ("title", "subtitle", "author", "date"):
        if key in spec:
            fm[key] = spec[key]

    will_meta: dict = {}
    if spec.get("page_size"):
        will_meta["page_size"] = spec["page_size"]
    if spec.get("margins"):
        will_meta["margins"] = spec["margins"]
    if spec.get("header"):
        will_meta["header"] = spec["header"]
    if spec.get("footer"):
        will_meta["footer"] = spec["footer"]
    if spec.get("table_of_contents"):
        will_meta["table_of_contents"] = True
    if spec.get("template"):
        will_meta["template"] = spec["template"]

    if will_meta:
        fm["will"] = will_meta

    if fm:
        parts.append("---")
        parts.append(yaml.dump(fm, default_flow_style=False, allow_unicode=True).rstrip())
        parts.append("---")
        parts.append("")

    # --- Compile sections -----------------------------------------------------
    for section in spec.get("sections", []):
        md = _compile_section(section)
        if md:
            parts.append(md)

    return "\n".join(parts) + "\n"


def compile_spec_file(path: str | Path) -> str:
    """Load a YAML or JSON spec file and compile to Markdown.

    Args:
        path: Path to a ``.yaml``, ``.yml``, or ``.json`` file.

    Returns:
        Compiled Markdown string.

    Raises:
        SpecCompileError: If the file cannot be read or parsed.
    """
    path = Path(path)
    try:
        text = path.read_text(encoding="utf-8")
    except OSError as exc:
        raise SpecCompileError(f"Cannot read spec file: {exc}") from exc

    try:
        if path.suffix.lower() in (".yaml", ".yml"):
            spec = yaml.safe_load(text)
        else:
            spec = json.loads(text)
    except Exception as exc:
        raise SpecCompileError(f"Cannot parse spec file: {exc}") from exc

    if not isinstance(spec, dict):
        raise SpecCompileError("Spec must be a YAML/JSON object (dict)")

    return compile_spec(spec)


# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Section compilers
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

def _compile_section(section: dict) -> str:
    """Compile a single spec section dict to Markdown."""
    section_type = section.get("type", "content")
    compiler = _SECTION_COMPILERS.get(section_type)
    if compiler is None:
        logger.warning("Unknown section type '%s', skipping", section_type)
        return ""
    return compiler(section)


def _heading(sec: dict) -> str:
    level = sec.get("level", 1)
    title = sec.get("title", "")
    prefix = "#" * level
    return f"{prefix} {title}\n"


def _section(sec: dict) -> str:
    parts: list[str] = []
    if sec.get("page_break"):
        parts.append("\\newpage\n")
    title = sec.get("title", "")
    parts.append(f"# {title}\n")
    if sec.get("subtitle"):
        parts.append(f"*{sec['subtitle']}*\n")
    return "\n".join(parts)


def _content(sec: dict) -> str:
    parts: list[str] = []
    if sec.get("title"):
        level = sec.get("level", 2)
        prefix = "#" * level
        parts.append(f"{prefix} {sec['title']}\n")
    if sec.get("text"):
        parts.append(sec["text"].rstrip() + "\n")
    if sec.get("bullets"):
        for item in sec["bullets"]:
            parts.append(f"- {item}")
        parts.append("")
    if sec.get("numbered"):
        for i, item in enumerate(sec["numbered"], 1):
            parts.append(f"{i}. {item}")
        parts.append("")
    return "\n".join(parts)


def _table(sec: dict) -> str:
    parts: list[str] = []
    if sec.get("title"):
        level = sec.get("level", 2)
        prefix = "#" * level
        parts.append(f"{prefix} {sec['title']}\n")

    headers: list[str] = sec.get("headers", [])
    data: list[list] = sec.get("data", [])

    if headers:
        parts.append("| " + " | ".join(str(h) for h in headers) + " |")
        parts.append("| " + " | ".join("---" for _ in headers) + " |")

    for row in data:
        parts.append("| " + " | ".join(str(c) for c in row) + " |")

    parts.append("")
    return "\n".join(parts)


def _image(sec: dict) -> str:
    path = sec.get("path", sec.get("image", ""))
    caption = sec.get("caption", sec.get("title", ""))
    width = sec.get("width")
    md = f"![{caption}]({path})"
    if width:
        md += f"{{ width={width}in }}"
    return md + "\n"


def _quote(sec: dict) -> str:
    text = sec.get("text", "")
    author = sec.get("author")
    parts = [f'> "{text}"']
    if author:
        parts.append(f"> --- {author}")
    parts.append("")
    return "\n".join(parts)


def _code(sec: dict) -> str:
    parts: list[str] = []
    if sec.get("title"):
        level = sec.get("level", 3)
        prefix = "#" * level
        parts.append(f"{prefix} {sec['title']}\n")
    language = sec.get("language", "")
    code = sec.get("code", sec.get("text", ""))
    parts.append(f"```{language}")
    parts.append(code.rstrip())
    parts.append("```\n")
    return "\n".join(parts)


def _page_break(_sec: dict) -> str:
    return "\\newpage\n"


def _horizontal_line(_sec: dict) -> str:
    return "---\n"


_SECTION_COMPILERS = {
    "heading": _heading,
    "section": _section,
    "content": _content,
    "table": _table,
    "image": _image,
    "quote": _quote,
    "code": _code,
    "page_break": _page_break,
    "horizontal_line": _horizontal_line,
}

File created successfully at: C:\Users\ydebray\Downloads\schopenhauer\src\will\spec_compiler.py

Assistant2026-02-22T22:37:26.088Z


📝 Write C:\Users\ydebray\Downloads\schopenhauer\src\will\pandoc_backend.py


C:\Users\ydebray\Downloads\schopenhauer\src\will\pandoc_backend.py


"""Pandoc backend — thin wrapper around pypandoc.

All actual document rendering goes through Pandoc.  This module provides
a clean interface for the rest of Schopenhauer to call into Pandoc without
knowing the details of pypandoc's API.
"""

import logging
import tempfile
from pathlib import Path

import pypandoc

logger = logging.getLogger(__name__)


class PandocBackendError(Exception):
    """Raised when a Pandoc conversion fails."""


# Format aliases: map short names to Pandoc format identifiers
_FORMAT_ALIASES: dict[str, str] = {
    "docx": "docx",
    "pdf": "pdf",
    "html": "html",
    "pptx": "pptx",
    "epub": "epub",
    "latex": "latex",
    "tex": "latex",
    "odt": "odt",
    "rtf": "rtf",
    "md": "markdown",
    "markdown": "markdown",
}


def _resolve_format(fmt: str) -> str:
    return _FORMAT_ALIASES.get(fmt.lower(), fmt)


def convert(
    source: str,
    output_path: str | Path,
    output_format: str = "docx",
    reference_doc: str | Path | None = None,
    extra_args: list[str] | None = None,
    metadata: dict | None = None,
) -> Path:
    """Convert a Markdown string to the target format via Pandoc.

    Args:
        source: Markdown content (string).
        output_path: Destination file path.
        output_format: Target format (``docx``, ``pdf``, ``html``, etc.).
        reference_doc: Path to a Pandoc reference doc (``.docx``).
        extra_args: Additional CLI arguments forwarded to Pandoc.
        metadata: Key-value metadata pairs passed via ``-M key=value``.

    Returns:
        The resolved output ``Path``.

    Raises:
        PandocBackendError: If Pandoc returns a non-zero exit code.
    """
    output_path = Path(output_path)
    fmt = _resolve_format(output_format)

    args: list[str] = list(extra_args or [])
    args.append("--standalone")

    # Format-specific defaults
    if fmt == "pdf":
        if not any(a.startswith("--pdf-engine") for a in args):
            args.append("--pdf-engine=xelatex")
    if fmt == "html":
        if not any(a.startswith("--self-contained") or a == "--embed-resources" for a in args):
            args.append("--embed-resources")

    if reference_doc:
        args.extend(["--reference-doc", str(reference_doc)])

    if metadata:
        for key, value in metadata.items():
            args.extend(["-M", f"{key}={value}"])

    try:
        pypandoc.convert_text(
            source,
            fmt,
            format="markdown",
            outputfile=str(output_path),
            extra_args=args,
        )
    except RuntimeError as exc:
        raise PandocBackendError(f"Pandoc conversion failed: {exc}") from exc

    return output_path


def convert_to_bytes(
    source: str,
    output_format: str = "docx",
    reference_doc: str | Path | None = None,
    extra_args: list[str] | None = None,
    metadata: dict | None = None,
) -> bytes:
    """Convert Markdown to the target format and return raw bytes.

    Same parameters as :func:`convert` but returns ``bytes`` instead of
    writing to a file.
    """
    suffix = f".{output_format}" if not output_format.startswith(".") else output_format
    with tempfile.NamedTemporaryFile(suffix=suffix, delete=False) as tmp:
        tmp_path = Path(tmp.name)

    try:
        convert(
            source,
            tmp_path,
            output_format=output_format,
            reference_doc=reference_doc,
            extra_args=extra_args,
            metadata=metadata,
        )
        return tmp_path.read_bytes()
    finally:
        tmp_path.unlink(missing_ok=True)


def get_pandoc_version() -> str:
    """Return the Pandoc version string."""
    return pypandoc.get_pandoc_version()


def get_supported_formats() -> dict[str, list[str]]:
    """Return a dict with ``input`` and ``output`` format lists."""
    return {
        "input": pypandoc.get_pandoc_formats()[0],
        "output": pypandoc.get_pandoc_formats()[1],

"""Render pipeline — the main entry point for document generation.

Orchestrates: input detection → spec compilation → frontmatter extraction →
variable preprocessing → Pandoc conversion.
"""

import logging
import re
from pathlib import Path

import yaml

from will.pandoc_backend import convert as pandoc_convert
from will.pandoc_backend import convert_to_bytes as pandoc_convert_to_bytes
from will.preprocess import preprocess
from will.spec_compiler import compile_spec_file

logger = logging.getLogger(__name__)

# Where Pandoc reference docs are located (relative to the package root).
_TEMPLATES_DIR = Path(__file__).resolve().parent.parent.parent / "templates"

# Regex for YAML frontmatter delimiters (leading ``---``)
_FRONTMATTER_RE = re.compile(r"\A---\n(.*?\n)---\n", re.DOTALL)


class RenderError(Exception):
    """Raised when the render pipeline fails."""


def render(
    source: str | Path,
    output: str | Path | None = None,
    format: str | None = None,
    template: str | None = None,
    variables: dict | None = None,
    backend: str = "pandoc",
) -> Path | bytes:
    """Render a document from Markdown or a legacy spec file.

    Args:
        source: Path to a ``.md``, ``.yaml``, ``.yml``, or ``.json`` file,
            or a raw Markdown string (detected by the absence of a file
            extension on disk).
        output: Destination file path.  When *None*, raw bytes are returned.
        format: Output format (``docx``, ``pdf``, ``html``, …).
            Inferred from *output* extension when omitted.
        template: Template name (resolved to ``templates/reference-docs/<name>.docx``)
            or an absolute/relative path to a ``.docx`` reference doc.
        variables: Extra variables for ``{{var}}`` substitution.
        backend: ``"pandoc"`` (default) or ``"legacy"`` (python-docx).

    Returns:
        The output ``Path`` when *output* is given, otherwise ``bytes``.

    Raises:
        RenderError: On any failure in the pipeline.
    """
    variables = dict(variables or {})

    # --- Legacy backend shortcut ---------------------------------------------
    if backend == "legacy":
        return _render_legacy(source, output, variables)

    # --- Determine Markdown content ------------------------------------------
    source_path = Path(source) if not isinstance(source, Path) else source

    if source_path.is_file():
        suffix = source_path.suffix.lower()
        if suffix in (".yaml", ".yml", ".json"):
            markdown = compile_spec_file(source_path)
        else:
            markdown = source_path.read_text(encoding="utf-8")
    elif isinstance(source, str) and not Path(source).exists():
        # Treat as raw Markdown string
        markdown = source
    else:
        raise RenderError(f"Source not found: {source}")

    # --- Extract frontmatter -------------------------------------------------
    frontmatter, body = _extract_frontmatter(markdown)

    # --- Merge variables -----------------------------------------------------
    will_meta = frontmatter.get("will", {}) or {}
    fm_vars = will_meta.get("variables", {}) or {}
    merged_vars = {**fm_vars, **variables}

    # --- Preprocess ----------------------------------------------------------
    body = preprocess(body, merged_vars)

    # --- Resolve output format -----------------------------------------------
    if format is None and output is not None:
        format = Path(output).suffix.lstrip(".")
    if format is None:
        format = "docx"

    # --- Resolve template / reference doc ------------------------------------
    template_name = template or will_meta.get("template")
    reference_doc = _resolve_template(template_name, format)

    # --- Rebuild full Markdown (frontmatter + processed body) ----------------
    final_md = _rebuild_markdown(frontmatter, body)

    # --- Convert via Pandoc --------------------------------------------------
    try:
        if output is not None:
            return pandoc_convert(
                final_md,
                output_path=output,
                output_format=format,
                reference_doc=reference_doc,
            )
        else:
            return pandoc_convert_to_bytes(
                final_md,
                output_format=format,
                reference_doc=reference_doc,
            )
    except Exception as exc:
        raise RenderError(f"Render failed: {exc}") from exc


# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Internal helpers
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

def _extract_frontmatter(markdown: str) -> tuple[dict, str]:
    """Split YAML frontmatter from the Markdown body.

    Returns:
        ``(frontmatter_dict, body_string)``
    """
    match = _FRONTMATTER_RE.match(markdown)
    if not match:
        return {}, markdown

    raw_fm = match.group(1)
    body = markdown[match.end():]

    try:
        fm = yaml.safe_load(raw_fm) or {}
    except yaml.YAMLError:
        logger.warning("Invalid YAML frontmatter, ignoring")
        fm = {}

    return fm, body


def _resolve_template(name: str | None, format: str) -> Path | None:
    """Map a template *name* to a reference-doc path.

    Resolution order:
    1. If *name* is ``None``, return ``None`` (Pandoc defaults).
    2. If *name* is an existing file path, use it directly.
    3. Look up ``templates/reference-docs/<name>.docx`` (for docx format).
    """
    if name is None:
        return None

    # Direct path?
    p = Path(name)
    if p.is_file():
        return p

    # Only docx templates for now
    if format == "docx":
        candidate = _TEMPLATES_DIR / "reference-docs" / f"{name}.docx"
        if candidate.is_file():
            return candidate

    return None


def _rebuild_markdown(frontmatter: dict, body: str) -> str:
    """Reconstruct Markdown with YAML frontmatter for Pandoc.

    Only standard Pandoc fields are forwarded (title, subtitle, author, date,
    lang, …).  The ``will:`` namespace is stripped because Pandoc doesn't
    understand it.
    """
    # Fields that Pandoc understands natively
    pandoc_keys = {"title", "subtitle", "author", "date", "lang", "abstract", "keywords"}
    pandoc_fm = {k: v for k, v in frontmatter.items() if k in pandoc_keys}

    if pandoc_fm:
        header = "---\n" + yaml.dump(pandoc_fm, default_flow_style=False, allow_unicode=True).rstrip() + "\n---\n\n"
    else:
        header = ""

    return header + body


def _render_legacy(
    source: str | Path,
    output: str | Path | None,
    variables: dict,
) -> Path | bytes:
    """Fallback to the python-docx legacy backend."""
    import json as _json

    from will.core import WordDocument

    source_path = Path(source)
    if source_path.suffix.lower() in (".yaml", ".yml"):
        doc = WordDocument.from_yaml(str(source_path))
    elif source_path.suffix.lower() == ".json":
        doc = WordDocument.from_json(str(source_path))
    else:
        raise RenderError(
            "Legacy backend only supports .yaml/.json specs, not Markdown."
        )

    if variables:
        doc.replace_placeholders(variables)

    if output is not None:
        doc.save(output)
        return Path(output)
    return doc.to_bytes()

# =============================================================================
# RENDER COMMAND (v0.2+ primary command)
# =============================================================================


@main.command()
@click.argument("input_file", type=click.Path(exists=True))
@click.option("--output", "-o", required=True, help="Output file path")
@click.option(
    "--format",
    "-f",
    "formats",
    multiple=True,
    help="Output format(s): docx, pdf, html, pptx, epub, latex. "
    "Can be specified multiple times for multi-format output. "
    "Inferred from output extension if not given.",
)
@click.option("--template", "-t", help="Template name or path to .docx reference doc")
@click.option("--var", "-V", multiple=True, help="Variable substitution (KEY=VALUE)")
@click.option(
    "--backend",
    type=click.Choice(["pandoc", "legacy"]),
    default="pandoc",
    help="Rendering backend (default: pandoc)",
)
def render(
    input_file: str,
    output: str,
    formats: tuple,
    template: Optional[str],
    var: tuple,
    backend: str,
):
    """
    Render a Markdown or YAML/JSON spec to one or more output formats.

    This is the primary document generation command (v0.2+).

    \b
    Examples:
        will render report.md -o report.docx
        will render report.md -o report.pdf -f pdf
        will render report.md -o outputs/report -f docx -f pdf -f html
        will render spec.yaml -o report.docx --backend legacy
        will render report.md -o report.docx -V COMPANY="Acme" -V YEAR=2024
        will render report.md -o report.docx -t report
    """
    from will.render import render as do_render

    # Parse variables
    variables = {}
    for v in var:
        if "=" in v:
            key, value = v.split("=", 1)
            variables[key] = value

    try:
        output_path = Path(output)

        if formats:
            # Multi-format: generate one file per format
            for fmt in formats:
                if len(formats) > 1:
                    out = output_path.parent / f"{output_path.stem}.{fmt}"
                else:
                    out = output_path
                do_render(
                    source=input_file,
                    output=str(out),
                    format=fmt,
                    template=template,
                    variables=variables,
                    backend=backend,
                )
                click.echo(click.style(f"  Rendered: {out}", fg="green"))
        else:
            # Single format (inferred from extension)
            do_render(
                source=input_file,
                output=str(output_path),
                template=template,
                variables=variables,
                backend=backend,
            )
            click.echo(click.style(f"  Rendered: {output_path}", fg="green"))

    except Exception as e:
        click.echo(click.style(f"Error rendering document: {e}", fg="red"), err=True)
        sys.exit(1)


# =============================================================================
# CREATE COMMAND
# =============================================================================

"""Tests for the preprocessing engine."""

import pytest

from will.preprocess import PreprocessError, preprocess


class TestPreprocess:
    """Tests for variable substitution."""

    def test_no_variables(self):
        assert preprocess("Hello world") == "Hello world"

    def test_simple_substitution(self):
        result = preprocess("Hello {{name}}", {"name": "World"})
        assert result == "Hello World"

    def test_multiple_variables(self):
        result = preprocess(
            "{{greeting}}, {{name}}!",
            {"greeting": "Hi", "name": "Alice"},
        )
        assert result == "Hi, Alice!"

    def test_unresolved_passthrough(self):
        """Unresolved variables should pass through as {{name}}."""
        result = preprocess("Hello {{unknown}}")
        assert result == "Hello {{unknown}}"

    def test_partial_resolution(self):
        """Resolved vars replaced, unresolved vars kept."""
        result = preprocess(
            "{{resolved}} and {{unresolved}}",
            {"resolved": "YES"},
        )
        assert result == "YES and {{unresolved}}"

    def test_empty_content(self):
        assert preprocess("") == ""

    def test_none_variables(self):
        result = preprocess("Hello {{x}}", None)
        assert result == "Hello {{x}}"

    def test_preserves_markdown(self):
        md = "# Title\n\n**bold** and *italic*\n\n- bullet\n"
        assert preprocess(md) == md

    def test_preserves_trailing_newline(self):
        result = preprocess("line\n", {})
        assert result.endswith("\n")

    def test_numeric_value(self):
        result = preprocess("Year: {{year}}", {"year": 2024})
        assert result == "Year: 2024"

"""Tests for the spec compiler (legacy YAML/JSON → Markdown)."""

import json
import os
import tempfile

import pytest

from will.spec_compiler import SpecCompileError, compile_spec, compile_spec_file


class TestCompileSpec:
    """Tests for compile_spec()."""

    def test_minimal_spec(self):
        md = compile_spec({"title": "Hello"})
        assert "title: Hello" in md

    def test_frontmatter_fields(self):
        md = compile_spec({
            "title": "Title",
            "subtitle": "Sub",
            "author": "Author",
        })
        assert "title:" in md
        assert "subtitle:" in md
        assert "author:" in md

    def test_will_metadata(self):
        md = compile_spec({
            "title": "T",
            "page_size": "a4",
            "margins": "narrow",
            "template": "report",
        })
        assert "will:" in md
        assert "page_size: a4" in md

    def test_heading_section(self):
        md = compile_spec({
            "sections": [
                {"type": "heading", "title": "My Heading", "level": 2},
            ],
        })
        assert "## My Heading" in md

    def test_section_with_page_break(self):
        md = compile_spec({
            "sections": [
                {"type": "section", "title": "Chapter", "page_break": True},
            ],
        })
        assert "\\newpage" in md
        assert "# Chapter" in md

    def test_section_with_subtitle(self):
        md = compile_spec({
            "sections": [
                {"type": "section", "title": "Ch", "subtitle": "Sub"},
            ],
        })
        assert "*Sub*" in md

    def test_content_with_text(self):
        md = compile_spec({
            "sections": [
                {"type": "content", "text": "Hello world"},
            ],
        })
        assert "Hello world" in md

    def test_content_with_bullets(self):
        md = compile_spec({
            "sections": [
                {"type": "content", "bullets": ["A", "B", "C"]},
            ],
        })
        assert "- A" in md
        assert "- B" in md
        assert "- C" in md

    def test_content_with_numbered(self):
        md = compile_spec({
            "sections": [
                {"type": "content", "numbered": ["First", "Second"]},
            ],
        })
        assert "1. First" in md
        assert "2. Second" in md

    def test_table(self):
        md = compile_spec({
            "sections": [
                {
                    "type": "table",
                    "title": "Data",
                    "headers": ["Col1", "Col2"],
                    "data": [["a", "b"], ["c", "d"]],
                },
            ],
        })
        assert "| Col1 | Col2 |" in md
        assert "| a | b |" in md

    def test_image(self):
        md = compile_spec({
            "sections": [
                {"type": "image", "path": "fig.png", "caption": "Figure 1", "width": 5},
            ],
        })
        assert "![Figure 1](fig.png)" in md
        assert "width=5in" in md

    def test_quote(self):
        md = compile_spec({
            "sections": [
                {"type": "quote", "text": "To be", "author": "Shakespeare"},
            ],
        })
        assert '> "To be"' in md
        assert "Shakespeare" in md

    def test_code(self):
        md = compile_spec({
            "sections": [
                {"type": "code", "code": "print('hi')", "language": "python"},
            ],
        })
        assert "```python" in md
        assert "print('hi')" in md

    def test_page_break(self):
        md = compile_spec({
            "sections": [{"type": "page_break"}],
        })
        assert "\\newpage" in md

    def test_horizontal_line(self):
        md = compile_spec({
            "sections": [{"type": "horizontal_line"}],
        })
        assert "---" in md

    def test_empty_sections(self):
        md = compile_spec({"sections": []})
        assert md.strip() == ""


class TestCompileSpecFile:
    """Tests for compile_spec_file()."""

    def test_yaml_file(self):
        with tempfile.NamedTemporaryFile(
            mode="w", suffix=".yaml", delete=False, encoding="utf-8"
        ) as f:
            f.write("title: Test\nsections:\n  - type: heading\n    title: Hello\n    level: 1\n")
            f.flush()
            md = compile_spec_file(f.name)
        os.unlink(f.name)
        assert "# Hello" in md

    def test_json_file(self):
        spec = {"title": "JSON Test", "sections": [{"type": "content", "text": "body"}]}
        with tempfile.NamedTemporaryFile(
            mode="w", suffix=".json", delete=False, encoding="utf-8"
        ) as f:
            json.dump(spec, f)
            f.flush()
            md = compile_spec_file(f.name)
        os.unlink(f.name)
        assert "body" in md

    def test_nonexistent_file(self):
        with pytest.raises(SpecCompileError):
            compile_spec_file("/nonexistent/file.yaml")

"""Tests for the Pandoc backend wrapper."""

import os
import tempfile

import pytest

from will.pandoc_backend import (
    PandocBackendError,
    convert,
    convert_to_bytes,
    get_pandoc_version,
    get_supported_formats,
)


class TestGetPandocVersion:
    def test_returns_string(self):
        version = get_pandoc_version()
        assert isinstance(version, str)
        assert len(version) > 0


class TestGetSupportedFormats:
    def test_returns_dict(self):
        fmts = get_supported_formats()
        assert "input" in fmts
        assert "output" in fmts
        assert "markdown" in fmts["input"]
        assert "docx" in fmts["output"]


class TestConvert:
    def test_markdown_to_docx(self):
        with tempfile.TemporaryDirectory() as tmpdir:
            out = os.path.join(tmpdir, "test.docx")
            result = convert("# Hello\n\nWorld", out, output_format="docx")
            assert result.exists()
            assert result.stat().st_size > 0

    def test_markdown_to_html(self):
        with tempfile.TemporaryDirectory() as tmpdir:
            out = os.path.join(tmpdir, "test.html")
            result = convert("# Hello\n\nWorld", out, output_format="html")
            assert result.exists()
            content = result.read_text(encoding="utf-8")
            assert "Hello" in content

    def test_with_metadata(self):
        with tempfile.TemporaryDirectory() as tmpdir:
            out = os.path.join(tmpdir, "test.html")
            convert(
                "# Doc\n\nBody",
                out,
                output_format="html",
                metadata={"title": "My Title"},
            )
            content = open(out, encoding="utf-8").read()
            assert "My Title" in content

    def test_invalid_format(self):
        with tempfile.TemporaryDirectory() as tmpdir:
            out = os.path.join(tmpdir, "test.xyz")
            with pytest.raises(PandocBackendError):
                convert("# Hello", out, output_format="not_a_format_xyz")


class TestConvertToBytes:
    def test_returns_bytes(self):
        data = convert_to_bytes("# Hello\n\nWorld", output_format="docx")
        assert isinstance(data, bytes)
        assert len(data) > 0

    def test_html_bytes(self):
        data = convert_to_bytes("# Hello", output_format="html")
        assert b"Hello" in data

"""Tests for the render pipeline."""

import os
import tempfile

import pytest

from will.render import RenderError, render


@pytest.fixture
def temp_dir():
    with tempfile.TemporaryDirectory() as tmpdir:
        yield tmpdir


@pytest.fixture
def sample_md(temp_dir):
    """Create a sample Markdown file."""
    path = os.path.join(temp_dir, "sample.md")
    with open(path, "w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        f.write(
            "---\n"
            "title: Test Document\n"
            "author: Tester\n"
            "will:\n"
            "  variables:\n"
            "    greeting: Hello\n"
            "---\n\n"
            "# {{greeting}} World\n\n"
            "This is a test document.\n"
        )
    return path


@pytest.fixture
def sample_yaml(temp_dir):
    """Create a sample YAML spec file."""
    path = os.path.join(temp_dir, "sample.yaml")
    with open(path, "w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        f.write(
            "title: YAML Document\n"
            "sections:\n"
            "  - type: heading\n"
            "    title: Introduction\n"
            "    level: 1\n"
            "  - type: content\n"
            "    text: This is from a YAML spec.\n"
        )
    return path


class TestRenderMarkdownToDocx:
    def test_basic(self, sample_md, temp_dir):
        out = os.path.join(temp_dir, "out.docx")
        result = render(sample_md, output=out)
        assert result.exists()
        assert result.stat().st_size > 0

    def test_returns_bytes_when_no_output(self, sample_md):
        data = render(sample_md)
        assert isinstance(data, bytes)
        assert len(data) > 0


class TestRenderMarkdownToHtml:
    def test_html_output(self, sample_md, temp_dir):
        out = os.path.join(temp_dir, "out.html")
        result = render(sample_md, output=out, format="html")
        assert result.exists()
        content = result.read_text(encoding="utf-8")
        assert "Hello World" in content


class TestRenderYamlSpec:
    def test_yaml_to_docx(self, sample_yaml, temp_dir):
        out = os.path.join(temp_dir, "out.docx")
        result = render(sample_yaml, output=out)
        assert result.exists()
        assert result.stat().st_size > 0


class TestRenderWithVariables:
    def test_cli_variables_override(self, temp_dir):
        """CLI variables should override frontmatter variables."""
        md_path = os.path.join(temp_dir, "vars.md")
        with open(md_path, "w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
            f.write(
                "---\n"
                "title: Vars Test\n"
                "will:\n"
                "  variables:\n"
                "    name: Default\n"
                "---\n\n"
                "Hello {{name}}\n"
            )
        out = os.path.join(temp_dir, "out.html")
        result = render(md_path, output=out, format="html", variables={"name": "Override"})
        content = result.read_text(encoding="utf-8")
        assert "Override" in content
        assert "Default" not in content


class TestRenderLegacyBackend:
    def test_legacy_yaml(self, sample_yaml, temp_dir):
        out = os.path.join(temp_dir, "legacy.docx")
        result = render(sample_yaml, output=out, backend="legacy")
        assert result.exists()
        assert result.stat().st_size > 0

    def test_legacy_rejects_markdown(self, sample_md, temp_dir):
        out = os.path.join(temp_dir, "legacy.docx")
        with pytest.raises(RenderError):
            render(sample_md, output=out, backend="legacy")


class TestRenderErrors:
    def test_nonexistent_source(self, temp_dir):
        out = os.path.join(temp_dir, "out.docx")
        with pytest.raises(RenderError):
            render("/nonexistent/file.md", output=out)

Tool reply2026-02-23T00:05:14.829Z
     1→# Introduction
     2→
     3→
     4→**How I Met Claude Code**
     5→
     6→As I was about to respond to a friend who had asked if there was a GPT to look up flight prices to Boston, it got me to reflect on the journey of AI for the past few years.
     7→
     8→GPTs were a thing, and then Claude Code arrived.
     9→
    10→This is the story of how I met Claude. But mainly Claude Code.
    11→
    12→It was December of 2025. For some reason, this is when the world started to pay attention to Claude Code, and what was happening with coding agents.
    13→
    14→The company I worked for (the MathWorks, developers of MATLAB) was starting a pilot to assess the potential for non-developers first. A few product managers were given access, with the responsibility to report back on their usage in the first quarter of 2026. I was one of the lucky ones.
    15→


Tool reply2026-02-23T00:05:15.890Z
     1→---
     2→title: "Chapter 1: Before Claude Code (BCC) — The ChatGPT Era"
     3→status: draft-v1
     4→last_editor: claude
     5→notes: "Covers the pre-Claude AI landscape, vibe coding origin story, and projects that set the stage"
     6→---
     7→
     8→# Chapter 1: Before Claude Code (BCC) — The ChatGPT Era
     9→
    10→If you are like me, you were probably deep in the ChatGPT era by mid-2025 and didn't even realize a seismic shift was coming.
    11→
    12→Everyone I knew was experimenting. Product managers were tip-toeing with the technology, prototyping with AI services or locally with Ollama. Marketing teams were generating content drafts. Developers were starting to evaluate copilots and some were happy enough about the productivity gain it was providing. It's in mid-2025 that the term "vibe coding" entered the vocabulary — this idea that you could describe what you wanted in plain language and watch an AI conjure it into existence. It felt like magic, and like most magic, we didn't fully understand the trick.
    13→
    14→I was spending all the free hours I had on AI. I had my ChatGPT subscription, my collection of apps connecting to the OpenAI web services, and a growing sense that something fundamental was changing about how software would be built. But everything I did with AI in those years prior to Claude and agentic involved a lot of copy-pasting, trial and error, and manual integration.
    15→
    16→That was about to change. But first, let me tell you about the night I built a website in my kitchen.
    17→
    18→## The Vibe Coding Origin Story
    19→
    20→Late August 2025. My in-laws were visiting, shortly after GPT-5's release. My father-in-law and I were in the kitchen late at night — the kind of hour where ambitious ideas feel perfectly reasonable.
    21→
    22→> **Me**: "What website or web app would you like to develop?"
    23→> **Father-in-law**: *looks around the kitchen, spots a recipe book*
    24→> **Me**: "Ok, let's build a website with French recipes."
    25→
    26→Single prompt. Single session. I opened ChatGPT, described what I wanted — a clean, browsable website of French recipes — and watched it generate the HTML, CSS, and JavaScript. The whole thing took maybe an hour, most of which was us discussing which recipes to feature and laughing about the absurdity of what we were doing.
    27→
    28→That same night, sometime around 2am, I purchased the domain on Cloudflare. recettesdezoe.com was born — named after my daughter Zoé. We deployed it right there, my father-in-law watching over my shoulder as the site went live.
    29→
    30→It was a demo. A party trick. A thing I'd built to show what was possible.

     1→---
     2→title: "Chapter 1: Before Claude Code (BCC) — The ChatGPT Era"
     3→status: draft-v1
     4→last_editor: claude
     5→notes: "Covers the pre-Claude AI landscape, vibe coding origin story, and projects that set the stage"
     6→---
     7→
     8→# Chapter 1: Before Claude Code (BCC) — The ChatGPT Era
     9→
    10→If you are like me, you were probably deep in the ChatGPT era by mid-2025 and didn't even realize a seismic shift was coming.
    11→
    12→Everyone I knew was experimenting. Product managers were tip-toeing with the technology, prototyping with AI services or locally with Ollama. Marketing teams were generating content drafts. Developers were starting to evaluate copilots and some were happy enough about the productivity gain it was providing. It's in mid-2025 that the term "vibe coding" entered the vocabulary — this idea that you could describe what you wanted in plain language and watch an AI conjure it into existence. It felt like magic, and like most magic, we didn't fully understand the trick.
    13→
    14→I was spending all the free hours I had on AI. I had my ChatGPT subscription, my collection of apps connecting to the OpenAI web services, and a growing sense that something fundamental was changing about how software would be built. But everything I did with AI in those years prior to Claude and agentic involved a lot of copy-pasting, trial and error, and manual integration.
    15→
    16→That was about to change. But first, let me tell you about the night I built a website in my kitchen.
    17→
    18→## The Vibe Coding Origin Story
    19→
    20→Late August 2025. My in-laws were visiting, shortly after GPT-5's release. My father-in-law and I were in the kitchen late at night — the kind of hour where ambitious ideas feel perfectly reasonable.
    21→
    22→> **Me**: "What website or web app would you like to develop?"
    23→> **Father-in-law**: *looks around the kitchen, spots a recipe book*
    24→> **Me**: "Ok, let's build a website with French recipes."
    25→
    26→Single prompt. Single session. I opened ChatGPT, described what I wanted — a clean, browsable website of French recipes — and watched it generate the HTML, CSS, and JavaScript. The whole thing took maybe an hour, most of which was us discussing which recipes to feature and laughing about the absurdity of what we were doing.
    27→
    28→That same night, sometime around 2am, I purchased the domain on Cloudflare. recettesdezoe.com was born — named after my daughter Zoé. We deployed it right there, my father-in-law watching over my shoulder as the site went live.
    29→
    30→It was a demo. A party trick. A thing I'd built to show what was possible.
    31→
    32→Or so I thought.
    33→
    34→## The Surprise Discovery
    35→
    36→Four months later — December 2025 — I was poking around my Cloudflare dashboard for an unrelated reason when something caught my eye. The analytics for recettesdezoe.com showed **1,300+ visitors in the past month.**
    37→
    38→I stared at the number. Zero edits since launch. Zero marketing. Zero SEO work. Zero social media promotion. Nothing. I had literally forgotten the site existed for weeks at a time, and over a thousand people had found it, visited it, and presumably cooked something from it.
    39→
    40→This broke something in my mental model. I had always assumed that building a useful product required sustained effort — iteration, marketing, user research, the whole product management playbook I'd internalized over years at MathWorks. But here was a website I'd built in a kitchen at 2am with an AI, and it was generating more organic traffic than projects I'd spent months planning.
    41→
    42→The lessons hit me one at a time over the following days:
    43→
    44→- **Vibe coding can produce genuinely useful, discoverable products.** Not just demos — real things that real people find and use.
    45→- **The "demo that becomes real" pattern is more powerful than planned projects.** When you remove the friction of building, you can test ideas at the speed of conversation.
    46→- **Domain purchases at 2am sometimes pay off.** I'm not saying it's a strategy. But I'm not saying it isn't.
    47→
    48→## Other Pre-Claude Projects
    49→
    50→recettesdezoe.com wasn't my only experiment in the pre-Claude era. That summer and fall, I'd been building a small portfolio of AI-assisted projects, each one pushing a little further into what was possible.
    51→
    52→**GhostRunners.app** was probably the most ambitious. I'm a cycling enthusiast, and I wanted something that didn't exist: real-time analytics that could compare my current ride to previous efforts on the same route — essentially racing against my own ghost, like in a video game. I built it using MATLAB Mobile for data capture, MATLAB Online for the analysis, and Python with the Garmin FIT SDK for parsing the ride files. I even recorded YouTube videos documenting the process on my channel @yannspeaksai. It wasn't pretty code, but it worked, and it scratched an itch no existing app could reach.
    53→
    54→**OpenAI Codex** came next — first as a VSCode integration where I could vibe-code small HTML and JavaScript apps, and later as a cloud-based agent that could run data analysis hands-off. The progression was revealing: from AI helping me write code, to AI writing code while I watched, to AI writing and running code while I did something else entirely.
    55→
    56→**OpenAI Canvas** I tried once and largely abandoned. For single-page web apps, I found myself defaulting to the Claude app — either on mobile or desktop. Even before Claude Code entered the picture, something about Claude's approach to conversation was pulling me in.
    57→
    58→## The Pattern I Couldn't See Yet
    59→
    60→Looking back, these projects trace an arc I couldn't see at the time. Each one was a single interaction — a prompt, a session, an output. I'd build something, feel the rush of creation, and move on. There was no continuity between sessions, no shared context, no sense that the AI remembered or built upon what we'd done before.
    61→
    62→Every conversation started from zero. Every project was an island.
    63→
    64→I didn't know it yet, but I was about to discover what happens when AI stops being a tool you pick up and put down, and starts becoming something closer to a collaborator who remembers where you left off.
    65→
    66→December 2025 was coming, and with it, an invitation to a pilot program that would change how I thought about all of this.
    67→

Tool reply2026-02-23T00:08:37.778Z
     1→---
     2→title: "Chapter 2: December 2025 — First Contact"
     3→status: draft-v1
     4→last_editor: claude
     5→notes: "Covers the MathWorks pilot, strategic conversations, blogging workflow, and the moment Claude became more than a chatbot"
     6→---
     7→
     8→# Chapter 2: December 2025 — First Contact
     9→
    10→The email arrived sometime in early December. MathWorks was running a pilot program — Claude Code for non-developers. A handful of product managers would get access, with one responsibility: report back on what they found in Q1 2026. I was one of the lucky ones.
    11→
    12→But I didn't start with Claude Code. That would have been too big a leap for someone coming from the ChatGPT world. Instead, I started where most people would: Claude Desktop.
    13→
    14→The Desktop app is a very approachable interface for those who come from chat apps like ChatGPT. Same text box, same conversational flow, same satisfying rhythm of typing a question and watching an answer materialize. The first question I asked myself was simple: *What kind of project can I tackle with Claude?*
    15→
    16→The answer surprised me. Because it wasn't code.
    17→
    18→## The Strategic Awakening
    19→
    20→My very first substantive conversation with Claude wasn't about building something — it was about *thinking* about something.
    21→
    22→I had been drafting a memo to our Director of Product Marketing. The core thesis was about a strategic tension I'd been mulling over for weeks: the difference between being best-in-class at coding agents and being best-in-class at building tools that coding agents call. For a company like MathWorks, this wasn't an abstract distinction — it was the question that would shape our positioning in the age of agentic AI.
    23→
    24→I won't share the full memo here, but I can tell you what happened when I brought it to Claude. I expected what I'd gotten from ChatGPT — a polished summary, maybe some rephrased bullet points, the kind of agreeable helpfulness that makes you feel good but doesn't make you think harder.
    25→
    26→Instead, Claude fact-checked my market claims. It confirmed that Cursor had crossed $1B ARR, that Claude Code had reached a $1B run-rate within six months of its launch. Then it stress-tested my arguments — praised the strategic logic but called out gaps. Where were the concrete examples? What about the risks I'd glossed over? The success metrics were too vague. The feedback was direct, specific, and genuinely useful.
    27→
    28→I sat with that for a moment. This wasn't what I was used to from AI conversations. ChatGPT would have told me my memo was great and offered to make it longer. Claude told me where it was weak and how to make it stronger.
    29→
    30→> *This set the tone for everything that followed: Claude as thinking partner, not just tool.*
    31→
    32→I went back and rewrote sections of the memo. The final version was meaningfully better — not because Claude wrote it for me, but because Claude pushed back on the parts that needed pushing. It was the kind of feedback you'd hope to get from a sharp colleague, delivered without ego or politics.
    33→
    34→I was also creating a presentation on the impact of agentic AI on the market of scientific and technical computing — something that most companies must have been asking themselves at that point. One diagram I developed described the chasm between the early adopters of agentic AI, evaluating solutions like Claude, and the majority still associating AI with chatbots like ChatGPT. That chasm was real, and I was watching myself cross it in real time.
    35→
    36→## The Blog Posts Begin
    37→
    38→Within the first week, the strategic conversations spilled over into something more tangible. I had taken over the MATLAB AI Blog on September 1st, and now, with Claude as a collaborator, I found myself drafting posts at a pace I hadn't thought possible.
    39→
    40→The list grew quickly:
    41→
    42→- MATLAB MCP Core Server — the foundational post about connecting MATLAB to the agentic ecosystem
    43→- Connecting to Hardware — data acquisition and image acquisition devices via MCP
    44→- Deploying a Quant Agent — financial analysis with MATLAB as the computational backbone
    45→- Antigravity + MATLAB — a playful post that turned out to be wicked good
    46→- Giving Local AI Agents the Ability to Use MATLAB with MCP
    47→- Building an Optimization Agent
    48→- Automating the Documentation of MATLAB Analysis
    49→- AI-Assisted Model-Based Design
    50→
    51→Eight posts in the pipeline within the first week. This wasn't because I suddenly became a faster writer. The content was still mine — the ideas, the technical depth, the perspective on where MATLAB fit in the agentic AI landscape. What changed was the workflow.
    52→
    53→Claude handled the boring parts. The formatting, the structuring, the initial research on competitor approaches, the first-pass drafts of sections where I knew what I wanted to say but didn't want to stare at a blank page. This freed me to concentrate on the two things that actually mattered: **ideation and code.** The thinking and the building. Everything else was scaffolding, and Claude was excellent at scaffolding.
    54→
    55→The conference report-out workflow was another revelation. We had gone to the JupyterCon conference in San Diego with my colleague Rohan in early November, and delivering the internal presentation afterward had taken us a long time. I wondered how much of that process could be automated. When PyData came to Boston — its first time there, usually it's in NYC — I tested a streamlined workflow: notes in, presentation out, with Claude handling the transformation in between. The quality was surprisingly good, and the time savings were real.
    56→
    57→## Claude Analyzes My Voice
    58→
    59→Then something happened that I hadn't expected.
    60→
    61→I asked Claude to study my published writing — the blog posts, the technical articles, the presentation scripts — and tell me what patterns it found. I wanted to know if my writing had a recognizable voice, and if so, whether Claude could capture it.
    62→
    63→Claude came back with what it called a "skill" — a structured guide that codified my writing voice into its component parts:
    64→
    65→**My signature patterns:**
    66→
    67→- **Opening hooks**: "If you are like me, you are probably familiar with this situation."
    68→- **Colleague references**: "my dear colleague [Name]"
    69→- **Honest limitations**: "I'm not an expert in Finance!"
    70→- **Closings**: "Happy hacking, and let me know what you build! 👋🏻"
    71→- **Structure**: Problem → Demo → Walkthrough → Resources
    72→
    73→I read through the analysis with a strange mix of recognition and surprise. It was accurate — almost uncannily so. These were patterns I'd developed over years of writing, and I'd never consciously catalogued them. Claude had done it in a single conversation.
    74→
    75→More importantly, it *worked*. When I asked Claude to draft sections using this skill, the output sounded like me. Not a generic approximation of a tech blogger, but specifically like me — my rhythms, my tendency to acknowledge what I don't know, my habit of opening with a shared experience. It was the difference between a cover band playing the notes and actually capturing the feel.
    76→
    77→This was when I started to understand that what was happening went beyond productivity. A tool that makes you faster is useful. A tool that learns your voice and helps you sound more like yourself — that's something else entirely.
    78→
    79→## Something Was Shifting
    80→
    81→By the end of that first week with Claude, I had:
    82→
    83→- Sharpened a strategic memo through genuine intellectual sparring
    84→- Drafted the foundations for eight blog posts
    85→- Streamlined a conference report-out workflow
    86→- Had my writing voice analyzed and codified in a way I could actually use
    87→
    88→But the numbers don't capture what was really changing. It was the *quality* of the interaction. Each conversation with Claude built on the ones before it. There was continuity — a shared context that grew with every exchange. When I mentioned the "agent calling toolboxes" strategy, Claude knew what I meant. When I referenced the blog voice, Claude could apply it. When I circled back to an idea from two days ago, I didn't have to re-explain everything from scratch.
    89→
    90→The conversations had something I'd never experienced with an AI before: **honest feedback.** Claude didn't just agree with me. It pushed back. It identified weaknesses. It suggested alternatives I hadn't considered. And it did all of this without the social dynamics that make human feedback complicated — no ego, no office politics, no tiptoeing around seniority.
    91→
    92→What had started as a productivity experiment — can this tool help me write blog posts faster? — was becoming something I didn't have a name for yet. It wasn't a chatbot. It wasn't an assistant. It was starting to feel like a colleague who happened to be available at 2am and never needed coffee.
    93→
    94→I didn't know where this was headed. But I knew that the one-shot, disposable AI interactions of the ChatGPT era were behind me. Whatever came next would be built on continuity, shared context, and the kind of thinking partnership I was only beginning to understand.
    95→
    96→Looking back, I'd later distill this first week into a slide I called "Agentic 101" — the foundation layer. Skills, tools, MCP. Claude Desktop as the gateway drug. Python first, then MATLAB. It was the simplest of the four weekly lessons I'd eventually present to colleagues, but it was also the most important. Everything that followed — the philosopher tools, the agent orchestration, the terminal becoming home — grew from this first week of unlearning what I thought AI could do.
    97→
    98→The philosopher tools were about to arrive. But that's the next chapter.
    99→

Tool reply2026-02-23T00:08:38.122Z
     1→---
     2→title: "Chapter 3: The Philosopher Tools"
     3→status: draft-v1
     4→last_editor: claude
     5→notes: "Covers Week 2 — the shift from thinking partner to building partner, CLI tools named after philosophers, the reading list that fed the naming, and the creative momentum that followed"
     6→---
     7→
     8→# Chapter 3: The Philosopher Tools
     9→
    10→The philosopher tools were about to arrive. That's how I closed the last chapter, and I meant it literally.
    11→
    12→Week 2 brought a shift I hadn't anticipated. Claude wasn't just a thinking partner anymore — it was a building partner. The strategic memos and blog drafts from Week 1 had been intellectually rewarding, but they were still fundamentally about *writing*. Now I found myself doing something different: building software. Real, distributable, reusable software. And the tools that emerged came with names I didn't expect.
    13→
    14→## From Skills to Tools
    15→
    16→The key insight of Week 2 was deceptively simple: **density — less is more.** Build tools, not just skills.
    17→
    18→Let me explain. In Week 1, Claude had helped me create "skills" — structured prompts that captured my writing voice, my presentation style, my analytical frameworks. They were useful, but they were also fragile. A skill lives in a conversation. You invoke it, it shapes the output, and then it vanishes when the session ends. It's a one-shot thing — powerful in the moment, but not reusable in any systematic way.
    19→
    20→A CLI tool is different. A CLI tool is a piece of software that lives on your machine, that agents can call deterministically, that follows a workflow you've defined and tested. It doesn't depend on the quality of your prompt or the mood of the conversation. It just runs.
    21→
    22→This distinction — between a prompt that works once and a tool that works every time — turned out to be the difference between playing with AI and actually building with it. And once I understood that, the tools started coming fast.
    23→
    24→## The Army of Philosophers
    25→
    26→If you are like me, you probably name your projects something boring and descriptive — `slide-generator`, `media-converter`, `search-tool`. Functional. Forgettable. I'd done this my whole career.
    27→
    28→But something about building with Claude changed the naming instinct. Maybe it was the late-night energy. Maybe it was the reading I'd been doing all year. Maybe it was the feeling that these tools deserved names that carried meaning beyond their function. Whatever the reason, I started naming my tools after philosophers.
    29→
    30→And once I started, I couldn't stop.
    31→
    32→**montaigne.cc** was the first. A media processing toolkit for presentation localization — transcription, translation, audio and video processing, all powered by Google Gemini AI. Montaigne's *Essais* are the original explorations — a mind wandering across languages, cultures, and ideas, interpreting everything it encounters through the lens of personal experience. The tool does the same thing, in its own way. It takes content in one language and interprets it into another. It listens to audio and renders it as text. It crosses boundaries between media the way Montaigne crossed boundaries between disciplines.
    33→
    34→**nietzsche.cc** came next. A presentation generator — a CLI where the `power` command generates slide decks. "The will to present." I know, I know. But hear me out. Nietzsche's Übermensch is about self-overcoming, about transcending the tedious and mundane to reach something higher. If you've ever spent three hours manually formatting a PowerPoint deck when the ideas were already clear in your head, you understand the appeal. The tool was my Übermensch moment — a way to transcend the drudgery and focus on what mattered: the ideas themselves.
    35→
    36→There's another Nietzschean concept that fits even better: eternal recurrence. The idea that you should live as though you'd have to repeat every action for eternity. In automation, this is exactly what you want — a workflow so clean, so reliable, that running it again and again produces consistent results. Create, iterate, reuse. Eternal recurrence, but for slide decks.
    37→
    38→**ricoeur.cc** was the one I was most proud of conceptually. A CLI search tool for conversation history — a way to dig through past dialogues and extract meaning from them. Paul Ricoeur's hermeneutic circle is the idea that you understand the parts through the whole, and the whole through the parts. Part, whole, part, whole — an endless loop of interpretation and reinterpretation. That's exactly what searching through conversation history feels like. You find a fragment, it reframes your understanding of the entire exchange, and that new understanding sends you looking for different fragments. The tool and the philosophy were almost uncomfortably aligned.
    39→
    40→I even asked Claude to evaluate the naming. The feedback was characteristically direct: ricoeur.cc edged ahead on conceptual coherence — the mapping between the tool's purpose and the philosopher's theme was the tightest. But nietzsche.cc was more polished in UX, more immediately usable. Claude recommended building a shared landing page to tell the story of why these tools existed as a family. Not just a collection of utilities, but a suite with a philosophical backbone.
    41→
    42→An army of philosophers. That's what I started calling them.
    43→
    44→## Why Philosophers?
    45→
    46→The naming wasn't random. It grew out of a reading list that had been building all year — a personal arc through books that, looking back, were preparing me for exactly this moment.
    47→
    48→It started with Kafka's *The Trial*. The individual against the system, navigating opaque bureaucracies that operate by rules no one fully understands. If you've ever tried to introduce AI tooling into a large organization, you know this story intimately. The committees, the approval processes, the security reviews — Kafka wrote the manual on corporate AI adoption before AI existed.
    49→
    50→Then Camus's *The Stranger*. Absurdism. The idea that life doesn't owe you meaning, so you'd better create your own. There's something deeply Camusian about vibe coding — this acceptance that the process is absurd, that you're building software by describing it in plain language to a machine, and that the appropriate response is not to demand it make sense but to embrace the creative freedom it offers.
    51→
    52→Nietzsche's *Thus Spoke Zarathustra* was the big one. Will to power, self-overcoming, the three metamorphoses. More on that in a moment.
    53→
    54→Plato's *Apology* — Socrates on trial, defending the examined life. Intellectual humility as a practice. The idea that wisdom begins with knowing what you don't know. Every time I typed "I'm not an expert in Finance!" into a blog post, I was channeling Socrates without realizing it.
    55→
    56→Irvin Yalom's *The Schopenhauer Cure* bridged philosophy and practice — the idea that thinking about the world and doing something in the world aren't separate activities. Philosophy as therapy. Code as philosophy.
    57→
    58→Then the modern voices: Harari's *Nexus*, framing AI as the next chapter in the human information story. Mollick's *Co-Intelligence*, providing the practical frameworks for working alongside AI systems.
    59→
    60→> "The existentialists ask 'how should I live authentically?' while Mollick and Harari ask 'how should we live with AI?' These questions are not separate. The tools we build shape the lives we lead."
    61→
    62→That synthesis emerged during a late-night conversation with Claude, and it became the philosophical thesis underneath everything I was building. The tools weren't just utilities. They were answers — my answers — to questions that philosophers had been asking for centuries.
    63→
    64→## The Zarathustra Project
    65→
    66→The philosopher tools and the reading list converged most dramatically in the Zarathustra project. I built a full voiceover script for a fifteen-slide presentation on *Thus Spoke Zarathustra*, and I built it using nietzsche.cc itself. The tool and its namesake, working together.
    67→
    68→The presentation traced the arc of Nietzsche's masterwork: the prophet Zarathustra descending from his mountain after ten years of solitude. The Übermensch — not a superhero, but a concept of human self-overcoming. The three metamorphoses: the Camel who bears the weight of tradition, the Lion who rebels and creates freedom, the Child who plays and creates new values. The Last Man — Nietzsche's warning about a humanity so comfortable it stops striving. The Will to Power as creative drive, not domination. Eternal Recurrence as the ultimate test of how you choose to live.
    69→
    70→Twelve to fifteen minutes of narrated content, generated from a tool I'd built with an AI, about a philosopher whose ideas had inspired the tool's name. If Nietzsche had known about recursion, he would have appreciated the structure.
    71→
    72→## The Philosopher Rap Battle
    73→
    74→And then — because creative momentum has a logic of its own — the rap battle happened.
    75→
    76→Once you've named your tools after philosophers and built a presentation about Zarathustra, the next step is obviously an educational web app where philosophers debate each other in rap format. Obviously.
    77→
    78→The concept was simple: pick two philosophers, set them against each other on a topic, and let the arguments fly — but in verse. Nietzsche vs. Schopenhauer was the flagship matchup. Master and student. Pessimism vs. affirmation. "The world is suffering" vs. "Embrace it all and will it again."
    79→
    80→It was playful. It was absurd. It was exactly the kind of project that happens when you're building with a collaborator who doesn't get tired, doesn't judge your ideas, and responds to "What if we made philosophers rap-battle each other?" with genuine creative energy rather than a raised eyebrow.
    81→
    82→This is something I didn't expect about building with Claude: the creative compound effect. Each project made the next one easier to imagine. The philosopher naming led to the reading list connections, which led to the Zarathustra presentation, which led to the rap battle. One idea feeding the next, accelerating, branching. Not because Claude was generating ideas autonomously, but because having a tireless collaborator meant I never had to stop and wonder "Is this too weird? Is this worth the time?" The answer was always: let's find out.
    83→
    84→## The Developer Workflow
    85→
    86→Behind the philosophical naming and the creative experiments, Week 2 was also when I became a real developer. Or at least, a closer approximation of one.
    87→
    88→Python for CLI is fire. I'd known Python before, but building command-line tools with it — using `fire` for argument parsing, structuring projects as proper packages — felt different. These weren't scripts. They were software.
    89→
    90→`uv` is life. The Python package manager that made everything faster — environment creation, dependency resolution, the whole toolchain. Once you've used `uv`, going back to `pip` feels like going back to dial-up.
    91→
    92→Publishing to PyPI was the moment it felt real. My tools weren't just local scripts anymore. Anyone could `pip install` them. They existed in the world, with version numbers and documentation and download counts. Let the game begin.
    93→
    94→GitHub Issues became my requirements management system. Instead of keeping a mental list of features to add, I'd have Claude generate issues — structured, labeled, prioritized. Then I'd work through them methodically. Requirements in, code out, tests validating, CI/CD deploying.
    95→
    96→Speaking of tests: automated testing via GitHub Actions meant that every push was validated. And when a test failed, I could ask the GitHub MCP server to retrieve the trace. No more copy-pasting error logs from one window to another. The feedback loop tightened until it was nearly instantaneous.
    97→
    98→I even recorded a YouTube demo — the whole workflow from concept to published package, captured on my @yannspeaksai channel. Because if you build an army of philosophers and don't document it, did it really happen?
    99→
   100→## Tools as Self-Portraits
   101→
   102→Here's what I think Chapter 3 is really about.
   103→
   104→Naming your tools after philosophers isn't vanity — it's a way of encoding your values into the things you build. Every developer names their projects. Most names are functional, forgettable, interchangeable. But when you name a tool after Ricoeur, you're making a claim about what the tool means, not just what it does. You're saying: this search tool embodies a philosophy of interpretation. This presentation generator carries a vision of self-overcoming. This media toolkit is an act of translation in the deepest sense.
   105→
   106→The philosopher tools were the first sign that my collaboration with Claude was producing something more personal than productivity gains. The blog posts from Week 1 were useful. The strategic memos were valuable. But the tools — the tools were self-portraits in code. They reflected not just what I could build, but how I thought about the world.
   107→
   108→Week 2 had taught me that the line between philosophy and programming was thinner than I'd imagined. The existentialists and the engineers were asking the same question: *What should I build, and why does it matter?*
   109→
   110→What I didn't know yet was that these weekly discoveries — the density principle, the philosopher army, the shift from skills to tools — would become the backbone of an internal evangelism campaign. Slides I'd present to three different audiences over the next six weeks, each time with a deeper understanding of what "build tools, not skills" really meant. But that story belongs to a later chapter.
   111→
   112→I didn't have a complete answer yet. But I had an army of philosophers, a PyPI account, and a growing suspicion that the most interesting work was still ahead.
   113→
   114→The competitive intelligence reports were about to begin. Claude was about to become my research partner. But that's the next chapter.
   115→
