     2→title: "Chapter 4: The Competitive Intelligence Machine"
     3→status: draft-v1
     4→last_editor: claude
     5→notes: "Covers how Claude became a research partner for competitive intelligence — the pattern, the examples (PIDO, neuroscience toolboxes, MATLAB community), and what this meant for the PM role"
     6→---
     7→
     8→# Chapter 4: The Competitive Intelligence Machine
     9→
    10→The philosopher tools had given me a taste of building. But building wasn't the only thing that happened in those early weeks. Alongside the CLI tools and the PyPI packages, a completely different body of work was taking shape — one that had nothing to do with code and everything to do with thinking.
    11→
    12→Claude was becoming my research partner. And the research was getting serious.
    13→
    14→## The Pattern
    15→
    16→If you are like me — a product manager at a technology company — you've spent a meaningful fraction of your career doing competitive intelligence. Reading analyst reports. Scanning press releases. Sitting in meetings where someone presents a slide that says "Market Size: $X Billion" and everyone nods as if the number is more than an educated guess.
    17→
    18→The traditional workflow is slow and fragmented. You gather information from a dozen sources, synthesize it in your head, write it up in a memo, circulate it for feedback, revise, and eventually arrive at something useful — weeks after you started. By then, the market has moved.
    19→
    20→With Claude, a new pattern emerged. It was simple, repeatable, and remarkably effective:
    21→
    22→1. **I bring the question.** A company, a technology, a market segment. Something I need to understand deeply for strategic planning.
    23→2. **Claude researches broadly.** Not just surface-level facts — deep dives into market dynamics, competitive positioning, technology trends, customer segments.
    24→3. **Together, we extract implications.** This is the part that matters. Not "what is the market size?" but "what does this mean for us? Where are the opportunities? Where are the threats?"
    25→
    26→This wasn't just fact-gathering. It was thinking partnership applied to strategy. And the volume of what we produced shocked me.
    27→
    28→## The PIDO Deep Dive
    29→
    30→The first major competitive intelligence effort was about a market I'd been tracking: Process Integration and Design Optimization — PIDO, for those who live in the engineering software world.
    31→
    32→In a traditional workflow, building a comprehensive market analysis of PIDO would take me the better part of a week. I'd need to identify the key players, understand their positioning, map the competitive landscape, estimate market sizes from fragmented data sources, and connect it all to MathWorks' strategic positioning.
    33→
    34→With Claude, I typed a single prompt. What came back was a comprehensive analysis: a $200–220M USD market in 2024, growing at 10–12% CAGR. But the numbers were the easy part. What made it valuable was the strategic layer — the implications for MathWorks' positioning, the gaps in our current approach, the segments where we were strong and the ones where we were vulnerable.
    35→
    36→I sat with the output for a while, cross-checking claims, testing the logic. Some of the analysis I'd adjust. Some of the market estimates I'd refine with internal data Claude didn't have access to. But the foundation was solid. The synthesis was genuinely useful. And it had taken an hour instead of a week.
    37→
    38→This is the part that most people misunderstand about AI-assisted research. They imagine you press a button and receive a report. That's not how it works — or at least, that's not how it works well. What actually happens is a dialogue. I'd push back on Claude's framing. Claude would defend it or adjust. I'd bring internal context — things I knew about our product roadmap, our customer conversations, our engineering investments — that Claude couldn't access. Claude would integrate that context and sharpen the analysis.
    39→
    40→The output was neither mine nor Claude's. It was ours.
    41→
    42→## The Neuroscience Toolbox Analysis
    43→
    44→The second example was more technical and, in some ways, more revealing. Industry marketing needed a competitive analysis of the neuroscience research tools ecosystem — specifically, how MATLAB-based tools compared to the rising Python alternatives.
    45→
    46→Claude dove into the landscape: FieldTrip, EEGLAB, MNE-Python, and the constellation of smaller tools orbiting them. The research surfaced something I hadn't fully appreciated. EEGLAB was leading in academic citations at 21,700+. But MNE-Python was showing the fastest growth trajectory — 3,200 GitHub stars, 416 contributors, and the kind of community momentum that's hard to reverse.
    47→
    48→The strategic insight was pointed: a clear bifurcation was emerging between MATLAB-based tools (historically dominant, deeply embedded in research workflows) and Python-based alternatives (rapidly growing, zero licensing costs, open-source community energy). This wasn't just a technical comparison. It was a strategic signal about where the neuroscience research community was heading.
    49→
    50→This is the kind of analysis that, delivered in a deck to industry marketing, changes how people think about their market. And it emerged from a conversation, not a consulting engagement.
    51→
    52→## MATLAB Community Intelligence
    53→
    54→The third stream of research was the most internally focused. Colleagues had started coming to me with requests.
    55→
    56→"Can you tell me what users say about the Live Editor?" — that was Rohan.
    57→
    58→"Can you tell me if a feature like Live Editor is useful and loved?" — that was Johanna.
    59→
    60→These weren't idle questions. They were the kind of product intelligence that normally requires weeks of manual analysis — combing through MATLAB Answers, reading forum threads, categorizing sentiment, identifying patterns. The kind of work that everyone agrees is important and nobody has time to do.
    61→
    62→Claude changed the equation. By analyzing MATLAB community data — forum posts, questions, answers, the whole corpus of user-generated content — we could extract product intelligence at a pace that made it practical. Not perfect. Not a replacement for proper user research. But a first pass that was fast enough to be useful and comprehensive enough to be informative.
    63→
    64→The fact that colleagues were bringing me their questions told me something important. The competitive intelligence work wasn't just useful to me — it was becoming useful to the people around me. The research partner I'd found in Claude was turning into something closer to a research service.
    65→
    66→## What Claude Brought, What I Brought
    67→
    68→Here's the honest accounting of who contributed what.
    69→
    70→**Claude brought breadth.** The ability to survey a landscape quickly, to pull together information from diverse sources, to identify patterns across large bodies of data. When I asked about the PIDO market, Claude could synthesize information from analyst reports, company announcements, technology trends, and market dynamics — the kind of breadth that would take me days to assemble manually.
    71→
    72→**I brought judgment.** The ability to say "that market estimate feels high" or "you're missing the political dimension of this competitive dynamic" or "that's technically correct but strategically irrelevant." I knew which of our products competed where. I knew which customer segments we were winning and which we were losing. I knew the internal politics — which initiatives had executive sponsorship and which were struggling for resources.
    73→
    74→**Together, we produced synthesis.** Neither of us could have done it alone. Claude without my judgment would produce technically accurate but strategically naive reports. My judgment without Claude's breadth would produce insightful but narrow analyses that took too long to assemble.
    75→
    76→This is the pattern that Ethan Mollick describes in *Co-Intelligence* — the idea that human-AI collaboration isn't about automation but about augmentation. You don't replace the human. You amplify them. And you don't use AI as a search engine. You use it as a thinking partner.
    77→
    78→## The Sleepless Nights
    79→
    80→There's a moment that Mollick writes about — the first time AI does something that makes you lie awake at night. Not in fear, exactly, but in a disorienting sense that the ground has shifted.
    81→
    82→My colleague Kyle Hakes had his moment during the marketing book club I organized around *Co-Intelligence*. He told the group that someone had replicated six months of his market analysis work with a single deep research prompt — in thirty minutes. "70% of the way there," he said. His reaction: "I was like, well, I'm out of a job."
    83→
    84→He was joking. Mostly. But the feeling was real. When you've spent years developing expertise in market analysis and competitive intelligence, and then you watch an AI produce something that's 70% as good in 0.1% of the time, the natural response is existential unease.
    85→
    86→I felt it too. The competitive intelligence work I was doing with Claude wasn't just faster than my old workflow — it was a different thing entirely. The old workflow was labor-intensive and slow, which meant I was selective about what I researched. I only deep-dived into markets that justified the time investment. With Claude, the marginal cost of research dropped close to zero. I could explore markets I'd never have bothered with before, just because the friction was gone.
    87→
    88→This changes the job. Not eliminates it — changes it. The product manager who spends 80% of their time gathering information and 20% thinking about it now spends 20% gathering and 80% thinking. The bottleneck shifts from research to judgment. And judgment, it turns out, is the part that's hard to automate.
    89→
    90→## The Quality Question
    91→
    92→I want to be honest about something. Not everything Claude produced was gold.
    93→
    94→Some market estimates were off — based on outdated data or extrapolations that didn't account for recent disruptions. Some competitive analyses missed nuances that only someone embedded in the industry would catch. Some strategic recommendations were plausible but generic — the kind of thing any competent consultant would suggest.
    95→
    96→The 70% number Kyle mentioned is about right. On a first pass, Claude gets you 70% of the way to a solid competitive intelligence report. The remaining 30% — the part that requires insider knowledge, strategic nuance, and the kind of judgment that comes from years of sitting in customer meetings — that's still you.
    97→
    98→But here's the thing: 70% in thirty minutes versus 100% in two weeks is not a close comparison. The first pass gives you a foundation to build on, a framework to critique, a starting point that's dramatically better than a blank page. And the iteration from 70% to 95% — adding your judgment, your context, your strategic perspective — takes hours, not weeks.
    99→
   100→The competitive intelligence machine wasn't perfect. But it was fast, it was comprehensive, and it freed me to do the part of my job that actually requires a human: deciding what the information means.
   101→
   102→## What This Changed
   103→
   104→By the end of those early weeks, I had produced competitive intelligence reports covering markets I'd never have had time to analyze before. Each one followed the same pattern — Claude's breadth plus my judgment, iterated through conversation until we arrived at something genuinely useful.
   105→
   106→But the bigger change was subtler. My relationship to information itself had shifted. I'd gone from being a person who carefully selected which questions to research (because research was expensive in time) to a person who could research anything, anytime, and focus my energy on the higher-order question: *So what?*
   107→
   108→The "so what" is where product managers earn their keep. And Claude had cleared the path to get there faster.
   109→
   110→The competitive intelligence work was happening alongside the philosopher tools, alongside the blog posts, alongside the strategic memos. All of it feeding into a growing body of work that would need a unifying infrastructure. That infrastructure had a name — the Model Context Protocol — and it was about to change everything. But that's the next chapter.
   111→

Tool reply2026-02-23T00:08:39.163Z
     1→---
     2→title: "Chapter 5: The MCP Revolution"
     3→status: draft-v1
     4→last_editor: claude
     5→notes: "Covers the Model Context Protocol — the MATLAB MCP Core Server, the 'USB-C for AI' metaphor, MCP server experiments, the onboarding campaign, commands vs. skills, and agents calling agents"
     6→---
     7→
     8→# Chapter 5: The MCP Revolution
     9→
    10→Everything I'd built so far — the philosopher tools, the competitive intelligence reports, the blog posts, the strategic memos — had one thing in common. They were separate. Each tool lived in its own world. Each workflow started from scratch. The blog posts didn't know about the CLI tools. The research didn't feed back into the presentations. The competitive intelligence existed in documents, not in systems.
    11→
    12→What I needed was infrastructure. A way to connect all of these pieces so that agents could use them together, not just one at a time. That infrastructure had a name, and it was about to become the central theme of everything I did.
    13→
    14→The Model Context Protocol. MCP.
    15→
    16→## USB-C for AI
    17→
    18→If you are like me, you remember the era of proprietary chargers. Every device had its own connector — a different shape, a different voltage, a different cable cluttering your desk drawer. Then USB-C arrived and suddenly everything plugged into everything. One standard. Universal compatibility. The end of cable chaos.
    19→
    20→MCP is USB-C for AI.
    21→
    22→That's the metaphor that stuck. Not because I coined it — it was already circulating in the developer community — but because it captured exactly what was happening. Before MCP, connecting an AI agent to an external tool meant building a custom integration for every combination. Claude needs to use MATLAB? Build a bespoke bridge. Copilot needs to access your database? Write another one. Every agent-tool pair required its own plumbing.
    23→
    24→MCP changed the equation. It defined a standard protocol — a universal connector — that any AI agent could use to talk to any tool. Build one MCP server for your tool, and suddenly every MCP-compatible agent can use it. Claude Code, GitHub Copilot, Cursor, Windsurf — all of them, through the same interface.
    25→
    26→For someone working at MathWorks, this wasn't an abstract standard. It was a strategic imperative.
    27→
    28→## The MATLAB MCP Core Server
    29→
    30→The MATLAB MCP Core Server became the centerpiece of my work. I wrote about it, created tutorials for it, built onboarding materials around it, and positioned it as the bridge between MATLAB and the agentic AI ecosystem.
    31→
    32→The server itself was elegant in its simplicity. Five tools:
    33→
    34→1. **`detect_matlab_toolboxes`** — Reports which toolboxes are installed. Simple, but essential. An agent needs to know what capabilities are available before it can plan how to solve a problem.
    35→2. **`check_matlab_code`** — Runs MATLAB's built-in `checkcode` function for style and correctness analysis. The agent can lint your code before running it.
    36→3. **`evaluate_matlab_code`** — Executes MATLAB code and returns the results. This is the core — the ability for an agent to actually *use* MATLAB, not just talk about it.
    37→4. **`start_matlab` / `quit_matlab`** — Session management. The agent can spin up a MATLAB instance and tear it down when it's done.
    38→
    39→Five tools. That's it. But those five tools turned MATLAB from a standalone application into something that any AI agent could call programmatically. The strategic implications were enormous.
    40→
    41→I remember the moment it clicked for me — watching Claude Code call `evaluate_matlab_code`, run a computation, inspect the output, decide it needed to adjust a parameter, and call it again. No human in the loop. The agent was *using* MATLAB the way a developer would. Not generating MATLAB code and hoping you'd copy-paste it (the ChatGPT pattern), but actually running it, reading the results, and iterating.
    42→
    43→This is the difference between AI that generates code and AI that uses tools. MCP made the latter possible.
    44→
    45→## The Integration Play
    46→
    47→Here's the positioning insight that emerged, and it was one of the most important strategic ideas I contributed during the pilot:
    48→
    49→> This is an **integration play**, not putting agentic front and center. MATLAB "integrates" with coding agents like Claude Code and GitHub Copilot. We're showing MATLAB users that they're not left behind.
    50→
    51→The temptation for any established software company watching the agentic AI wave is to panic and try to build their own AI agent from scratch. "We need our own copilot! Our own agentic platform!" That's the instinct, and it's usually wrong.
    52→
    53→The smarter play — the one I kept advocating — was integration. MATLAB doesn't need to be an AI agent. MATLAB needs to be the tool that AI agents reach for when they need to do serious computation. The MCP server was the bridge that made this possible.
    54→
    55→I developed this into a pocket guide strategy aimed at two audiences:
    56→
    57→**Primary audience:** MATLAB users evaluating coding agents. The people asking, "Does MATLAB work with these new coding agents I keep hearing about?" These are the users who've seen colleagues using Copilot or Claude Code and are wondering if they're being left behind. The answer: no. MATLAB works with all of them, through MCP.
    58→
    59→**Secondary audience:** MATLAB users who already use GitHub Copilot and want to extend it. They've got the coding agent — now they want it to actually run MATLAB, not just generate MATLAB code. The MCP server closes that gap.
    60→
    61→> "If you can think of it as an AND — if we can do for humans AND for AI, then maybe we have something here."
    62→
    63→That became my closing line in more than one presentation. MathWorks serving both humans and AI agents. Not zero-sum. An AND.
    64→
    65→## The MCP Server Experiments
    66→
    67→The MATLAB MCP Core Server was just the beginning. Once you understand the MCP pattern, you start seeing servers everywhere.
    68→
    69→**ThingSpeak** — IoT data access via MCP. ThingSpeak is MathWorks' platform for IoT data storage and analysis. Wrapping it in an MCP server meant that any AI agent could query IoT sensor data, analyze trends, and trigger actions — all through the same standard protocol.
    70→
    71→**Google Spreadsheet** — Private data access. This one actually got deployed. My colleague and I built an MCP server that let agents read from and write to Google Sheets — a bridge between the structured world of spreadsheets and the conversational world of AI agents. It sounds mundane, but think about how many business workflows live in spreadsheets. Giving agents access to that data, through a standard interface, unlocked an enormous amount of practical utility.
    72→
    73→**MATLAB Remote Service** — For remote execution in the cloud or on a local server. This was the ambitious one. Instead of requiring a local MATLAB installation, the remote server let agents call MATLAB running somewhere else — in the cloud, on a compute cluster, on a colleague's machine. The MCP server abstracted away the location, so the agent didn't care whether MATLAB was local or remote.
    74→
    75→Each server was a small project, but together they painted a picture: MCP wasn't just a way to connect one tool to one agent. It was the foundation for an ecosystem. Every server you build multiplies the capabilities of every agent that connects to it.
    76→
    77→## The Blog Post Machine
    78→
    79→The blog posts I'd started drafting in Week 1 had a recurring character: MCP. Almost every post I published on the MATLAB AI Blog connected back to the protocol:
    80→
    81→- **MATLAB MCP Core Server** — the foundational post, announcing the server and explaining the architecture
    82→- **Connecting to Hardware** — data acquisition and image acquisition devices via MCP. The physical world, accessible to agents
    83→- **Deploying a Quant Agent** — financial analysis with MATLAB as the computational backbone, connected through MCP
    84→- **Giving Local AI Agents the Ability to Use MATLAB** — the on-ramp for users running local models
    85→- **Building an Optimization Agent** — engineering optimization workflows, orchestrated through MCP
    86→- **Automating the Documentation of MATLAB Analysis** — the full pipeline from analysis to documentation, with MCP as the connective tissue
    87→
    88→Six posts, all variations on the same theme: MATLAB + MCP = MATLAB in the agentic era. The blog posts weren't just content marketing. They were proof of concept. Each one demonstrated a different use case, a different audience, a different reason why the MCP integration mattered.
    89→
    90→## The Onboarding Campaign
    91→
    92→As I went deeper into the MCP ecosystem, I realized something: the technology was only half the challenge. The other half was getting people to use it.
    93→
    94→So I built an onboarding package. A complete Claude Code 101 for MathWorkers: installation guide, CLAUDE.md files for common project types, slash commands for repeatable workflows, MCP server setup instructions, Git workflow best practices, tips and tricks I'd accumulated over weeks of daily use.
    95→
    96→This is where the Claude Code features I'd been exploring became teachable material:
    97→
    98→**`/init`** — Bootstrap a project with CLAUDE.md and initial configuration. The command that turns a blank directory into a project that Claude understands. I showed colleagues how one command could set up the context, conventions, and instructions that would make every subsequent conversation more productive.
    99→
   100→**`/context`** — Load context into a session. The ability to bring Claude up to speed on a project without re-explaining everything from scratch. For someone who'd experienced the "every session starts from zero" frustration of the ChatGPT era, this was transformative.
   101→
   102→**`/commands`** — Custom slash commands for repeatable workflows. The bridge between "I do this manually every time" and "I type one command and it happens." I built commands for blog post drafting, for presentation generation, for competitive intelligence — all the workflows I'd been doing manually, now formalized and shareable.
   103→
   104→The distinction between commands and skills became one of my favorite things to explain:
   105→
   106→| Aspect | Commands | Skills |
   107→|--------|----------|--------|
   108→| Invocation | Manual: /command | Automatic: Claude decides |
   109→| Structure | Single .md file | Directory with SKILL.md |
   110→| Location | .claude/commands/ | .claude/skills/ |
   111→| Discovery | User must type command | Claude matches description |
   112→| Best for | Quick templates | Complex workflows |
   113→
   114→Commands are things you ask for. Skills are things Claude recognizes it needs. The difference is subtle but profound — it's the difference between a tool you pick up and a capability that activates itself when the situation calls for it.
   115→
   116→## Agents Calling Agents
   117→
   118→And then things got weird. In a good way.
   119→
   120→The MCP thesis was "agents calling tools" — an AI agent reaching out to external capabilities through a standard protocol. But somewhere around Week 3, a more advanced pattern emerged: **agents calling agents.**
   121→
   122→Not just Claude using MATLAB. Claude orchestrating Codex and Gemini CLI. Multiple AI agents, working in parallel, each assigned a different piece of a larger task. Claude as the conductor, the other agents as the orchestra.
   123→
   124→The inspiration came from NetworkChuck — the idea of sending multiple agents down different research paths and synthesizing the results. Deep research, but parallelized. Instead of Claude doing everything itself, it could delegate: "You go research the market size. You go analyze the technical architecture. You go find the competitive landscape." Then it would synthesize the results into something more comprehensive than any single agent could produce.
   125→
   126→Gemini CLI's headless mode made this practical. OpenAI's Codex CLI offered another execution path. Even GitHub Copilot could be subcontracted through the `/github:AssignCodingAgent` MCP command — Claude assigning implementation work to Copilot through GitHub Issues.
   127→
   128→This escalation from tool-use to agent-orchestration felt like watching a gear shift. The MCP server gave agents hands. Agents calling agents gave them a team. The metaphor wasn't a single worker with better tools anymore — it was a manager coordinating a team of specialists, each with their own strengths.
   129→
   130→I showed this slide to the industry marketing team during my Week 3 presentation. I could see the moment it clicked for some of them — and the moment it overwhelmed others. The gap between "I use ChatGPT for emails" and "I orchestrate multiple AI agents in parallel" was wider than anyone had expected.
   131→
   132→## What MCP Actually Changed
   133→
   134→Here's what I think the MCP revolution is really about.
   135→
   136→Before MCP, every AI integration was a one-off. You'd connect one agent to one tool, and the integration was fragile, proprietary, and hard to maintain. The cost of connecting anything to anything was high, so people connected very little.
   137→
   138→After MCP, connection became cheap. Build one server, and every agent can use it. Build one client, and every server is accessible. The network effects kick in: every new server makes every agent more capable, and every new agent makes every server more valuable.
   139→
   140→For MathWorks, this meant MATLAB could join the agentic ecosystem without rebuilding itself. The MCP server was the bridge — lightweight, standards-based, and infinitely extensible. For me personally, it meant the philosopher tools, the competitive intelligence workflows, the blog post pipelines, and the MATLAB integrations could all talk to each other through the same protocol.
   141→
   142→The infrastructure I'd been looking for — the thing that would connect all the separate pieces of work into a coherent system — had been there all along, waiting for me to understand it.
   143→
   144→And understanding it changed everything that came after. The side projects were about to get ambitious. But that's the next chapter.
   145→

     1→---
     2→title: "Chapter 6: The Side Projects That Kept Growing"
     3→status: draft-v1
     4→last_editor: claude
     5→notes: "Covers the transformation of side projects from one-shot demos to evolving portfolio — recettesdezoe.com business validation, sports analytics YouTube channel, music recommendation deep dive"
     6→---
     7→
     8→# Chapter 6: The Side Projects That Kept Growing
     9→
    10→If you are like me, you probably have a graveyard of side projects. Half-finished repos, abandoned domain names, ideas that burned bright for an evening and cooled by morning. The normal lifecycle of a side project is: excitement, prototype, neglect, guilt, quiet deletion. Maybe you keep them around in a private GitHub folder as monuments to good intentions. I certainly did.
    11→
    12→Something different happened this time.
    13→
    14→It was early January, a few weeks into the new year. I was in the terminal one evening, working on something for the recipe site — I don't even remember what, probably a content pipeline — when I realized I had three different projects open in three different tabs, and all of them were *active*. Not in the "I should really get back to that" sense. Active as in: I'd made progress on each one in the past week, and each one had a clear next step waiting for me. I stopped typing and tried to remember the last time that had happened with a side project. I couldn't.
    15→
    16→Over the previous chapters, I'd built an infrastructure I hadn't planned. The philosopher tools gave me reusable CLIs and a workflow for turning ideas into distributable software. The competitive intelligence work with Claude taught me how to research markets rigorously instead of guessing. The MCP revolution connected my tools to each other and to external services in ways that multiplied what a single person could do. None of this had been designed as a system. It was more like — each piece created the conditions for the next piece to exist.
    17→
    18→And then the side projects stopped being "side." They started growing on their own.
    19→
    20→Not all of them. Not into businesses. But the pattern was unmistakable: projects that would normally have died after one session were instead evolving, branching, connecting to each other. The graveyard wasn't filling up anymore. Things were staying alive.
    21→
    22→This chapter is about three of those projects — a recipe website that almost became a business, a YouTube channel that became a laboratory, and a music recommendation app that became a philosophical statement. Each one started as an island. Each one turned into something I didn't expect.
    23→
    24→## recettesdezoe.com — From Demo to Business?
    25→
    26→You already know the origin story from Chapter 1. Late-night kitchen session, father-in-law watching over my shoulder, a recipe website conjured from a single ChatGPT prompt, domain purchased at 2am. Then the surprise: 1,300 organic visitors four months later, with zero marketing, zero updates, zero effort.
    27→
    28→What you don't know is what happened next.
    29→
    30→The traffic numbers broke something in my mental model, I said that already. But breaking a mental model is one thing. Deciding what to build on the rubble is another. Because once I had evidence that people were actually visiting this site — cooking from it, maybe — I had to ask a question I hadn't planned on asking: *Is this a product?*
    31→
    32→I did what any self-respecting product manager would do with an existential question about a 2am side project. I reached for a framework.
    33→
    34→### The Mom Test
    35→
    36→Rob Fitzpatrick's *The Mom Test* is the gold standard for product validation. The core idea is deceptively simple: stop asking people if your idea is good (they'll lie to be polite) and start asking about their actual behavior. Don't say "Would you use a recipe website?" Say "Tell me about the last time you looked for a recipe online. What happened?"
    37→
    38→I couldn't interview the 1,300 visitors — I didn't know who they were. But I could do the next best thing: study the market they were already choosing from.
    39→
    40→### The Competitive Landscape
    41→
    42→This is where the Chapter 4 pattern kicked in. I'd spent weeks training Claude to be a competitive intelligence partner — structured research, market sizing, strategic analysis. Now I turned that same lens on the French recipe market. And the landscape was sobering.
    43→
    44→**Marmiton** — the absolute behemoth. 10 million monthly visitors, user-generated content, a brand so embedded in French cooking culture that "je vais regarder sur Marmiton" is practically a reflex. **750g** (owned by Webedia) — professional content, video-forward, with the media infrastructure to dominate on search and social. **Cuisine AZ** — another giant, part of the same media group as 750g, covering every conceivable recipe with industrial-scale content production.
    45→
    46→Then there was recettesdezoe.com. A site built in a kitchen. By a product manager. At 2am.
    47→
    48→The analysis was honest, and Claude didn't sugarcoat it: the French recipe market was a red ocean. The incumbents had decades of SEO authority, millions of indexed pages, and content pipelines that no solo operator could match on volume. The 1,300 visitors were real — probably finding the site through long-tail keywords that the giants hadn't bothered to optimize for. But scaling against Marmiton was like opening a corner bakery and hoping to outcompete an industrial bread factory.
    49→
    50→The insight wasn't "give up." It was "compete differently." The big players wrote recipes like food blogs — SEO-optimized, template-driven, drowning in ads and pop-ups. recettesdezoe.com had something they didn't: a voice.
    51→
    52→### The Voice Test
    53→
    54→This was the moment where Chapter 2's voice analysis came back in a new form. Remember the blogging workflow I'd developed with Claude — the careful attention to preserving an authentic writing voice, the resistance to letting AI flatten everything into generic corporate prose? The same principle applied here, but the domain was entirely different.
    55→
    56→I defined a quality test for any recipe the site would publish:
    57→
    58→> "Could this have been written by someone sharing their grandmother's recipe over coffee? If it sounds like a food blog template or a restaurant menu, it's wrong."
    59→
    60→That was the moat. Not SEO. Not scale. Voice. The feeling that a real person had cooked this dish, tasted it, adjusted the seasoning, and wanted to tell you about it the way they'd tell a friend.
    61→
    62→### Content Scaling with Zoé
    63→
    64→If voice was the moat, I still needed a way to produce content without spending every evening writing recipes by hand. This is where the Chapter 3 pattern kicked in again: build tools, not just skills.
    65→
    66→I designed a CLI tool for recipe generation. I called it "Zoé" — the same name as my daughter, the same name that was already in the domain. If the philosopher tools got names that carried conceptual meaning, this one carried something more personal: a family name, a familial tradition. Where montaigne.cc and nietzsche.cc encoded philosophical values, Zoé encoded the domestic warmth that the whole project was supposed to embody.
    67→
    68→The tool was built with the Copilot SDK and followed a structured workflow: research a recipe category, generate drafts that matched the established voice, validate against the Voice Test, and produce Markdown files ready for the site. Claude was the engine, but the CLI was the guardrail — deterministic, repeatable, consistent. A skill would have drifted with every conversation. A tool kept the voice stable.
    69→
    70→Did recettesdezoe.com become a business? No. Not yet, maybe not ever. But it stopped being a demo. It had a competitive analysis, a content strategy, a voice standard, and a production tool named after my daughter. The 2am party trick had become something I could pick up, work on for an evening, put down, and come back to a week later without starting from zero.
    71→
    72→That's what "kept growing" means. Not exponential growth. Not viral success. Just — alive.
    73→
    74→## The YouTube Channel That Became a Lab
    75→
    76→@yannspeaksai started as a way to document my projects. Record what I'm building, explain the process, put it out there. The kind of thing a lot of technical people do — part portfolio, part accountability mechanism, part excuse to learn video editing.
    77→
    78→Seven videos in 2025. Five different sports. Five completely different technology stacks. And without planning it, the channel became less of a content outlet and more of a laboratory — each video an experiment in a different domain, with the only constant being Claude as a collaborator.
    79→
    80→### Tennis — Predicting the French Open
    81→
    82→The first sports video was about prediction. I built an ELO rating system enhanced with machine learning to forecast French Open match outcomes. The model achieved 75% accuracy, which sounds impressive until you realize that tennis prediction is a well-studied problem and the best models in the literature hover around the same range. The point wasn't to beat the state of the art. The point was that a product manager with no background in sports statistics could build a credible prediction system in a few evenings.
    83→
    84→The stack was MATLAB for the analysis and Python for the data pipeline. Claude handled the integration between the two — something that would have taken me days of Stack Overflow diving in the Before Claude Code era.
    85→
    86→### NBA — Dashboards and Automation
    87→
    88→The basketball project pushed into web development territory. Season ranking animations, a React dashboard showing team statistics, and — the part I was proudest of — a full GitHub Actions CI/CD pipeline that automatically updated the data and rebuilt the visualizations.
    89→
    90→This was the "build tools, not skills" philosophy applied to content creation. I didn't just make a dashboard. I built a system that kept the dashboard alive. Every new game night triggered an automatic update. The infrastructure outlasted the session that created it.
    91→
    92→### NFL — Clutch Analysis
    93→
    94→The American football project was the most MATLAB-native. Clutch play analysis — identifying which plays in which moments had the highest impact on game outcomes. I built it as a MATLAB toolbox, complete with documentation and examples. It was the kind of niche analytical tool that a few hundred sports analytics enthusiasts might find useful, and that I'd never have built if the cost of building it had been measured in weeks instead of evenings.
    95→
    96→### Cycling — The Ghost That Kept Running
    97→
    98→And then there was cycling. GhostRunners.app from Chapter 1 — the ride comparison tool built with MATLAB Mobile data capture. The clearest example of "kept growing" in the entire portfolio.
    99→
   100→In the pre-Claude era, GhostRunners was a functional but rough prototype. MATLAB Mobile captured the data, MATLAB Online crunched the analysis, Python handled the Garmin FIT file parsing. It worked. I recorded YouTube videos about it. And then it sat there.
   101→
   102→With Claude, I came back to it. The MCP server connected MATLAB to the broader workflow. The CLI patterns from the philosopher tools gave me a structure for the user interface. The CI/CD learnings from the NBA project meant I could automate the data pipeline. Each previous project had deposited a capability that GhostRunners could now draw on.
   103→
   104→The ghost was still running. But now it was running faster.
   105→
   106→### The Pattern
   107→
   108→Here's what the YouTube channel taught me: **the marginal cost of exploration had dropped to near zero.**
   109→
   110→Each video required learning a new domain — tennis statistics, NBA data APIs, NFL play-by-play formats, cycling biomechanics. In the old world, the domain learning alone would have consumed weeks. With Claude as a research and implementation partner, I could go from "I know nothing about ELO ratings" to "here's a working prediction system" in an evening or two.
   111→
   112→Five sports. Five stacks. One year. The channel wasn't just documenting what I built. It was proving, video by video, that the barrier to entry for any technical domain had collapsed. Not to zero — you still needed taste, judgment, and the willingness to learn. But the activation energy had dropped from "this will take months, is it worth it?" to "let's find out this weekend."
   113→
   114→## Gaining Back Control Over the Algorithm
   115→
   116→The music project started with an itch.
   117→
   118→If you use Spotify — and statistically, you probably do — you've experienced this: the algorithm decides what you like. It feeds you recommendations based on your listening history, and over time, your taste and the algorithm's model of your taste begin to converge. You listen to what it suggests, which reinforces its model, which narrows its suggestions, which narrows what you listen to. The feedback loop is invisible and relentless.
   119→
   120→I didn't hate Spotify's recommendations. Some of them were genuinely good. But I resented the passivity of it. My music taste was becoming a mirror of an algorithm's assumptions about me, and I wanted to break out of that loop.
   121→
   122→### The Architecture
   123→
   124→The idea was simple in concept, ambitious in scope: build a personal music recommendation system using Spotify's own audio features — energy, acousticness, danceability, valence, tempo — but with *my* logic instead of *their* algorithm.
   125→
   126→The core engine was a K-nearest neighbors (KNN) model. Take a song I loved, find the songs in my library that were most similar across those audio features, and surface the ones I'd forgotten about or never noticed. Not discovery of new music (Spotify already does that well) but rediscovery of music I already owned and had lost in the algorithmic shuffle.
   127→
   128→Claude helped me design the system architecture and integrate the Spotify API. The data pipeline pulled my listening history and audio features for every track. The KNN model ran locally — no cloud dependency, no algorithm I didn't control. The recommendations were mine, based on rules I could see and modify.
   129→
   130→### Form Factors
   131→
   132→This is where the project got interesting. Once the recommendation engine existed, the question became: how do you interact with it?
   133→
   134→I explored four different form factors, each one a different answer to that question:
   135→
   136→**Web app** — the obvious first choice. A clean interface where you pick a seed song and see recommendations. Good for exploration, but it required a browser and a server.
   137→
   138→**CLI tool** — because by this point, everything I built had a CLI. Type a song name, get recommendations in the terminal. Fast, scriptable, composable with other tools. The philosopher tool ethos applied to music.
   139→
   140→**Desktop app** — for a more visual experience. Album art, audio previews, the kind of rich interface that a terminal can't provide.
   141→
   142→**Hybrid** — the most ambitious version. A CLI that could launch a lightweight web interface when you wanted the visual experience, but stayed in the terminal when you wanted speed. The best of both worlds, if you could stomach the complexity.
   143→
   144→Each form factor taught me something different about interface design. But the lesson that stuck was this: having the recommendation engine as a separate, well-defined module meant I could experiment with interfaces freely. The tool-first approach from Chapter 3 — building the engine before the interface — paid dividends I hadn't anticipated.
   145→
   146→### Use Cases That Multiplied
   147→
   148→The recommendation engine was the seed, but the use cases kept branching:
   149→
   150→**Local music management** — I had years of downloaded music scattered across hard drives. The same audio feature analysis that powered recommendations could also organize, tag, and deduplicate my local collection.
   151→
   152→**Personal Spotify Wrapped** — Spotify's annual summary is fun but superficial. With raw access to my listening data and audio features, I could build something deeper: trends in my taste over time, the acoustic signature of my moods, the genres I gravitated to during specific seasons.
   153→
   154→**Mood-based playlists** — Instead of Spotify's generic "Chill" or "Focus" categories, playlists generated from my own definition of what those moods sounded like, calibrated to my library, my features, my thresholds.
   155→
   156→### The Philosophical Resonance
   157→
   158→Here's where the project connected back to something deeper.
   159→
   160→In Chapter 3, the existentialist synthesis emerged: *"The existentialists ask 'how should I live authentically?' while Mollick and Harari ask 'how should we live with AI?' These questions are not separate."* The philosopher tools were answers to philosophical questions encoded in code.
   161→
   162→The music project was the same impulse, just in a different domain. Building your own recommendation algorithm is an act of self-determination. It says: I refuse to let a platform decide what I like. I'll examine my own taste, build my own model of it, and use that model to rediscover what I've been missing. Socrates meets Spotify. The examined playlist is worth listening to.
   163→
   164→> "Gaining back control over the algorithm isn't about rejecting technology. It's about choosing which technology reflects who you are instead of who a platform thinks you should be."
   165→
   166→This wasn't just a music project. It was the book's thesis — AI as amplifier of human intention — applied to the most personal domain I could think of: what sounds fill your house on a Sunday morning.
   167→
   168→## The Portfolio Effect
   169→
   170→Let me step back and look at what had happened.
   171→
   172→In Chapter 1, I described a pattern: *"Every conversation started from zero. Every project was an island."* recettesdezoe.com was a single-session creation. GhostRunners was a rough prototype that worked but couldn't grow. The YouTube channel was a series of disconnected experiments. Each project lived in its own bubble, with no shared infrastructure, no common patterns, no way for one to feed into another.
   173→
   174→By the time I'd reached the projects in this chapter, that had changed entirely. The projects weren't islands anymore — they were nodes in a network.
   175→
   176→recettesdezoe.com drew on the competitive intelligence patterns from Chapter 4 for its market analysis, on the voice preservation techniques from Chapter 2 for its content standard, and on the "build tools, not skills" philosophy from Chapter 3 for the Zoé CLI. The YouTube sports projects cross-pollinated: CI/CD from the NBA project fed into GhostRunners, MATLAB toolbox patterns from the NFL project informed the cycling analysis, and every project deposited knowledge that the next one could draw on. The music recommendation app used the same CLI architecture as the philosopher tools, the same research methodology as the competitive analysis, and connected back to the existentialist themes that ran through the whole journey.
   177→
   178→The infrastructure chapters — the philosopher tools, the competitive intelligence machine, the MCP revolution — hadn't just produced outputs. They had produced a *way of working*. A set of patterns and capabilities that made every subsequent project cheaper, faster, and more connected than it would have been in isolation.
   179→
   180→This is the portfolio effect. Not a planned portfolio — I never sat down and said "I'm going to build an interconnected network of side projects." It emerged from the compounding of tools, patterns, and knowledge across dozens of conversations with Claude. Each project made the next one possible. Each tool reduced the cost of the next tool. Each experiment deposited a capability that future experiments could inherit.
   181→
   182→I want to be honest about the limits. Most of these projects are still side projects. recettesdezoe.com isn't generating revenue. The YouTube channel has a modest audience. The music app is something I use personally. None of this is a startup success story.
   183→
   184→But that's precisely the point. The transformation wasn't about commercial outcomes — it was about the *cost of exploration*. When it takes weeks to build something, you have to be strategic about what you build. You plan, you evaluate, you calculate the expected return on investment. When it takes an evening, you can afford to follow curiosity wherever it leads.
   185→
   186→> "When exploration becomes cheap, you stop asking 'is this worth my time?' and start asking 'what am I curious about?'"
   187→
   188→That shift — from strategic allocation of scarce building capacity to abundant, curiosity-driven exploration — is, I think, the most underappreciated consequence of working with an AI collaborator. The projects themselves matter less than the fact that they exist at all. Each one is evidence that the barrier between "I wonder if..." and "let's find out" has been nearly erased.
   189→
   190→## What the Side Projects Taught Me
   191→
   192→The side projects taught me something that the work projects couldn't.
   193→
   194→At MathWorks, the value of Claude was measurable — faster blog posts, sharper competitive analyses, better presentations. The ROI story was clear and presentable. But the side projects operated outside the logic of ROI. Nobody asked me to build a recipe website or a music recommendation engine. Nobody was tracking the output. The only metric was whether I wanted to keep going.
   195→
   196→And I kept going. That was the data point that mattered.
   197→
   198→Because when you strip away the professional context — the reporting obligations, the pilot program requirements, the need to justify your tools budget — what you're left with is the raw question: *Do I want to spend my evenings doing this?* And the answer, project after project, evening after evening, was yes.
   199→
   200→There's a specific kind of knowledge that comes from building things nobody asked you to build. Work projects teach you what's possible. Side projects teach you what's *exciting*. And when the time came to explain this journey to colleagues — to stand in front of a room and make the case that something fundamental had changed — I needed both kinds of knowledge. The work projects gave me the evidence: productivity gains, workflow improvements, measurable outputs. But the side projects gave me the conviction. The ability to say, with genuine enthusiasm and not just professional obligation, that this technology had changed how I spent my free time. That I *chose* to be in the terminal at 11pm on a Tuesday, not because anyone was watching, but because the next experiment was calling.
   201→
   202→That conviction was about to matter more than I expected. Over the following weeks, I'd present to three different teams at MathWorks, each time refining the message, each time drawing on both the professional evidence and the personal story. The internal evangelism campaign was about to begin — and with it, a new challenge: translating a felt experience into something that could change other people's minds.
   203→
   204→But that's the next chapter.
   205→


Tool reply2026-02-23T00:08:40.245Z
     1→---
     2→title: "Chapter 7: Writing the Future — Sharing Knowledge"
     3→status: draft-v1
     4→last_editor: claude
     5→notes: "Covers the internal evangelism campaign — three presentations to different marketing groups, the live demo that changed minds, the blog post feedback loop, the Marketing Book Club on Co-Intelligence, and the transition from user to advocate"
     6→---
     7→
     8→# Chapter 7: Writing the Future — Sharing Knowledge
     9→
    10→By mid-January 2026, I had been using Claude Code for about six weeks. I'd built tools, sharpened strategies, drafted blog posts at a pace that surprised even me, and spent more late nights in the terminal than I probably should have. But now I faced a challenge that no amount of agentic wizardry could automate: explaining what I'd found to colleagues who hadn't experienced it.
    11→
    12→The pilot had a simple requirement — report back on your usage in Q1. What started as a reporting obligation became something I hadn't anticipated: an internal evangelism campaign.
    13→
    14→You might know the feeling if you are passionate about tech. You discover something that changes how you work, and suddenly you can't stop talking about it. The problem is that enthusiasm without structure is just noise. I needed to show, not tell. And I needed to tailor the message to audiences who cared about very different things.
    15→
    16→## The Presentation That Kept Evolving
    17→
    18→The story of these presentations actually begins in September 2025, months before Claude Code entered my life (remember chapter 1). My dear colleague Jacob George and I had co-created this deck called "GenAI Tools for Modern Tech Marketers" — a practical overview of how marketers could use AI tools to speed up their workflows. It was a solid deck. Useful. Conventional.
    19→
    20→By January, that deck had been transformed beyond recognition. Most of the original slides were gone. In their place was something called "Agentic Learnings" — and over the next six weeks, I would rewrite it every week, each time for a different audience. Each version wasn't just a reshuffled slide order. Each one reflected genuine learning, additional weeks of experience, and a deeper understanding of what mattered to the people in the room.
    21→
    22→### Outbound Marketing — The Workflow Version
    23→
    24→The first presentation landed on January 15th, Week 2 of the new year. I presented to the outbound marketing team, together with Eric, the head of product management for MATLAB. The subtitle said it plainly: "Learnings after a few weeks of Claude Code."
    25→
    26→I organized everything into three categories that would carry through all subsequent presentations:
    27→
    28→**Agentic MVP** — building apps and prototypes. I posed the question the audience was probably thinking: "MVP for Minimal Viable Product or Minimal Viable Prototype?" The ambiguity was intentional. This wasn't just Vibe coding web sites anymore. It was architecture work, guiding a senior developer (Claude).
    29→
    30→**Agentic Digital Marketing** — automating the boring steps of the blogging workflow so you can concentrate on ideation and code writing. I showed the workflow diagram: Claude handles formatting, structuring, and first-pass drafts. The human focuses on ideas and technical content.
    31→
    32→**Agentic Market Intelligence** — this was the one that made people lean forward. I showed the PIDO example: Process Integration and Design Optimization market analysis from a single prompt. The output? A comprehensive analysis: "$200M USD market in 2024, growing at 10% CAGR," with strategic implications included. One prompt. What used to take a week of research distilled into minutes.
    33→
    34→The outbound team needed concrete before-and-after comparisons of their daily work — blogging, competitive analysis, community monitoring. So that's what I gave them. This was the most workflow-oriented of the three decks.
    35→
    36→### Industry Marketing — The Technically Ambitious Version
    37→
    38→A week later, same core material, completely different emphasis. The industry marketing team needed to understand the competitive landscape — that was their job, after all. So I added depth.
    39→
    40→A new category appeared that hadn't existed in the outbound deck. **Agentic Development** showed Claude Code porting a Python package to MATLAB — a technical use case proving this wasn't just a marketing productivity tool. 
    41→
    42→Then some more non-coding related use cases.The deep research examples were chosen for strategic relevance. I showed a neuroscience community toolbox analysis — FieldTrip, EEGLAB, MNE-Python. The research revealed a bifurcation: MATLAB-based tools historically dominant, Python-based alternatives rapidly growing with zero licensing costs. EEGLAB leading citations at 21,700+, MNE-Python showing the fastest growth. That kind of intelligence was directly actionable for an academic disciplines and industry marketing team deciding where to invest.
    43→
    44→The weekly learnings section had evolved too. By Week 3, the slides carried three layers of accumulating insight — from Agentic 101 through tool-building to multi-agent orchestration. The audience could see the learning curve accelerating in real time.
    45→
    46→### Educator Programs — The One Built from Scratch
    47→
    48→By Week 6, I didn't just update the deck. I rebuilt it entirely.
    49→
    50→The educator audience cared about teaching content creation, forum support, and MATLAB live script generation. The generic "blogging workflow" and "market intelligence" sections from earlier decks? Gone. Replaced with education-specific use cases: creating content for a linear algebra workshop at the University of Oklahoma, analyzing community toolboxes for academic adoption, generating teaching materials that could scale.
    51→
    52→This was the most audience-adapted version. And it was the one where something unexpected happened.
    53→
    54→## The Learning Curve in Four Weeks
    55→
    56→Each version of the deck included a "weekly learnings" section — a running log of what I'd discovered since the last presentation. What started as a simple bullet list became, without my planning it, a narrative of transformation. Four weeks, four stages.
    57→
    58→**Week 1: Agentic 101.** Start with Claude Desktop. Get a taste of the agentic capabilities — the ability to read and write directly on the file system. Discover a few concepts: skills, tools, the Model Context Protocol. See how this applies to coding. Begin with Python, then connect to MATLAB via the MCP Core Server. This was the foundation — understanding that an AI agent isn't just a chatbot with delusions of grandeur, but a system that can use tools autonomously. When I presented this to the outbound team, it was already two weeks old. But for most people in the room, it was new. That gap — between where I was and where my audience was — would widen with every passing week.
    59→
    60→**Week 2: Density — less is more.** Focus on just the necessary context. Filter and condense the information you need. The crucial insight: build tools, not just skills. CLI tools can be used by your agents and follow deterministic behavior — a workflow for slide deck creation, information extraction from YouTube videos. This is when the army of philosophers was born: Montaigne, Nietzsche, Schopenhauer. Claude generating GitHub Issues to manage requirements. Tests validated automatically through GitHub Actions. When a test failed, ask the GitHub MCP server to retrieve the trace — no more copy-pasting error logs. `uv` is life. Python for CLI is fire. Publish to PyPI — let the game begin. If Week 1 was about learning what agents could do, Week 2 was about building things that agents could use. The shift from consumer to creator.
    61→
    62→**Week 3: Agents calling agents.** Beyond "agents calling tools" — the MCP thesis — a more advanced pattern emerged: Claude orchestrating Codex and Gemini CLI. Deep research workflows inspired by NetworkChuck — sending multiple agents down different research paths and synthesizing the results. This wasn't just tool use anymore. This was orchestration. The conductor stepping up from the orchestra pit. When I showed this slide to the industry marketing team, I could see the moment it clicked for some of them — and the moment it overwhelmed others. The gap between "I use ChatGPT for emails" and "I orchestrate multiple AI agents in parallel" was wider than anyone had expected.
    63→
    64→**Week 4: The terminal is my happy place.** Building CLIs for Claude and for myself. TUIs — terminal user interfaces, yes, very nerdy. Even SSH apps that could be accessed remotely. The terminal had become home. Not the IDE, not the browser, not the chat window — the terminal. For a non-developer who started this journey in a ChatGPT text box three months earlier, landing in the terminal and feeling comfortable there was perhaps the most telling sign that something fundamental had shifted.
    65→
    66→The progression told a story I hadn't planned but couldn't deny:
    67→
    68→| Week | Theme | Identity |
    69→|------|-------|----------|
    70→| 1 | Agentic 101 | Consumer |
    71→| 2 | Density — less is more | Creator |
    72→| 3 | Agents calling agents | Conductor |
    73→| 4 | The terminal is my happy place | Native |
    74→
    75→Each stage wasn't just a skill upgrade — it was an identity shift. And each presentation caught me at a different point on that curve, which is why no two versions of the deck told the same story. The audience mattered, yes. But so did where I was on the journey. The person who presented Agentic 101 in Week 2 was not the same person who rebuilt the entire deck for educators in Week 6. The tool had changed me, and the presentations were the evidence.
    76→
    77→## The Live Demo That Changed Minds
    78→
    79→The educator meeting had about seven people — Stefano, Jeff, Maria Elena, Will, Nandini, and Ascension joining remotely. I had just walked through the PCA-to-video pipeline: MATLAB script, Claude Code generating content, export to Word, feed into NotebookLM for slides, AI-generated voiceover. Total pipeline time: under 30 minutes. I added the honest caveat: "People don't like the AI-generated voice. So that's the part you should probably get rid of. But the rest is actually quite interesting."
    80→
    81→Then Ascension asked the question that mattered: "What is the agentic part about this?"
    82→
    83→She wasn't being difficult. She was being precise. She wasn't convinced this was different from regular generative AI — from the ChatGPT-era copy-paste workflows we'd all been doing. And she was right to push. If I couldn't explain the distinction clearly, the whole presentation was just a show.
    84→
    85→So I did something I hadn't planned. I opened Claude Code. Live. Unrehearsed.
    86→
    87→I typed `claude` in the terminal and prompted: "Look at the linear algebra folder and the existing examples. Now do the same for Fourier analysis."
    88→
    89→What happened next was the kind of thing you can't fake. Claude Code autonomously explored the folder structure, read the existing MATLAB live scripts, learned the format and conventions from the examples, and generated new Fourier analysis content — code, documentation, everything. It used three or four tools on its own, making decisions about what to read, what to generate, and how to structure the output.
    90→
    91→I used the Matrix analogy to explain what we'd just seen: "I don't know if some of you saw the movie The Matrix... 'I know Kung Fu,' right? Like he plugs himself into the skill and then he knows." Teaching Claude how to write MATLAB live scripts through structured documentation — that was the agentic part. Not just generating text, but autonomously learning and applying a format.
    92→
    93→> "It's a spectacle. Like this just now. It turns into a performance. This thing is insane."
    94→
    95→And the practical advice I gave right after: "Always read the code."
    96→
    97→Ascension's arc during that meeting was the most rewarding part. She started skeptical — "Not yet, Yann. But I'm sure you will impress me." She ended engaged, seeing the potential for scaling educational content production on GitHub and File Exchange. The live demo did what slides never could.
    98→
    99→## The Blog Post Feedback Loop
   100→
   101→Around the same time, a parallel conversation was shaping how I thought about all of this.
   102→
   103→A colleague had given me feedback on a blog post draft — the one about automating documentation with the MCP Core Server. Their response cut right to the core:
   104→
   105→> "Based on the first paragraph, it sounded like you were going to show me how to take an existing script/analysis and get that ready for GitHub. But the workflow below starts from scratch and delegates the analysis to the LLM as well. I actually really liked the idea that you were going with in the first paragraph — do we have like an initial version of the script that we can start from? That way it's still the human doing the 'interesting' parts, and then you're using the AI to do the 'tedious' parts?"
   106→
   107→I read that three times. It crystallized something I'd been circling around in every presentation, every demo, every late-night terminal session: **the human does the interesting parts, AI handles the tedious parts.** Not AI replacing humans. AI as amplifier.
   108→
   109→This wasn't a new idea — but hearing it reflected back from a colleague who had independently arrived at the same framing made it feel like a thesis, not just a hunch. It connected the presentations back to the book's central question: what is this relationship, actually?
   110→
   111→## The Book Club — Co-Intelligence as Mirror
   112→
   113→If the presentations were about showing what Claude Code could do, the Marketing Book Club was about wrestling with what it all meant.
   114→
   115→Kyle, Jacob, and I co-organized the club around Ethan Mollick's "Co-Intelligence." The choice was deliberate — Mollick's book explores practical frameworks for human-AI collaboration, and I'd re-listened to the audiobook recently. It was still remarkably relevant, even though it had been written before the agentic AI wave arrived.
   116→
   117→The first session — Introduction and Chapter 1 — went places I didn't expect.
   118→
   119→Mollick opens with the concept of "Sleepless Nights" — that first visceral encounter with AI that keeps you up. The participants shared their own moments. Kyle had perhaps the most striking one: someone had replicated six months of his market analysis work with a single deep research prompt in thirty minutes. "70% of the way there," he said. His reaction was immediate: "I was like, well, I'm out of a job."
   120→
   121→Six months of work. Thirty minutes. Seventy percent. Those numbers tell a story that no slide deck can.
   122→
   123→The discussion spiraled into territory that felt more like philosophy than a marketing meeting. Mollick's "Alien Mind" metaphor — AI as fundamentally non-human intelligence — resonated with what I'd been experiencing. AI doesn't think like us, but that's exactly what makes it a powerful thinking partner. If it thought like us, it would have the same blind spots.
   124→
   125→I showed them ChatGPT's memory feature, which described me in absurdly flattering terms — "deep technical," endlessly praiseworthy. The room laughed. This was sycophancy in action, the AI telling you what you want to hear. I contrasted it with my experience of Claude pushing back on my strategic memo in the first week. The difference mattered.
   126→
   127→Then I introduced Jean Claude — my personal AI agent on Telegram, complete with a French accent. I called the concept "productive anthropomorphism." Giving AI a persona and a name isn't a cognitive trap — it makes collaboration more intuitive, more natural. You don't anthropomorphize your spreadsheet, but maybe you should anthropomorphize your thinking partner.
   128→
   129→The most substantive exchange came when I posed a question I'd been sitting with for weeks:
   130→
   131→> "When AI starts writing good code, what is the part of your identity that you feel is threatened, if any?"
   132→
   133→David Barnes paused. "I really like that articulation. About somewhat of the identity. It's not something I've thought about before."
   134→
   135→I tried to frame it honestly: "If intelligence is just so cheap, so accessible, so scalable, what is me?... It's not necessarily insecurity, it's evolution."
   136→
   137→I described my personal timeline as having three eras — the OpenAI days, "Google is so back," and now Claude Code. "There's BC — Before ChatGPT. Now there's also BCC — Before Claude Code."
   138→
   139→The session ended with a message I'd been building toward across all the presentations and conversations: "If you can think of it as an AND — if we can do for humans AND for AI, then maybe we have something here." MathWorks should serve both humans and AI agents. Not zero-sum. AND.
   140→
   141→## Resistance and Real Concerns
   142→
   143→I'd be painting a dishonest picture if I suggested everyone walked out of these sessions converted. They didn't. And the pushback wasn't ignorance — it was legitimate concern.
   144→
   145→Jeff raised the desktop-versus-cloud tension that ran through the company like a fault line. Agentic tools needed desktop software installed to be most effective, but the education market was demanding a cloud-first experience. That wasn't a problem I could demo my way out of.
   146→
   147→The cost conversation came up in every session. "With great powers come great consumptions," I'd say, and it always got a knowing laugh. The $200-a-month subscription with daily limits was real. I was honest about it: "I have a 5-hour window where I start in the morning and then until 2... at some point I'm eating 100% and then I cannot work anymore. I need to go and chat with colleagues."
   148→
   149→The governance gray areas were real too. What could AI-generated content be used for internally? Externally? Where was the line? "Very gray area" was the consensus, and nobody had a satisfying answer.
   150→
   151→And then there was the elephant in the room that I tried to address head-on: "This is a controversial topic that puts pressure on our very business model." If AI could write MATLAB code, what happened to the value proposition of MATLAB itself? I didn't pretend to have the answer. But I did tell the room: "Don't shoot the messenger. I'm just sharing what's currently out there in the market."
   152→
   153→These concerns weren't obstacles to adoption. They were evidence that the conversations mattered. Easy ideas don't generate pushback. Only ideas that challenge the status quo do.
   154→
   155→The governance question, in particular, hung over every session like a fog that nobody could quite disperse. What could AI-generated content be used for internally? What about externally? If Claude drafted a competitive analysis, could it go into a customer-facing presentation? If it generated MATLAB code examples, who owned the intellectual property? "Very gray area" was the consensus, and nobody — not in marketing, not in legal, not anywhere I could find — had a satisfying answer yet. The honest response was that we were all figuring it out together, in real time, and pretending otherwise would have been the worst kind of evangelism.
   156→
   157→> "The question you need to ask yourself now is not what it can do, but what it cannot do."
   158→
   159→## From User to Advocate
   160→
   161→Looking back at those six weeks, the trajectory surprises me. I walked into the pilot as someone who'd been asked to evaluate a tool. I walked out as something closer to an internal organizer.
   162→
   163→At the end of the educator meeting, after Ascension's skepticism had turned to engagement and the team had seen Claude Code work in real time, I proposed creating a new Gen AI Tiger Team specifically for Educator Programs. Not a vague "let's keep talking about this" — a concrete team with scheduled sessions, a backlog of use cases to explore, and a commitment to document what worked and what didn't. The room said yes. We discussed follow-up sessions for the remaining use cases: community toolboxes, conference report-outs, and more.
   164→
   165→That proposal was one piece of a larger pattern. I was also co-leading the Marketing Book Club on human-AI collaboration, creating onboarding materials for other MathWorkers, and sharing internal tools — the MATLAB Central MCP server, the skills repos, the philosopher-named tool chain.
   166→
   167→The presentations evolved because I evolved. Week 2 was about workflows. Week 3 was about technical possibilities. Week 6 was about meeting people where they were. The book club was about grappling with what it all meant for who we are and what we do.
   168→
   169→What started as "report back on your usage" became something more: an argument that we needed to surf on the wave, because otherwise we were going to get crushed.
   170→
   171→But here's the thing I didn't expect. The most valuable part of having a thinking partnership wasn't what it did for me — it was what it taught me to share. Every demo, every slide, every uncomfortable question from a skeptical colleague pushed me to understand the technology more deeply, articulate its implications more clearly, and confront its limitations more honestly.
   172→
   173→The evangelist doesn't just spread the word. The evangelist refines it.
   174→
   175→And I was about to refine it further — in the most intense way possible. The hackathon was coming.
   176→
