Tool reply2026-02-23T00:08:40.268Z
     1→---
     2→title: "Chapter 8: What This Relationship Actually Is"
     3→status: draft-v1
     4→last_editor: claude
     5→notes: "The concluding chapter — synthesizes the journey across all prior chapters, names the five modes of collaboration, confronts the identity question, and lands on what human-AI partnership actually means"
     6→---
     7→
     8→# Chapter 8: What This Relationship Actually Is
     9→
    10→Three months. That's how long this story spans — December 2025 to February 2026. In the arc of a career, it's nothing. In the arc of what happened, it's everything.
    11→
    12→I started this book with a simple sentence: "GPTs were a thing, and then Claude Code arrived." Now I want to try to explain what that sentence actually means. Not the technology. Not the features. What it means for the person sitting at the keyboard.
    13→
    14→## The Five Modes
    15→
    16→Looking back across all those weeks — the late nights in the terminal, the presentations that kept evolving, the tools named after philosophers, the competitive intelligence that rewired how I thought about research — a pattern emerges. Claude wasn't one thing. It was five things, and the relationship shifted between them constantly.
    17→
    18→**Claude as strategic thinking partner.** This was the first mode I discovered, and in some ways the most surprising. I didn't come to Claude expecting intellectual sparring. I came expecting a faster way to write memos. What I got was an entity that fact-checked my market claims, stress-tested my arguments, and told me where my strategic logic had gaps. The "agent calling toolboxes" positioning wasn't Claude's idea or mine — it was forged in dialogue, each of us pushing back on the other until the argument was sharp enough to present to a director.
    19→
    20→**Claude as creative collaborator.** The philosopher-themed tools. The Zarathustra voiceover script. The rap battle concept. The recipe site content strategy. None of these emerged from a single prompt. They emerged from iterative conversation — one idea feeding the next, building creative momentum that neither of us could have generated alone. Claude didn't create for me. Claude created *with* me, and the distinction matters more than I expected.
    21→
    22→**Claude as research engine.** The competitive intelligence work changed how I understood my own job. I brought context and strategic judgment. Claude brought breadth and synthesis. The PIDO analysis, the neuroscience toolbox landscape, the MATLAB community intelligence — each one followed the same pattern: Claude's breadth plus my judgment, iterated through dialogue until we arrived at something genuinely useful. 70% of the way there in thirty minutes, and then my expertise closing the remaining distance.
    23→
    24→**Claude as writing partner.** My blog voice was analyzed, codified into a skill, and then used to draft posts that sounded like me — not a generic approximation, but specifically like me. My rhythms, my habit of acknowledging what I don't know, my tendency to open with a shared experience. A tool that makes you faster is useful. A tool that learns your voice and helps you sound more like yourself — that's something else.
    25→
    26→**Claude as builder.** Presentations, documents, web apps, architecture designs, onboarding materials, CLI tools published to PyPI, MCP servers deployed and running. Actual artifacts. Not drafts or suggestions — working software and finished documents that existed in the world and did useful things.
    27→
    28→Five modes. One relationship. And the most interesting part is that I rarely chose which mode to operate in. The conversation would shift naturally — from strategy to research to building to writing — and Claude would shift with it. There was no mode switch, no "now I'm your research assistant." The transitions were seamless because the underlying dynamic was consistent: two intelligences working on a shared problem, each contributing what the other couldn't.
    29→
    30→## What Makes This Different
    31→
    32→The introduction to this book established a before and after. Before Claude Code, there was the ChatGPT era — single interactions, disposable sessions, no continuity, no memory. Every conversation started from zero. Every project was an island.
    33→
    34→The difference isn't just capability. It's the nature of the interaction.
    35→
    36→**Continuity.** Each conversation built on the ones before it. When I mentioned the "agent calling toolboxes" strategy in Week 3, Claude knew what I meant because we'd developed it together in Week 1. When I referenced my blog voice, Claude could apply it because it had analyzed it weeks ago. The shared context grew with every exchange, and that accumulation made each new conversation more productive than the last.
    37→
    38→**Honest feedback.** Claude didn't just agree with me. It pushed back. It identified weaknesses in my arguments. It suggested alternatives I hadn't considered. And it did all of this without the social dynamics that make human feedback complicated — no ego, no office politics, no tiptoeing around seniority. I could share a half-formed idea at midnight and get direct, substantive feedback without worrying about who I was imposing on.
    39→
    40→**Mutual shaping.** My ideas shaped Claude's outputs, obviously — I was the one asking the questions and setting the direction. But Claude's responses shaped my thinking too. The competitive intelligence work didn't just produce reports — it changed how I understood market analysis. The philosopher tools didn't just automate presentation creation — they changed how I thought about the relationship between my values and my work. The influence ran both ways.
    41→
    42→These three qualities — continuity, honest feedback, mutual shaping — are what separate a collaboration from a transaction. ChatGPT was transactional. You put a prompt in, you got an output, you moved on. Claude was collaborative. The relationship had history, direction, and cumulative depth.
    43→
    44→## The Identity Question
    45→
    46→There's a question that surfaced during the Marketing Book Club and that I've been carrying ever since:
    47→
    48→> "When AI starts writing good code, what is the part of your identity that you feel is threatened, if any?"
    49→
    50→David Barnes paused when I asked this. "I really like that articulation. About somewhat of the identity. It's not something I've thought about before."
    51→
    52→I've thought about it a lot. Here's where I've landed.
    53→
    54→If intelligence is cheap — so accessible, so scalable, so available at 2am — then what is *me*? What's the distinctly human contribution when the machine can research faster, write cleaner first drafts, and explore more options in parallel than I ever could?
    55→
    56→The answer, I think, is judgment. Taste. Values. The ability to look at a technically correct output and say "that's not what matters here." The ability to name your tools after philosophers because you believe the things you build should carry meaning beyond their function. The ability to read a competitive analysis and know, from years of customer conversations, which insights are strategically relevant and which are noise.
    57→
    58→Kyle's story haunts me in a productive way. Six months of market analysis replicated in thirty minutes. Seventy percent of the way there. But that remaining thirty percent — the part that requires lived experience, institutional knowledge, strategic intuition — that's not the leftover crumbs. That's the meal. The seventy percent is the plate it's served on.
    59→
    60→This isn't insecurity. It's evolution. The product manager who spent 80% of their time gathering information and 20% thinking now spends 20% gathering and 80% thinking. The bottleneck has shifted. And the new bottleneck — judgment — is the part that's genuinely, stubbornly human.
    61→
    62→## Productive Anthropomorphism
    63→
    64→I gave my personal AI agent a name — Jean Claude — and a French accent. I named my tools after philosophers. I used the word "colleague" to describe an AI system. Some people would call this naive. Others would call it dangerous — projecting humanity onto a machine, setting yourself up for disappointment.
    65→
    66→I call it productive anthropomorphism.
    67→
    68→Here's the thing: the way we relate to our tools shapes how effectively we use them. Nobody anthropomorphizes their spreadsheet, and that's appropriate — a spreadsheet is a grid of cells that does math. But Claude isn't a spreadsheet. Claude is a system you have conversations with, that remembers context, that pushes back on your ideas, that generates creative output you didn't expect. The interface *is* conversational. Treating it as purely mechanical — "input prompt, receive output" — actually makes you worse at using it.
    69→
    70→When I named a tool "ricoeur.cc," I wasn't pretending the tool was Paul Ricoeur. I was encoding a set of values — about interpretation, about the hermeneutic circle, about how we understand things — into the tool's identity. That encoding made the tool more meaningful to me, which made me more thoughtful about how I used it. The anthropomorphism wasn't a bug. It was a feature.
    71→
    72→## The Honest Accounting
    73→
    74→I want to end with honesty, because honesty is what set this relationship apart from the beginning.
    75→
    76→**What Claude is good at:** Breadth. Speed. Consistency. First drafts. Research synthesis. Code generation. Pattern matching across large bodies of information. Tireless iteration. Availability at 2am without complaint.
    77→
    78→**What Claude is not good at:** Insider knowledge. Political nuance. The kind of judgment that comes from sitting in a thousand customer meetings. Knowing when a technically correct answer is strategically wrong. Understanding that sometimes the right response to a colleague's question isn't the most accurate answer but the most diplomatic one. Taste — that ineffable sense of what matters and what doesn't.
    79→
    80→**What surprised me:** The honest feedback. I expected helpfulness. I got intellectual partnership. The difference between Claude pushing back on my strategic memo and ChatGPT telling me it was great — that single contrast shaped everything that followed.
    81→
    82→**What concerns me:** The speed of change. The governance gray areas that nobody has satisfying answers to yet. The pressure this puts on business models — including the one that pays my salary. The cost — "with great powers come great consumptions" — and the daily limits that remind you this technology is still in its infrastructure-constrained adolescence.
    83→
    84→**What I believe:** That this is an AND, not an OR. We can build for humans AND for AI. The tools we build can carry our values AND be technically excellent. The relationship between human and AI can be productive AND honest about its limitations. The future belongs to people who can hold both sides of that AND without collapsing into either extreme.
    85→
    86→## Where This Goes
    87→
    88→I started this journey as a product manager evaluating a tool. I ended it as something I don't have a clean title for. An evangelist? An internal organizer? A person who builds tools named after philosophers and presents them to skeptical colleagues?
    89→
    90→Maybe the simplest description is: a person who found a thinking partner and took the partnership seriously.
    91→
    92→The pilot had a simple requirement — report back on your usage in Q1. What I'm reporting back is this: Claude Code isn't a productivity tool. It's not a coding assistant. It's not a chatbot with better features. It's the first AI system I've used where the word "collaboration" isn't a euphemism for "I type and it generates." The collaboration is real. The influence is mutual. The relationship has history and direction.
    93→
    94→Three months is not a long time. But three months with a thinking partner who's available at 2am, who remembers where you left off, who pushes back when you're wrong, and who helps you build things that encode your values into working software — three months like that can change how you think about what's possible.
    95→
    96→There's BC — Before ChatGPT. There's BCC — Before Claude Code. And now there's whatever comes next.
    97→
    98→I don't know what to call this era yet. But I know the tools I'll build in it will carry the names of philosophers, and the conversations that shape them will be honest ones.
    99→
   100→> "The tools we build shape the lives we lead."
   101→
   102→Happy hacking, and let me know what you build! 👋🏻
   103→

---
title: "Programming with Claude Code"
subtitle: "How I Met Claude Code"
author: "Yann Debray"
---

# Introduction


**How I Met Claude Code**

As I was about to respond to a friend who had asked if there was a GPT to look up flight prices to Boston, it got me to reflect on the journey of AI for the past few years.

GPTs were a thing, and then Claude Code arrived.

This is the story of how I met Claude. But mainly Claude Code.

It was December of 2025. For some reason, this is when the world started to pay attention to Claude Code, and what was happening with coding agents.

The company I worked for (the MathWorks, developers of MATLAB) was starting a pilot to assess the potential for non-developers first. A few product managers were given access, with the responsibility to report back on their usage in the first quarter of 2026. I was one of the lucky ones.

\newpage

# Chapter 1: Before Claude Code (BCC) — The ChatGPT Era

If you are like me, you were probably deep in the ChatGPT era by mid-2025 and didn't even realize a seismic shift was coming.

Everyone I knew was experimenting. Product managers were tip-toeing with the technology, prototyping with AI services or locally with Ollama. Marketing teams were generating content drafts. Developers were starting to evaluate copilots and some were happy enough about the productivity gain it was providing. It's in mid-2025 that the term "vibe coding" entered the vocabulary — this idea that you could describe what you wanted in plain language and watch an AI conjure it into existence. It felt like magic, and like most magic, we didn't fully understand the trick.

I was spending all the free hours I had on AI. I had my ChatGPT subscription, my collection of apps connecting to the OpenAI web services, and a growing sense that something fundamental was changing about how software would be built. But everything I did with AI in those years prior to Claude and agentic involved a lot of copy-pasting, trial and error, and manual integration.

That was about to change. But first, let me tell you about the night I built a website in my kitchen.

## The Vibe Coding Origin Story

Late August 2025. My in-laws were visiting, shortly after GPT-5's release. My father-in-law and I were in the kitchen late at night — the kind of hour where ambitious ideas feel perfectly reasonable.

> **Me**: "What website or web app would you like to develop?"
> **Father-in-law**: *looks around the kitchen, spots a recipe book*
> **Me**: "Ok, let's build a website with French recipes."

Single prompt. Single session. I opened ChatGPT, described what I wanted — a clean, browsable website of French recipes — and watched it generate the HTML, CSS, and JavaScript. The whole thing took maybe an hour, most of which was us discussing which recipes to feature and laughing about the absurdity of what we were doing.

That same night, sometime around 2am, I purchased the domain on Cloudflare. recettesdezoe.com was born — named after my daughter Zoé. We deployed it right there, my father-in-law watching over my shoulder as the site went live.

It was a demo. A party trick. A thing I'd built to show what was possible.

Or so I thought.

## The Surprise Discovery

Four months later — December 2025 — I was poking around my Cloudflare dashboard for an unrelated reason when something caught my eye. The analytics for recettesdezoe.com showed **1,300+ visitors in the past month.**

I stared at the number. Zero edits since launch. Zero marketing. Zero SEO work. Zero social media promotion. Nothing. I had literally forgotten the site existed for weeks at a time, and over a thousand people had found it, visited it, and presumably cooked something from it.

This broke something in my mental model. I had always assumed that building a useful product required sustained effort — iteration, marketing, user research, the whole product management playbook I'd internalized over years at MathWorks. But here was a website I'd built in a kitchen at 2am with an AI, and it was generating more organic traffic than projects I'd spent months planning.

The lessons hit me one at a time over the following days:

- **Vibe coding can produce genuinely useful, discoverable products.** Not just demos — real things that real people find and use.
- **The "demo that becomes real" pattern is more powerful than planned projects.** When you remove the friction of building, you can test ideas at the speed of conversation.
- **Domain purchases at 2am sometimes pay off.** I'm not saying it's a strategy. But I'm not saying it isn't.

## Other Pre-Claude Projects

recettesdezoe.com wasn't my only experiment in the pre-Claude era. That summer and fall, I'd been building a small portfolio of AI-assisted projects, each one pushing a little further into what was possible.

**GhostRunners.app** was probably the most ambitious. I'm a cycling enthusiast, and I wanted something that didn't exist: real-time analytics that could compare my current ride to previous efforts on the same route — essentially racing against my own ghost, like in a video game. I built it using MATLAB Mobile for data capture, MATLAB Online for the analysis, and Python with the Garmin FIT SDK for parsing the ride files. I even recorded YouTube videos documenting the process on my channel @yannspeaksai. It wasn't pretty code, but it worked, and it scratched an itch no existing app could reach.

**OpenAI Codex** came next — first as a VSCode integration where I could vibe-code small HTML and JavaScript apps, and later as a cloud-based agent that could run data analysis hands-off. The progression was revealing: from AI helping me write code, to AI writing code while I watched, to AI writing and running code while I did something else entirely.

**OpenAI Canvas** I tried once and largely abandoned. For single-page web apps, I found myself defaulting to the Claude app — either on mobile or desktop. Even before Claude Code entered the picture, something about Claude's approach to conversation was pulling me in.

## The Pattern I Couldn't See Yet

Looking back, these projects trace an arc I couldn't see at the time. Each one was a single interaction — a prompt, a session, an output. I'd build something, feel the rush of creation, and move on. There was no continuity between sessions, no shared context, no sense that the AI remembered or built upon what we'd done before.

Every conversation started from zero. Every project was an island.

I didn't know it yet, but I was about to discover what happens when AI stops being a tool you pick up and put down, and starts becoming something closer to a collaborator who remembers where you left off.

December 2025 was coming, and with it, an invitation to a pilot program that would change how I thought about all of this.

\newpage

# Chapter 2: December 2025 — First Contact

The email arrived sometime in early December. MathWorks was running a pilot program — Claude Code for non-developers. A handful of product managers would get access, with one responsibility: report back on what they found in Q1 2026. I was one of the lucky ones.

But I didn't start with Claude Code. That would have been too big a leap for someone coming from the ChatGPT world. Instead, I started where most people would: Claude Desktop.

The Desktop app is a very approachable interface for those who come from chat apps like ChatGPT. Same text box, same conversational flow, same satisfying rhythm of typing a question and watching an answer materialize. The first question I asked myself was simple: *What kind of project can I tackle with Claude?*

The answer surprised me. Because it wasn't code.

## The Strategic Awakening

My very first substantive conversation with Claude wasn't about building something — it was about *thinking* about something.

I had been drafting a memo to our Director of Product Marketing. The core thesis was about a strategic tension I'd been mulling over for weeks: the difference between being best-in-class at coding agents and being best-in-class at building tools that coding agents call. For a company like MathWorks, this wasn't an abstract distinction — it was the question that would shape our positioning in the age of agentic AI.

I won't share the full memo here, but I can tell you what happened when I brought it to Claude. I expected what I'd gotten from ChatGPT — a polished summary, maybe some rephrased bullet points, the kind of agreeable helpfulness that makes you feel good but doesn't make you think harder.

Instead, Claude fact-checked my market claims. It confirmed that Cursor had crossed $1B ARR, that Claude Code had reached a $1B run-rate within six months of its launch. Then it stress-tested my arguments — praised the strategic logic but called out gaps. Where were the concrete examples? What about the risks I'd glossed over? The success metrics were too vague. The feedback was direct, specific, and genuinely useful.

I sat with that for a moment. This wasn't what I was used to from AI conversations. ChatGPT would have told me my memo was great and offered to make it longer. Claude told me where it was weak and how to make it stronger.

> *This set the tone for everything that followed: Claude as thinking partner, not just tool.*

I went back and rewrote sections of the memo. The final version was meaningfully better — not because Claude wrote it for me, but because Claude pushed back on the parts that needed pushing. It was the kind of feedback you'd hope to get from a sharp colleague, delivered without ego or politics.

I was also creating a presentation on the impact of agentic AI on the market of scientific and technical computing — something that most companies must have been asking themselves at that point. One diagram I developed described the chasm between the early adopters of agentic AI, evaluating solutions like Claude, and the majority still associating AI with chatbots like ChatGPT. That chasm was real, and I was watching myself cross it in real time.

## The Blog Posts Begin

Within the first week, the strategic conversations spilled over into something more tangible. I had taken over the MATLAB AI Blog on September 1st, and now, with Claude as a collaborator, I found myself drafting posts at a pace I hadn't thought possible.

The list grew quickly:

- MATLAB MCP Core Server — the foundational post about connecting MATLAB to the agentic ecosystem
- Connecting to Hardware — data acquisition and image acquisition devices via MCP
- Deploying a Quant Agent — financial analysis with MATLAB as the computational backbone
- Antigravity + MATLAB — a playful post that turned out to be wicked good
- Giving Local AI Agents the Ability to Use MATLAB with MCP
- Building an Optimization Agent
- Automating the Documentation of MATLAB Analysis
- AI-Assisted Model-Based Design

Eight posts in the pipeline within the first week. This wasn't because I suddenly became a faster writer. The content was still mine — the ideas, the technical depth, the perspective on where MATLAB fit in the agentic AI landscape. What changed was the workflow.

Claude handled the boring parts. The formatting, the structuring, the initial research on competitor approaches, the first-pass drafts of sections where I knew what I wanted to say but didn't want to stare at a blank page. This freed me to concentrate on the two things that actually mattered: **ideation and code.** The thinking and the building. Everything else was scaffolding, and Claude was excellent at scaffolding.

The conference report-out workflow was another revelation. We had gone to the JupyterCon conference in San Diego with my colleague Rohan in early November, and delivering the internal presentation afterward had taken us a long time. I wondered how much of that process could be automated. When PyData came to Boston — its first time there, usually it's in NYC — I tested a streamlined workflow: notes in, presentation out, with Claude handling the transformation in between. The quality was surprisingly good, and the time savings were real.

## Claude Analyzes My Voice

Then something happened that I hadn't expected.

I asked Claude to study my published writing — the blog posts, the technical articles, the presentation scripts — and tell me what patterns it found. I wanted to know if my writing had a recognizable voice, and if so, whether Claude could capture it.

Claude came back with what it called a "skill" — a structured guide that codified my writing voice into its component parts:

**My signature patterns:**

- **Opening hooks**: "If you are like me, you are probably familiar with this situation."
- **Colleague references**: "my dear colleague [Name]"
- **Honest limitations**: "I'm not an expert in Finance!"
- **Closings**: "Happy hacking, and let me know what you build! 👋🏻"
- **Structure**: Problem → Demo → Walkthrough → Resources

I read through the analysis with a strange mix of recognition and surprise. It was accurate — almost uncannily so. These were patterns I'd developed over years of writing, and I'd never consciously catalogued them. Claude had done it in a single conversation.

More importantly, it *worked*. When I asked Claude to draft sections using this skill, the output sounded like me. Not a generic approximation of a tech blogger, but specifically like me — my rhythms, my tendency to acknowledge what I don't know, my habit of opening with a shared experience. It was the difference between a cover band playing the notes and actually capturing the feel.

This was when I started to understand that what was happening went beyond productivity. A tool that makes you faster is useful. A tool that learns your voice and helps you sound more like yourself — that's something else entirely.

## Something Was Shifting

By the end of that first week with Claude, I had:

- Sharpened a strategic memo through genuine intellectual sparring
- Drafted the foundations for eight blog posts
- Streamlined a conference report-out workflow
- Had my writing voice analyzed and codified in a way I could actually use

But the numbers don't capture what was really changing. It was the *quality* of the interaction. Each conversation with Claude built on the ones before it. There was continuity — a shared context that grew with every exchange. When I mentioned the "agent calling toolboxes" strategy, Claude knew what I meant. When I referenced the blog voice, Claude could apply it. When I circled back to an idea from two days ago, I didn't have to re-explain everything from scratch.

The conversations had something I'd never experienced with an AI before: **honest feedback.** Claude didn't just agree with me. It pushed back. It identified weaknesses. It suggested alternatives I hadn't considered. And it did all of this without the social dynamics that make human feedback complicated — no ego, no office politics, no tiptoeing around seniority.

What had started as a productivity experiment — can this tool help me write blog posts faster? — was becoming something I didn't have a name for yet. It wasn't a chatbot. It wasn't an assistant. It was starting to feel like a colleague who happened to be available at 2am and never needed coffee.

I didn't know where this was headed. But I knew that the one-shot, disposable AI interactions of the ChatGPT era were behind me. Whatever came next would be built on continuity, shared context, and the kind of thinking partnership I was only beginning to understand.

Looking back, I'd later distill this first week into a slide I called "Agentic 101" — the foundation layer. Skills, tools, MCP. Claude Desktop as the gateway drug. Python first, then MATLAB. It was the simplest of the four weekly lessons I'd eventually present to colleagues, but it was also the most important. Everything that followed — the philosopher tools, the agent orchestration, the terminal becoming home — grew from this first week of unlearning what I thought AI could do.

The philosopher tools were about to arrive. But that's the next chapter.

\newpage

# Chapter 3: The Philosopher Tools

The philosopher tools were about to arrive. That's how I closed the last chapter, and I meant it literally.

Week 2 brought a shift I hadn't anticipated. Claude wasn't just a thinking partner anymore — it was a building partner. The strategic memos and blog drafts from Week 1 had been intellectually rewarding, but they were still fundamentally about *writing*. Now I found myself doing something different: building software. Real, distributable, reusable software. And the tools that emerged came with names I didn't expect.

## From Skills to Tools

The key insight of Week 2 was deceptively simple: **density — less is more.** Build tools, not just skills.

Let me explain. In Week 1, Claude had helped me create "skills" — structured prompts that captured my writing voice, my presentation style, my analytical frameworks. They were useful, but they were also fragile. A skill lives in a conversation. You invoke it, it shapes the output, and then it vanishes when the session ends. It's a one-shot thing — powerful in the moment, but not reusable in any systematic way.

A CLI tool is different. A CLI tool is a piece of software that lives on your machine, that agents can call deterministically, that follows a workflow you've defined and tested. It doesn't depend on the quality of your prompt or the mood of the conversation. It just runs.

This distinction — between a prompt that works once and a tool that works every time — turned out to be the difference between playing with AI and actually building with it. And once I understood that, the tools started coming fast.

## The Army of Philosophers

If you are like me, you probably name your projects something boring and descriptive — `slide-generator`, `media-converter`, `search-tool`. Functional. Forgettable. I'd done this my whole career.

But something about building with Claude changed the naming instinct. Maybe it was the late-night energy. Maybe it was the reading I'd been doing all year. Maybe it was the feeling that these tools deserved names that carried meaning beyond their function. Whatever the reason, I started naming my tools after philosophers.

And once I started, I couldn't stop.

**montaigne.cc** was the first. A media processing toolkit for presentation localization — transcription, translation, audio and video processing, all powered by Google Gemini AI. Montaigne's *Essais* are the original explorations — a mind wandering across languages, cultures, and ideas, interpreting everything it encounters through the lens of personal experience. The tool does the same thing, in its own way. It takes content in one language and interprets it into another. It listens to audio and renders it as text. It crosses boundaries between media the way Montaigne crossed boundaries between disciplines.

**nietzsche.cc** came next. A presentation generator — a CLI where the `power` command generates slide decks. "The will to present." I know, I know. But hear me out. Nietzsche's Übermensch is about self-overcoming, about transcending the tedious and mundane to reach something higher. If you've ever spent three hours manually formatting a PowerPoint deck when the ideas were already clear in your head, you understand the appeal. The tool was my Übermensch moment — a way to transcend the drudgery and focus on what mattered: the ideas themselves.

There's another Nietzschean concept that fits even better: eternal recurrence. The idea that you should live as though you'd have to repeat every action for eternity. In automation, this is exactly what you want — a workflow so clean, so reliable, that running it again and again produces consistent results. Create, iterate, reuse. Eternal recurrence, but for slide decks.

**ricoeur.cc** was the one I was most proud of conceptually. A CLI search tool for conversation history — a way to dig through past dialogues and extract meaning from them. Paul Ricoeur's hermeneutic circle is the idea that you understand the parts through the whole, and the whole through the parts. Part, whole, part, whole — an endless loop of interpretation and reinterpretation. That's exactly what searching through conversation history feels like. You find a fragment, it reframes your understanding of the entire exchange, and that new understanding sends you looking for different fragments. The tool and the philosophy were almost uncomfortably aligned.

I even asked Claude to evaluate the naming. The feedback was characteristically direct: ricoeur.cc edged ahead on conceptual coherence — the mapping between the tool's purpose and the philosopher's theme was the tightest. But nietzsche.cc was more polished in UX, more immediately usable. Claude recommended building a shared landing page to tell the story of why these tools existed as a family. Not just a collection of utilities, but a suite with a philosophical backbone.

An army of philosophers. That's what I started calling them.

## Why Philosophers?

The naming wasn't random. It grew out of a reading list that had been building all year — a personal arc through books that, looking back, were preparing me for exactly this moment.

It started with Kafka's *The Trial*. The individual against the system, navigating opaque bureaucracies that operate by rules no one fully understands. If you've ever tried to introduce AI tooling into a large organization, you know this story intimately. The committees, the approval processes, the security reviews — Kafka wrote the manual on corporate AI adoption before AI existed.

Then Camus's *The Stranger*. Absurdism. The idea that life doesn't owe you meaning, so you'd better create your own. There's something deeply Camusian about vibe coding — this acceptance that the process is absurd, that you're building software by describing it in plain language to a machine, and that the appropriate response is not to demand it make sense but to embrace the creative freedom it offers.

Nietzsche's *Thus Spoke Zarathustra* was the big one. Will to power, self-overcoming, the three metamorphoses. More on that in a moment.

Plato's *Apology* — Socrates on trial, defending the examined life. Intellectual humility as a practice. The idea that wisdom begins with knowing what you don't know. Every time I typed "I'm not an expert in Finance!" into a blog post, I was channeling Socrates without realizing it.

Irvin Yalom's *The Schopenhauer Cure* bridged philosophy and practice — the idea that thinking about the world and doing something in the world aren't separate activities. Philosophy as therapy. Code as philosophy.

Then the modern voices: Harari's *Nexus*, framing AI as the next chapter in the human information story. Mollick's *Co-Intelligence*, providing the practical frameworks for working alongside AI systems.

> "The existentialists ask 'how should I live authentically?' while Mollick and Harari ask 'how should we live with AI?' These questions are not separate. The tools we build shape the lives we lead."

That synthesis emerged during a late-night conversation with Claude, and it became the philosophical thesis underneath everything I was building. The tools weren't just utilities. They were answers — my answers — to questions that philosophers had been asking for centuries.

## The Zarathustra Project

The philosopher tools and the reading list converged most dramatically in the Zarathustra project. I built a full voiceover script for a fifteen-slide presentation on *Thus Spoke Zarathustra*, and I built it using nietzsche.cc itself. The tool and its namesake, working together.

The presentation traced the arc of Nietzsche's masterwork: the prophet Zarathustra descending from his mountain after ten years of solitude. The Übermensch — not a superhero, but a concept of human self-overcoming. The three metamorphoses: the Camel who bears the weight of tradition, the Lion who rebels and creates freedom, the Child who plays and creates new values. The Last Man — Nietzsche's warning about a humanity so comfortable it stops striving. The Will to Power as creative drive, not domination. Eternal Recurrence as the ultimate test of how you choose to live.

Twelve to fifteen minutes of narrated content, generated from a tool I'd built with an AI, about a philosopher whose ideas had inspired the tool's name. If Nietzsche had known about recursion, he would have appreciated the structure.

## The Philosopher Rap Battle

And then — because creative momentum has a logic of its own — the rap battle happened.

Once you've named your tools after philosophers and built a presentation about Zarathustra, the next step is obviously an educational web app where philosophers debate each other in rap format. Obviously.

The concept was simple: pick two philosophers, set them against each other on a topic, and let the arguments fly — but in verse. Nietzsche vs. Schopenhauer was the flagship matchup. Master and student. Pessimism vs. affirmation. "The world is suffering" vs. "Embrace it all and will it again."

It was playful. It was absurd. It was exactly the kind of project that happens when you're building with a collaborator who doesn't get tired, doesn't judge your ideas, and responds to "What if we made philosophers rap-battle each other?" with genuine creative energy rather than a raised eyebrow.

This is something I didn't expect about building with Claude: the creative compound effect. Each project made the next one easier to imagine. The philosopher naming led to the reading list connections, which led to the Zarathustra presentation, which led to the rap battle. One idea feeding the next, accelerating, branching. Not because Claude was generating ideas autonomously, but because having a tireless collaborator meant I never had to stop and wonder "Is this too weird? Is this worth the time?" The answer was always: let's find out.

## The Developer Workflow

Behind the philosophical naming and the creative experiments, Week 2 was also when I became a real developer. Or at least, a closer approximation of one.

Python for CLI is fire. I'd known Python before, but building command-line tools with it — using `fire` for argument parsing, structuring projects as proper packages — felt different. These weren't scripts. They were software.

`uv` is life. The Python package manager that made everything faster — environment creation, dependency resolution, the whole toolchain. Once you've used `uv`, going back to `pip` feels like going back to dial-up.

Publishing to PyPI was the moment it felt real. My tools weren't just local scripts anymore. Anyone could `pip install` them. They existed in the world, with version numbers and documentation and download counts. Let the game begin.

GitHub Issues became my requirements management system. Instead of keeping a mental list of features to add, I'd have Claude generate issues — structured, labeled, prioritized. Then I'd work through them methodically. Requirements in, code out, tests validating, CI/CD deploying.

Speaking of tests: automated testing via GitHub Actions meant that every push was validated. And when a test failed, I could ask the GitHub MCP server to retrieve the trace. No more copy-pasting error logs from one window to another. The feedback loop tightened until it was nearly instantaneous.

I even recorded a YouTube demo — the whole workflow from concept to published package, captured on my @yannspeaksai channel. Because if you build an army of philosophers and don't document it, did it really happen?

## Tools as Self-Portraits

Here's what I think Chapter 3 is really about.

Naming your tools after philosophers isn't vanity — it's a way of encoding your values into the things you build. Every developer names their projects. Most names are functional, forgettable, interchangeable. But when you name a tool after Ricoeur, you're making a claim about what the tool means, not just what it does. You're saying: this search tool embodies a philosophy of interpretation. This presentation generator carries a vision of self-overcoming. This media toolkit is an act of translation in the deepest sense.

The philosopher tools were the first sign that my collaboration with Claude was producing something more personal than productivity gains. The blog posts from Week 1 were useful. The strategic memos were valuable. But the tools — the tools were self-portraits in code. They reflected not just what I could build, but how I thought about the world.

Week 2 had taught me that the line between philosophy and programming was thinner than I'd imagined. The existentialists and the engineers were asking the same question: *What should I build, and why does it matter?*

What I didn't know yet was that these weekly discoveries — the density principle, the philosopher army, the shift from skills to tools — would become the backbone of an internal evangelism campaign. Slides I'd present to three different audiences over the next six weeks, each time with a deeper understanding of what "build tools, not skills" really meant. But that story belongs to a later chapter.

I didn't have a complete answer yet. But I had an army of philosophers, a PyPI account, and a growing suspicion that the most interesting work was still ahead.

The competitive intelligence reports were about to begin. Claude was about to become my research partner. But that's the next chapter.

\newpage

# Chapter 4: The Competitive Intelligence Machine

The philosopher tools had given me a taste of building. But building wasn't the only thing that happened in those early weeks. Alongside the CLI tools and the PyPI packages, a completely different body of work was taking shape — one that had nothing to do with code and everything to do with thinking.

Claude was becoming my research partner. And the research was getting serious.

## The Pattern

If you are like me — a product manager at a technology company — you've spent a meaningful fraction of your career doing competitive intelligence. Reading analyst reports. Scanning press releases. Sitting in meetings where someone presents a slide that says "Market Size: $X Billion" and everyone nods as if the number is more than an educated guess.

The traditional workflow is slow and fragmented. You gather information from a dozen sources, synthesize it in your head, write it up in a memo, circulate it for feedback, revise, and eventually arrive at something useful — weeks after you started. By then, the market has moved.

With Claude, a new pattern emerged. It was simple, repeatable, and remarkably effective:

1. **I bring the question.** A company, a technology, a market segment. Something I need to understand deeply for strategic planning.
2. **Claude researches broadly.** Not just surface-level facts — deep dives into market dynamics, competitive positioning, technology trends, customer segments.
3. **Together, we extract implications.** This is the part that matters. Not "what is the market size?" but "what does this mean for us? Where are the opportunities? Where are the threats?"

This wasn't just fact-gathering. It was thinking partnership applied to strategy. And the volume of what we produced shocked me.

## The PIDO Deep Dive

The first major competitive intelligence effort was about a market I'd been tracking: Process Integration and Design Optimization — PIDO, for those who live in the engineering software world.

In a traditional workflow, building a comprehensive market analysis of PIDO would take me the better part of a week. I'd need to identify the key players, understand their positioning, map the competitive landscape, estimate market sizes from fragmented data sources, and connect it all to MathWorks' strategic positioning.

With Claude, I typed a single prompt. What came back was a comprehensive analysis: a $200–220M USD market in 2024, growing at 10–12% CAGR. But the numbers were the easy part. What made it valuable was the strategic layer — the implications for MathWorks' positioning, the gaps in our current approach, the segments where we were strong and the ones where we were vulnerable.

I sat with the output for a while, cross-checking claims, testing the logic. Some of the analysis I'd adjust. Some of the market estimates I'd refine with internal data Claude didn't have access to. But the foundation was solid. The synthesis was genuinely useful. And it had taken an hour instead of a week.

This is the part that most people misunderstand about AI-assisted research. They imagine you press a button and receive a report. That's not how it works — or at least, that's not how it works well. What actually happens is a dialogue. I'd push back on Claude's framing. Claude would defend it or adjust. I'd bring internal context — things I knew about our product roadmap, our customer conversations, our engineering investments — that Claude couldn't access. Claude would integrate that context and sharpen the analysis.

The output was neither mine nor Claude's. It was ours.

## The Neuroscience Toolbox Analysis

The second example was more technical and, in some ways, more revealing. Industry marketing needed a competitive analysis of the neuroscience research tools ecosystem — specifically, how MATLAB-based tools compared to the rising Python alternatives.

Claude dove into the landscape: FieldTrip, EEGLAB, MNE-Python, and the constellation of smaller tools orbiting them. The research surfaced something I hadn't fully appreciated. EEGLAB was leading in academic citations at 21,700+. But MNE-Python was showing the fastest growth trajectory — 3,200 GitHub stars, 416 contributors, and the kind of community momentum that's hard to reverse.

The strategic insight was pointed: a clear bifurcation was emerging between MATLAB-based tools (historically dominant, deeply embedded in research workflows) and Python-based alternatives (rapidly growing, zero licensing costs, open-source community energy). This wasn't just a technical comparison. It was a strategic signal about where the neuroscience research community was heading.

This is the kind of analysis that, delivered in a deck to industry marketing, changes how people think about their market. And it emerged from a conversation, not a consulting engagement.

## MATLAB Community Intelligence

The third stream of research was the most internally focused. Colleagues had started coming to me with requests.

"Can you tell me what users say about the Live Editor?" — that was Rohan.

"Can you tell me if a feature like Live Editor is useful and loved?" — that was Johanna.

These weren't idle questions. They were the kind of product intelligence that normally requires weeks of manual analysis — combing through MATLAB Answers, reading forum threads, categorizing sentiment, identifying patterns. The kind of work that everyone agrees is important and nobody has time to do.

Claude changed the equation. By analyzing MATLAB community data — forum posts, questions, answers, the whole corpus of user-generated content — we could extract product intelligence at a pace that made it practical. Not perfect. Not a replacement for proper user research. But a first pass that was fast enough to be useful and comprehensive enough to be informative.

The fact that colleagues were bringing me their questions told me something important. The competitive intelligence work wasn't just useful to me — it was becoming useful to the people around me. The research partner I'd found in Claude was turning into something closer to a research service.

## What Claude Brought, What I Brought

Here's the honest accounting of who contributed what.

**Claude brought breadth.** The ability to survey a landscape quickly, to pull together information from diverse sources, to identify patterns across large bodies of data. When I asked about the PIDO market, Claude could synthesize information from analyst reports, company announcements, technology trends, and market dynamics — the kind of breadth that would take me days to assemble manually.

**I brought judgment.** The ability to say "that market estimate feels high" or "you're missing the political dimension of this competitive dynamic" or "that's technically correct but strategically irrelevant." I knew which of our products competed where. I knew which customer segments we were winning and which we were losing. I knew the internal politics — which initiatives had executive sponsorship and which were struggling for resources.

**Together, we produced synthesis.** Neither of us could have done it alone. Claude without my judgment would produce technically accurate but strategically naive reports. My judgment without Claude's breadth would produce insightful but narrow analyses that took too long to assemble.

This is the pattern that Ethan Mollick describes in *Co-Intelligence* — the idea that human-AI collaboration isn't about automation but about augmentation. You don't replace the human. You amplify them. And you don't use AI as a search engine. You use it as a thinking partner.

## The Sleepless Nights

There's a moment that Mollick writes about — the first time AI does something that makes you lie awake at night. Not in fear, exactly, but in a disorienting sense that the ground has shifted.

My colleague Kyle Hakes had his moment during the marketing book club I organized around *Co-Intelligence*. He told the group that someone had replicated six months of his market analysis work with a single deep research prompt — in thirty minutes. "70% of the way there," he said. His reaction: "I was like, well, I'm out of a job."

He was joking. Mostly. But the feeling was real. When you've spent years developing expertise in market analysis and competitive intelligence, and then you watch an AI produce something that's 70% as good in 0.1% of the time, the natural response is existential unease.

I felt it too. The competitive intelligence work I was doing with Claude wasn't just faster than my old workflow — it was a different thing entirely. The old workflow was labor-intensive and slow, which meant I was selective about what I researched. I only deep-dived into markets that justified the time investment. With Claude, the marginal cost of research dropped close to zero. I could explore markets I'd never have bothered with before, just because the friction was gone.

This changes the job. Not eliminates it — changes it. The product manager who spends 80% of their time gathering information and 20% thinking about it now spends 20% gathering and 80% thinking. The bottleneck shifts from research to judgment. And judgment, it turns out, is the part that's hard to automate.

## The Quality Question

I want to be honest about something. Not everything Claude produced was gold.

Some market estimates were off — based on outdated data or extrapolations that didn't account for recent disruptions. Some competitive analyses missed nuances that only someone embedded in the industry would catch. Some strategic recommendations were plausible but generic — the kind of thing any competent consultant would suggest.

The 70% number Kyle mentioned is about right. On a first pass, Claude gets you 70% of the way to a solid competitive intelligence report. The remaining 30% — the part that requires insider knowledge, strategic nuance, and the kind of judgment that comes from years of sitting in customer meetings — that's still you.

But here's the thing: 70% in thirty minutes versus 100% in two weeks is not a close comparison. The first pass gives you a foundation to build on, a framework to critique, a starting point that's dramatically better than a blank page. And the iteration from 70% to 95% — adding your judgment, your context, your strategic perspective — takes hours, not weeks.

The competitive intelligence machine wasn't perfect. But it was fast, it was comprehensive, and it freed me to do the part of my job that actually requires a human: deciding what the information means.

## What This Changed

By the end of those early weeks, I had produced competitive intelligence reports covering markets I'd never have had time to analyze before. Each one followed the same pattern — Claude's breadth plus my judgment, iterated through conversation until we arrived at something genuinely useful.

But the bigger change was subtler. My relationship to information itself had shifted. I'd gone from being a person who carefully selected which questions to research (because research was expensive in time) to a person who could research anything, anytime, and focus my energy on the higher-order question: *So what?*

The "so what" is where product managers earn their keep. And Claude had cleared the path to get there faster.

The competitive intelligence work was happening alongside the philosopher tools, alongside the blog posts, alongside the strategic memos. All of it feeding into a growing body of work that would need a unifying infrastructure. That infrastructure had a name — the Model Context Protocol — and it was about to change everything. But that's the next chapter.

\newpage

# Chapter 5: The MCP Revolution

Everything I'd built so far — the philosopher tools, the competitive intelligence reports, the blog posts, the strategic memos — had one thing in common. They were separate. Each tool lived in its own world. Each workflow started from scratch. The blog posts didn't know about the CLI tools. The research didn't feed back into the presentations. The competitive intelligence existed in documents, not in systems.

What I needed was infrastructure. A way to connect all of these pieces so that agents could use them together, not just one at a time. That infrastructure had a name, and it was about to become the central theme of everything I did.

The Model Context Protocol. MCP.

## USB-C for AI

If you are like me, you remember the era of proprietary chargers. Every device had its own connector — a different shape, a different voltage, a different cable cluttering your desk drawer. Then USB-C arrived and suddenly everything plugged into everything. One standard. Universal compatibility. The end of cable chaos.

MCP is USB-C for AI.

That's the metaphor that stuck. Not because I coined it — it was already circulating in the developer community — but because it captured exactly what was happening. Before MCP, connecting an AI agent to an external tool meant building a custom integration for every combination. Claude needs to use MATLAB? Build a bespoke bridge. Copilot needs to access your database? Write another one. Every agent-tool pair required its own plumbing.

MCP changed the equation. It defined a standard protocol — a universal connector — that any AI agent could use to talk to any tool. Build one MCP server for your tool, and suddenly every MCP-compatible agent can use it. Claude Code, GitHub Copilot, Cursor, Windsurf — all of them, through the same interface.

For someone working at MathWorks, this wasn't an abstract standard. It was a strategic imperative.

## The MATLAB MCP Core Server

The MATLAB MCP Core Server became the centerpiece of my work. I wrote about it, created tutorials for it, built onboarding materials around it, and positioned it as the bridge between MATLAB and the agentic AI ecosystem.

The server itself was elegant in its simplicity. Five tools:

1. **`detect_matlab_toolboxes`** — Reports which toolboxes are installed. Simple, but essential. An agent needs to know what capabilities are available before it can plan how to solve a problem.
2. **`check_matlab_code`** — Runs MATLAB's built-in `checkcode` function for style and correctness analysis. The agent can lint your code before running it.
3. **`evaluate_matlab_code`** — Executes MATLAB code and returns the results. This is the core — the ability for an agent to actually *use* MATLAB, not just talk about it.
4. **`start_matlab` / `quit_matlab`** — Session management. The agent can spin up a MATLAB instance and tear it down when it's done.

Five tools. That's it. But those five tools turned MATLAB from a standalone application into something that any AI agent could call programmatically. The strategic implications were enormous.

I remember the moment it clicked for me — watching Claude Code call `evaluate_matlab_code`, run a computation, inspect the output, decide it needed to adjust a parameter, and call it again. No human in the loop. The agent was *using* MATLAB the way a developer would. Not generating MATLAB code and hoping you'd copy-paste it (the ChatGPT pattern), but actually running it, reading the results, and iterating.

This is the difference between AI that generates code and AI that uses tools. MCP made the latter possible.

## The Integration Play

Here's the positioning insight that emerged, and it was one of the most important strategic ideas I contributed during the pilot:

> This is an **integration play**, not putting agentic front and center. MATLAB "integrates" with coding agents like Claude Code and GitHub Copilot. We're showing MATLAB users that they're not left behind.

The temptation for any established software company watching the agentic AI wave is to panic and try to build their own AI agent from scratch. "We need our own copilot! Our own agentic platform!" That's the instinct, and it's usually wrong.

The smarter play — the one I kept advocating — was integration. MATLAB doesn't need to be an AI agent. MATLAB needs to be the tool that AI agents reach for when they need to do serious computation. The MCP server was the bridge that made this possible.

I developed this into a pocket guide strategy aimed at two audiences:

**Primary audience:** MATLAB users evaluating coding agents. The people asking, "Does MATLAB work with these new coding agents I keep hearing about?" These are the users who've seen colleagues using Copilot or Claude Code and are wondering if they're being left behind. The answer: no. MATLAB works with all of them, through MCP.

**Secondary audience:** MATLAB users who already use GitHub Copilot and want to extend it. They've got the coding agent — now they want it to actually run MATLAB, not just generate MATLAB code. The MCP server closes that gap.

> "If you can think of it as an AND — if we can do for humans AND for AI, then maybe we have something here."

That became my closing line in more than one presentation. MathWorks serving both humans and AI agents. Not zero-sum. An AND.

## The MCP Server Experiments

The MATLAB MCP Core Server was just the beginning. Once you understand the MCP pattern, you start seeing servers everywhere.

**ThingSpeak** — IoT data access via MCP. ThingSpeak is MathWorks' platform for IoT data storage and analysis. Wrapping it in an MCP server meant that any AI agent could query IoT sensor data, analyze trends, and trigger actions — all through the same standard protocol.

**Google Spreadsheet** — Private data access. This one actually got deployed. My colleague and I built an MCP server that let agents read from and write to Google Sheets — a bridge between the structured world of spreadsheets and the conversational world of AI agents. It sounds mundane, but think about how many business workflows live in spreadsheets. Giving agents access to that data, through a standard interface, unlocked an enormous amount of practical utility.

**MATLAB Remote Service** — For remote execution in the cloud or on a local server. This was the ambitious one. Instead of requiring a local MATLAB installation, the remote server let agents call MATLAB running somewhere else — in the cloud, on a compute cluster, on a colleague's machine. The MCP server abstracted away the location, so the agent didn't care whether MATLAB was local or remote.

Each server was a small project, but together they painted a picture: MCP wasn't just a way to connect one tool to one agent. It was the foundation for an ecosystem. Every server you build multiplies the capabilities of every agent that connects to it.

## The Blog Post Machine

The blog posts I'd started drafting in Week 1 had a recurring character: MCP. Almost every post I published on the MATLAB AI Blog connected back to the protocol:

- **MATLAB MCP Core Server** — the foundational post, announcing the server and explaining the architecture
- **Connecting to Hardware** — data acquisition and image acquisition devices via MCP. The physical world, accessible to agents
- **Deploying a Quant Agent** — financial analysis with MATLAB as the computational backbone, connected through MCP
- **Giving Local AI Agents the Ability to Use MATLAB** — the on-ramp for users running local models
- **Building an Optimization Agent** — engineering optimization workflows, orchestrated through MCP
- **Automating the Documentation of MATLAB Analysis** — the full pipeline from analysis to documentation, with MCP as the connective tissue

Six posts, all variations on the same theme: MATLAB + MCP = MATLAB in the agentic era. The blog posts weren't just content marketing. They were proof of concept. Each one demonstrated a different use case, a different audience, a different reason why the MCP integration mattered.

## The Onboarding Campaign

As I went deeper into the MCP ecosystem, I realized something: the technology was only half the challenge. The other half was getting people to use it.

So I built an onboarding package. A complete Claude Code 101 for MathWorkers: installation guide, CLAUDE.md files for common project types, slash commands for repeatable workflows, MCP server setup instructions, Git workflow best practices, tips and tricks I'd accumulated over weeks of daily use.

This is where the Claude Code features I'd been exploring became teachable material:

**`/init`** — Bootstrap a project with CLAUDE.md and initial configuration. The command that turns a blank directory into a project that Claude understands. I showed colleagues how one command could set up the context, conventions, and instructions that would make every subsequent conversation more productive.

**`/context`** — Load context into a session. The ability to bring Claude up to speed on a project without re-explaining everything from scratch. For someone who'd experienced the "every session starts from zero" frustration of the ChatGPT era, this was transformative.

**`/commands`** — Custom slash commands for repeatable workflows. The bridge between "I do this manually every time" and "I type one command and it happens." I built commands for blog post drafting, for presentation generation, for competitive intelligence — all the workflows I'd been doing manually, now formalized and shareable.

The distinction between commands and skills became one of my favorite things to explain:

| Aspect | Commands | Skills |
|--------|----------|--------|
| Invocation | Manual: /command | Automatic: Claude decides |
| Structure | Single .md file | Directory with SKILL.md |
| Location | .claude/commands/ | .claude/skills/ |
| Discovery | User must type command | Claude matches description |
| Best for | Quick templates | Complex workflows |

Commands are things you ask for. Skills are things Claude recognizes it needs. The difference is subtle but profound — it's the difference between a tool you pick up and a capability that activates itself when the situation calls for it.

## Agents Calling Agents

And then things got weird. In a good way.

The MCP thesis was "agents calling tools" — an AI agent reaching out to external capabilities through a standard protocol. But somewhere around Week 3, a more advanced pattern emerged: **agents calling agents.**

Not just Claude using MATLAB. Claude orchestrating Codex and Gemini CLI. Multiple AI agents, working in parallel, each assigned a different piece of a larger task. Claude as the conductor, the other agents as the orchestra.

The inspiration came from NetworkChuck — the idea of sending multiple agents down different research paths and synthesizing the results. Deep research, but parallelized. Instead of Claude doing everything itself, it could delegate: "You go research the market size. You go analyze the technical architecture. You go find the competitive landscape." Then it would synthesize the results into something more comprehensive than any single agent could produce.

Gemini CLI's headless mode made this practical. OpenAI's Codex CLI offered another execution path. Even GitHub Copilot could be subcontracted through the `/github:AssignCodingAgent` MCP command — Claude assigning implementation work to Copilot through GitHub Issues.

This escalation from tool-use to agent-orchestration felt like watching a gear shift. The MCP server gave agents hands. Agents calling agents gave them a team. The metaphor wasn't a single worker with better tools anymore — it was a manager coordinating a team of specialists, each with their own strengths.

I showed this slide to the industry marketing team during my Week 3 presentation. I could see the moment it clicked for some of them — and the moment it overwhelmed others. The gap between "I use ChatGPT for emails" and "I orchestrate multiple AI agents in parallel" was wider than anyone had expected.

## What MCP Actually Changed

Here's what I think the MCP revolution is really about.

Before MCP, every AI integration was a one-off. You'd connect one agent to one tool, and the integration was fragile, proprietary, and hard to maintain. The cost of connecting anything to anything was high, so people connected very little.

After MCP, connection became cheap. Build one server, and every agent can use it. Build one client, and every server is accessible. The network effects kick in: every new server makes every agent more capable, and every new agent makes every server more valuable.

For MathWorks, this meant MATLAB could join the agentic ecosystem without rebuilding itself. The MCP server was the bridge — lightweight, standards-based, and infinitely extensible. For me personally, it meant the philosopher tools, the competitive intelligence workflows, the blog post pipelines, and the MATLAB integrations could all talk to each other through the same protocol.

The infrastructure I'd been looking for — the thing that would connect all the separate pieces of work into a coherent system — had been there all along, waiting for me to understand it.

And understanding it changed everything that came after. The side projects were about to get ambitious. But that's the next chapter.

\newpage

# Chapter 6: The Side Projects That Kept Growing

If you are like me, you probably have a graveyard of side projects. Half-finished repos, abandoned domain names, ideas that burned bright for an evening and cooled by morning. The normal lifecycle of a side project is: excitement, prototype, neglect, guilt, quiet deletion. Maybe you keep them around in a private GitHub folder as monuments to good intentions. I certainly did.

Something different happened this time.

It was early January, a few weeks into the new year. I was in the terminal one evening, working on something for the recipe site — I don't even remember what, probably a content pipeline — when I realized I had three different projects open in three different tabs, and all of them were *active*. Not in the "I should really get back to that" sense. Active as in: I'd made progress on each one in the past week, and each one had a clear next step waiting for me. I stopped typing and tried to remember the last time that had happened with a side project. I couldn't.

Over the previous chapters, I'd built an infrastructure I hadn't planned. The philosopher tools gave me reusable CLIs and a workflow for turning ideas into distributable software. The competitive intelligence work with Claude taught me how to research markets rigorously instead of guessing. The MCP revolution connected my tools to each other and to external services in ways that multiplied what a single person could do. None of this had been designed as a system. It was more like — each piece created the conditions for the next piece to exist.

And then the side projects stopped being "side." They started growing on their own.

Not all of them. Not into businesses. But the pattern was unmistakable: projects that would normally have died after one session were instead evolving, branching, connecting to each other. The graveyard wasn't filling up anymore. Things were staying alive.

This chapter is about three of those projects — a recipe website that almost became a business, a YouTube channel that became a laboratory, and a music recommendation app that became a philosophical statement. Each one started as an island. Each one turned into something I didn't expect.

## recettesdezoe.com — From Demo to Business?

You already know the origin story from Chapter 1. Late-night kitchen session, father-in-law watching over my shoulder, a recipe website conjured from a single ChatGPT prompt, domain purchased at 2am. Then the surprise: 1,300 organic visitors four months later, with zero marketing, zero updates, zero effort.

What you don't know is what happened next.

The traffic numbers broke something in my mental model, I said that already. But breaking a mental model is one thing. Deciding what to build on the rubble is another. Because once I had evidence that people were actually visiting this site — cooking from it, maybe — I had to ask a question I hadn't planned on asking: *Is this a product?*

I did what any self-respecting product manager would do with an existential question about a 2am side project. I reached for a framework.

### The Mom Test

Rob Fitzpatrick's *The Mom Test* is the gold standard for product validation. The core idea is deceptively simple: stop asking people if your idea is good (they'll lie to be polite) and start asking about their actual behavior. Don't say "Would you use a recipe website?" Say "Tell me about the last time you looked for a recipe online. What happened?"

I couldn't interview the 1,300 visitors — I didn't know who they were. But I could do the next best thing: study the market they were already choosing from.

### The Competitive Landscape

This is where the Chapter 4 pattern kicked in. I'd spent weeks training Claude to be a competitive intelligence partner — structured research, market sizing, strategic analysis. Now I turned that same lens on the French recipe market. And the landscape was sobering.

**Marmiton** — the absolute behemoth. 10 million monthly visitors, user-generated content, a brand so embedded in French cooking culture that "je vais regarder sur Marmiton" is practically a reflex. **750g** (owned by Webedia) — professional content, video-forward, with the media infrastructure to dominate on search and social. **Cuisine AZ** — another giant, part of the same media group as 750g, covering every conceivable recipe with industrial-scale content production.

Then there was recettesdezoe.com. A site built in a kitchen. By a product manager. At 2am.

The analysis was honest, and Claude didn't sugarcoat it: the French recipe market was a red ocean. The incumbents had decades of SEO authority, millions of indexed pages, and content pipelines that no solo operator could match on volume. The 1,300 visitors were real — probably finding the site through long-tail keywords that the giants hadn't bothered to optimize for. But scaling against Marmiton was like opening a corner bakery and hoping to outcompete an industrial bread factory.

The insight wasn't "give up." It was "compete differently." The big players wrote recipes like food blogs — SEO-optimized, template-driven, drowning in ads and pop-ups. recettesdezoe.com had something they didn't: a voice.

### The Voice Test

This was the moment where Chapter 2's voice analysis came back in a new form. Remember the blogging workflow I'd developed with Claude — the careful attention to preserving an authentic writing voice, the resistance to letting AI flatten everything into generic corporate prose? The same principle applied here, but the domain was entirely different.

I defined a quality test for any recipe the site would publish:

> "Could this have been written by someone sharing their grandmother's recipe over coffee? If it sounds like a food blog template or a restaurant menu, it's wrong."

That was the moat. Not SEO. Not scale. Voice. The feeling that a real person had cooked this dish, tasted it, adjusted the seasoning, and wanted to tell you about it the way they'd tell a friend.

### Content Scaling with Zoé

If voice was the moat, I still needed a way to produce content without spending every evening writing recipes by hand. This is where the Chapter 3 pattern kicked in again: build tools, not just skills.

I designed a CLI tool for recipe generation. I called it "Zoé" — the same name as my daughter, the same name that was already in the domain. If the philosopher tools got names that carried conceptual meaning, this one carried something more personal: a family name, a familial tradition. Where montaigne.cc and nietzsche.cc encoded philosophical values, Zoé encoded the domestic warmth that the whole project was supposed to embody.

The tool was built with the Copilot SDK and followed a structured workflow: research a recipe category, generate drafts that matched the established voice, validate against the Voice Test, and produce Markdown files ready for the site. Claude was the engine, but the CLI was the guardrail — deterministic, repeatable, consistent. A skill would have drifted with every conversation. A tool kept the voice stable.

Did recettesdezoe.com become a business? No. Not yet, maybe not ever. But it stopped being a demo. It had a competitive analysis, a content strategy, a voice standard, and a production tool named after my daughter. The 2am party trick had become something I could pick up, work on for an evening, put down, and come back to a week later without starting from zero.

That's what "kept growing" means. Not exponential growth. Not viral success. Just — alive.

## The YouTube Channel That Became a Lab

@yannspeaksai started as a way to document my projects. Record what I'm building, explain the process, put it out there. The kind of thing a lot of technical people do — part portfolio, part accountability mechanism, part excuse to learn video editing.

Seven videos in 2025. Five different sports. Five completely different technology stacks. And without planning it, the channel became less of a content outlet and more of a laboratory — each video an experiment in a different domain, with the only constant being Claude as a collaborator.

### Tennis — Predicting the French Open

The first sports video was about prediction. I built an ELO rating system enhanced with machine learning to forecast French Open match outcomes. The model achieved 75% accuracy, which sounds impressive until you realize that tennis prediction is a well-studied problem and the best models in the literature hover around the same range. The point wasn't to beat the state of the art. The point was that a product manager with no background in sports statistics could build a credible prediction system in a few evenings.

The stack was MATLAB for the analysis and Python for the data pipeline. Claude handled the integration between the two — something that would have taken me days of Stack Overflow diving in the Before Claude Code era.

### NBA — Dashboards and Automation

The basketball project pushed into web development territory. Season ranking animations, a React dashboard showing team statistics, and — the part I was proudest of — a full GitHub Actions CI/CD pipeline that automatically updated the data and rebuilt the visualizations.

This was the "build tools, not skills" philosophy applied to content creation. I didn't just make a dashboard. I built a system that kept the dashboard alive. Every new game night triggered an automatic update. The infrastructure outlasted the session that created it.

### NFL — Clutch Analysis

The American football project was the most MATLAB-native. Clutch play analysis — identifying which plays in which moments had the highest impact on game outcomes. I built it as a MATLAB toolbox, complete with documentation and examples. It was the kind of niche analytical tool that a few hundred sports analytics enthusiasts might find useful, and that I'd never have built if the cost of building it had been measured in weeks instead of evenings.

### Cycling — The Ghost That Kept Running

And then there was cycling. GhostRunners.app from Chapter 1 — the ride comparison tool built with MATLAB Mobile data capture. The clearest example of "kept growing" in the entire portfolio.

In the pre-Claude era, GhostRunners was a functional but rough prototype. MATLAB Mobile captured the data, MATLAB Online crunched the analysis, Python handled the Garmin FIT file parsing. It worked. I recorded YouTube videos about it. And then it sat there.

With Claude, I came back to it. The MCP server connected MATLAB to the broader workflow. The CLI patterns from the philosopher tools gave me a structure for the user interface. The CI/CD learnings from the NBA project meant I could automate the data pipeline. Each previous project had deposited a capability that GhostRunners could now draw on.

The ghost was still running. But now it was running faster.

### The Pattern

Here's what the YouTube channel taught me: **the marginal cost of exploration had dropped to near zero.**

Each video required learning a new domain — tennis statistics, NBA data APIs, NFL play-by-play formats, cycling biomechanics. In the old world, the domain learning alone would have consumed weeks. With Claude as a research and implementation partner, I could go from "I know nothing about ELO ratings" to "here's a working prediction system" in an evening or two.

Five sports. Five stacks. One year. The channel wasn't just documenting what I built. It was proving, video by video, that the barrier to entry for any technical domain had collapsed. Not to zero — you still needed taste, judgment, and the willingness to learn. But the activation energy had dropped from "this will take months, is it worth it?" to "let's find out this weekend."

## Gaining Back Control Over the Algorithm

The music project started with an itch.

If you use Spotify — and statistically, you probably do — you've experienced this: the algorithm decides what you like. It feeds you recommendations based on your listening history, and over time, your taste and the algorithm's model of your taste begin to converge. You listen to what it suggests, which reinforces its model, which narrows its suggestions, which narrows what you listen to. The feedback loop is invisible and relentless.

I didn't hate Spotify's recommendations. Some of them were genuinely good. But I resented the passivity of it. My music taste was becoming a mirror of an algorithm's assumptions about me, and I wanted to break out of that loop.

### The Architecture
