The idea was simple in concept, ambitious in scope: build a personal music recommendation system using Spotify's own audio features — energy, acousticness, danceability, valence, tempo — but with *my* logic instead of *their* algorithm.

The core engine was a K-nearest neighbors (KNN) model. Take a song I loved, find the songs in my library that were most similar across those audio features, and surface the ones I'd forgotten about or never noticed. Not discovery of new music (Spotify already does that well) but rediscovery of music I already owned and had lost in the algorithmic shuffle.

Claude helped me design the system architecture and integrate the Spotify API. The data pipeline pulled my listening history and audio features for every track. The KNN model ran locally — no cloud dependency, no algorithm I didn't control. The recommendations were mine, based on rules I could see and modify.

### Form Factors

This is where the project got interesting. Once the recommendation engine existed, the question became: how do you interact with it?

I explored four different form factors, each one a different answer to that question:

**Web app** — the obvious first choice. A clean interface where you pick a seed song and see recommendations. Good for exploration, but it required a browser and a server.

**CLI tool** — because by this point, everything I built had a CLI. Type a song name, get recommendations in the terminal. Fast, scriptable, composable with other tools. The philosopher tool ethos applied to music.

**Desktop app** — for a more visual experience. Album art, audio previews, the kind of rich interface that a terminal can't provide.

**Hybrid** — the most ambitious version. A CLI that could launch a lightweight web interface when you wanted the visual experience, but stayed in the terminal when you wanted speed. The best of both worlds, if you could stomach the complexity.

Each form factor taught me something different about interface design. But the lesson that stuck was this: having the recommendation engine as a separate, well-defined module meant I could experiment with interfaces freely. The tool-first approach from Chapter 3 — building the engine before the interface — paid dividends I hadn't anticipated.

### Use Cases That Multiplied

The recommendation engine was the seed, but the use cases kept branching:

**Local music management** — I had years of downloaded music scattered across hard drives. The same audio feature analysis that powered recommendations could also organize, tag, and deduplicate my local collection.

**Personal Spotify Wrapped** — Spotify's annual summary is fun but superficial. With raw access to my listening data and audio features, I could build something deeper: trends in my taste over time, the acoustic signature of my moods, the genres I gravitated to during specific seasons.

**Mood-based playlists** — Instead of Spotify's generic "Chill" or "Focus" categories, playlists generated from my own definition of what those moods sounded like, calibrated to my library, my features, my thresholds.

### The Philosophical Resonance

Here's where the project connected back to something deeper.

In Chapter 3, the existentialist synthesis emerged: *"The existentialists ask 'how should I live authentically?' while Mollick and Harari ask 'how should we live with AI?' These questions are not separate."* The philosopher tools were answers to philosophical questions encoded in code.

The music project was the same impulse, just in a different domain. Building your own recommendation algorithm is an act of self-determination. It says: I refuse to let a platform decide what I like. I'll examine my own taste, build my own model of it, and use that model to rediscover what I've been missing. Socrates meets Spotify. The examined playlist is worth listening to.

> "Gaining back control over the algorithm isn't about rejecting technology. It's about choosing which technology reflects who you are instead of who a platform thinks you should be."

This wasn't just a music project. It was the book's thesis — AI as amplifier of human intention — applied to the most personal domain I could think of: what sounds fill your house on a Sunday morning.

## The Portfolio Effect

Let me step back and look at what had happened.

In Chapter 1, I described a pattern: *"Every conversation started from zero. Every project was an island."* recettesdezoe.com was a single-session creation. GhostRunners was a rough prototype that worked but couldn't grow. The YouTube channel was a series of disconnected experiments. Each project lived in its own bubble, with no shared infrastructure, no common patterns, no way for one to feed into another.

By the time I'd reached the projects in this chapter, that had changed entirely. The projects weren't islands anymore — they were nodes in a network.

recettesdezoe.com drew on the competitive intelligence patterns from Chapter 4 for its market analysis, on the voice preservation techniques from Chapter 2 for its content standard, and on the "build tools, not skills" philosophy from Chapter 3 for the Zoé CLI. The YouTube sports projects cross-pollinated: CI/CD from the NBA project fed into GhostRunners, MATLAB toolbox patterns from the NFL project informed the cycling analysis, and every project deposited knowledge that the next one could draw on. The music recommendation app used the same CLI architecture as the philosopher tools, the same research methodology as the competitive analysis, and connected back to the existentialist themes that ran through the whole journey.

The infrastructure chapters — the philosopher tools, the competitive intelligence machine, the MCP revolution — hadn't just produced outputs. They had produced a *way of working*. A set of patterns and capabilities that made every subsequent project cheaper, faster, and more connected than it would have been in isolation.

This is the portfolio effect. Not a planned portfolio — I never sat down and said "I'm going to build an interconnected network of side projects." It emerged from the compounding of tools, patterns, and knowledge across dozens of conversations with Claude. Each project made the next one possible. Each tool reduced the cost of the next tool. Each experiment deposited a capability that future experiments could inherit.

I want to be honest about the limits. Most of these projects are still side projects. recettesdezoe.com isn't generating revenue. The YouTube channel has a modest audience. The music app is something I use personally. None of this is a startup success story.

But that's precisely the point. The transformation wasn't about commercial outcomes — it was about the *cost of exploration*. When it takes weeks to build something, you have to be strategic about what you build. You plan, you evaluate, you calculate the expected return on investment. When it takes an evening, you can afford to follow curiosity wherever it leads.

> "When exploration becomes cheap, you stop asking 'is this worth my time?' and start asking 'what am I curious about?'"

That shift — from strategic allocation of scarce building capacity to abundant, curiosity-driven exploration — is, I think, the most underappreciated consequence of working with an AI collaborator. The projects themselves matter less than the fact that they exist at all. Each one is evidence that the barrier between "I wonder if..." and "let's find out" has been nearly erased.

## What the Side Projects Taught Me

The side projects taught me something that the work projects couldn't.

At MathWorks, the value of Claude was measurable — faster blog posts, sharper competitive analyses, better presentations. The ROI story was clear and presentable. But the side projects operated outside the logic of ROI. Nobody asked me to build a recipe website or a music recommendation engine. Nobody was tracking the output. The only metric was whether I wanted to keep going.

And I kept going. That was the data point that mattered.

Because when you strip away the professional context — the reporting obligations, the pilot program requirements, the need to justify your tools budget — what you're left with is the raw question: *Do I want to spend my evenings doing this?* And the answer, project after project, evening after evening, was yes.

There's a specific kind of knowledge that comes from building things nobody asked you to build. Work projects teach you what's possible. Side projects teach you what's *exciting*. And when the time came to explain this journey to colleagues — to stand in front of a room and make the case that something fundamental had changed — I needed both kinds of knowledge. The work projects gave me the evidence: productivity gains, workflow improvements, measurable outputs. But the side projects gave me the conviction. The ability to say, with genuine enthusiasm and not just professional obligation, that this technology had changed how I spent my free time. That I *chose* to be in the terminal at 11pm on a Tuesday, not because anyone was watching, but because the next experiment was calling.

That conviction was about to matter more than I expected. Over the following weeks, I'd present to three different teams at MathWorks, each time refining the message, each time drawing on both the professional evidence and the personal story. The internal evangelism campaign was about to begin — and with it, a new challenge: translating a felt experience into something that could change other people's minds.

But that's the next chapter.

\newpage

# Chapter 7: Writing the Future — Sharing Knowledge

By mid-January 2026, I had been using Claude Code for about six weeks. I'd built tools, sharpened strategies, drafted blog posts at a pace that surprised even me, and spent more late nights in the terminal than I probably should have. But now I faced a challenge that no amount of agentic wizardry could automate: explaining what I'd found to colleagues who hadn't experienced it.

The pilot had a simple requirement — report back on your usage in Q1. What started as a reporting obligation became something I hadn't anticipated: an internal evangelism campaign.

You might know the feeling if you are passionate about tech. You discover something that changes how you work, and suddenly you can't stop talking about it. The problem is that enthusiasm without structure is just noise. I needed to show, not tell. And I needed to tailor the message to audiences who cared about very different things.

## The Presentation That Kept Evolving

The story of these presentations actually begins in September 2025, months before Claude Code entered my life (remember chapter 1). My dear colleague Jacob George and I had co-created this deck called "GenAI Tools for Modern Tech Marketers" — a practical overview of how marketers could use AI tools to speed up their workflows. It was a solid deck. Useful. Conventional.

By January, that deck had been transformed beyond recognition. Most of the original slides were gone. In their place was something called "Agentic Learnings" — and over the next six weeks, I would rewrite it every week, each time for a different audience. Each version wasn't just a reshuffled slide order. Each one reflected genuine learning, additional weeks of experience, and a deeper understanding of what mattered to the people in the room.

### Outbound Marketing — The Workflow Version

The first presentation landed on January 15th, Week 2 of the new year. I presented to the outbound marketing team, together with Eric, the head of product management for MATLAB. The subtitle said it plainly: "Learnings after a few weeks of Claude Code."

I organized everything into three categories that would carry through all subsequent presentations:

**Agentic MVP** — building apps and prototypes. I posed the question the audience was probably thinking: "MVP for Minimal Viable Product or Minimal Viable Prototype?" The ambiguity was intentional. This wasn't just Vibe coding web sites anymore. It was architecture work, guiding a senior developer (Claude).

**Agentic Digital Marketing** — automating the boring steps of the blogging workflow so you can concentrate on ideation and code writing. I showed the workflow diagram: Claude handles formatting, structuring, and first-pass drafts. The human focuses on ideas and technical content.

**Agentic Market Intelligence** — this was the one that made people lean forward. I showed the PIDO example: Process Integration and Design Optimization market analysis from a single prompt. The output? A comprehensive analysis: "$200M USD market in 2024, growing at 10% CAGR," with strategic implications included. One prompt. What used to take a week of research distilled into minutes.

The outbound team needed concrete before-and-after comparisons of their daily work — blogging, competitive analysis, community monitoring. So that's what I gave them. This was the most workflow-oriented of the three decks.

### Industry Marketing — The Technically Ambitious Version

A week later, same core material, completely different emphasis. The industry marketing team needed to understand the competitive landscape — that was their job, after all. So I added depth.

A new category appeared that hadn't existed in the outbound deck. **Agentic Development** showed Claude Code porting a Python package to MATLAB — a technical use case proving this wasn't just a marketing productivity tool.

Then some more non-coding related use cases. The deep research examples were chosen for strategic relevance. I showed a neuroscience community toolbox analysis — FieldTrip, EEGLAB, MNE-Python. The research revealed a bifurcation: MATLAB-based tools historically dominant, Python-based alternatives rapidly growing with zero licensing costs. EEGLAB leading citations at 21,700+, MNE-Python showing the fastest growth. That kind of intelligence was directly actionable for an academic disciplines and industry marketing team deciding where to invest.

The weekly learnings section had evolved too. By Week 3, the slides carried three layers of accumulating insight — from Agentic 101 through tool-building to multi-agent orchestration. The audience could see the learning curve accelerating in real time.

### Educator Programs — The One Built from Scratch

By Week 6, I didn't just update the deck. I rebuilt it entirely.

The educator audience cared about teaching content creation, forum support, and MATLAB live script generation. The generic "blogging workflow" and "market intelligence" sections from earlier decks? Gone. Replaced with education-specific use cases: creating content for a linear algebra workshop at the University of Oklahoma, analyzing community toolboxes for academic adoption, generating teaching materials that could scale.

This was the most audience-adapted version. And it was the one where something unexpected happened.

## The Learning Curve in Four Weeks

Each version of the deck included a "weekly learnings" section — a running log of what I'd discovered since the last presentation. What started as a simple bullet list became, without my planning it, a narrative of transformation. Four weeks, four stages.

**Week 1: Agentic 101.** Start with Claude Desktop. Get a taste of the agentic capabilities — the ability to read and write directly on the file system. Discover a few concepts: skills, tools, the Model Context Protocol. See how this applies to coding. Begin with Python, then connect to MATLAB via the MCP Core Server. This was the foundation — understanding that an AI agent isn't just a chatbot with delusions of grandeur, but a system that can use tools autonomously. When I presented this to the outbound team, it was already two weeks old. But for most people in the room, it was new. That gap — between where I was and where my audience was — would widen with every passing week.

**Week 2: Density — less is more.** Focus on just the necessary context. Filter and condense the information you need. The crucial insight: build tools, not just skills. CLI tools can be used by your agents and follow deterministic behavior — a workflow for slide deck creation, information extraction from YouTube videos. This is when the army of philosophers was born: Montaigne, Nietzsche, Schopenhauer. Claude generating GitHub Issues to manage requirements. Tests validated automatically through GitHub Actions. When a test failed, ask the GitHub MCP server to retrieve the trace — no more copy-pasting error logs. `uv` is life. Python for CLI is fire. Publish to PyPI — let the game begin. If Week 1 was about learning what agents could do, Week 2 was about building things that agents could use. The shift from consumer to creator.

**Week 3: Agents calling agents.** Beyond "agents calling tools" — the MCP thesis — a more advanced pattern emerged: Claude orchestrating Codex and Gemini CLI. Deep research workflows inspired by NetworkChuck — sending multiple agents down different research paths and synthesizing the results. This wasn't just tool use anymore. This was orchestration. The conductor stepping up from the orchestra pit. When I showed this slide to the industry marketing team, I could see the moment it clicked for some of them — and the moment it overwhelmed others. The gap between "I use ChatGPT for emails" and "I orchestrate multiple AI agents in parallel" was wider than anyone had expected.

**Week 4: The terminal is my happy place.** Building CLIs for Claude and for myself. TUIs — terminal user interfaces, yes, very nerdy. Even SSH apps that could be accessed remotely. The terminal had become home. Not the IDE, not the browser, not the chat window — the terminal. For a non-developer who started this journey in a ChatGPT text box three months earlier, landing in the terminal and feeling comfortable there was perhaps the most telling sign that something fundamental had shifted.

The progression told a story I hadn't planned but couldn't deny:

| Week | Theme | Identity |
|------|-------|----------|
| 1 | Agentic 101 | Consumer |
| 2 | Density — less is more | Creator |
| 3 | Agents calling agents | Conductor |
| 4 | The terminal is my happy place | Native |

Each stage wasn't just a skill upgrade — it was an identity shift. And each presentation caught me at a different point on that curve, which is why no two versions of the deck told the same story. The audience mattered, yes. But so did where I was on the journey. The person who presented Agentic 101 in Week 2 was not the same person who rebuilt the entire deck for educators in Week 6. The tool had changed me, and the presentations were the evidence.

## The Live Demo That Changed Minds

The educator meeting had about seven people — Stefano, Jeff, Maria Elena, Will, Nandini, and Ascension joining remotely. I had just walked through the PCA-to-video pipeline: MATLAB script, Claude Code generating content, export to Word, feed into NotebookLM for slides, AI-generated voiceover. Total pipeline time: under 30 minutes. I added the honest caveat: "People don't like the AI-generated voice. So that's the part you should probably get rid of. But the rest is actually quite interesting."

Then Ascension asked the question that mattered: "What is the agentic part about this?"

She wasn't being difficult. She was being precise. She wasn't convinced this was different from regular generative AI — from the ChatGPT-era copy-paste workflows we'd all been doing. And she was right to push. If I couldn't explain the distinction clearly, the whole presentation was just a show.

So I did something I hadn't planned. I opened Claude Code. Live. Unrehearsed.

I typed `claude` in the terminal and prompted: "Look at the linear algebra folder and the existing examples. Now do the same for Fourier analysis."

What happened next was the kind of thing you can't fake. Claude Code autonomously explored the folder structure, read the existing MATLAB live scripts, learned the format and conventions from the examples, and generated new Fourier analysis content — code, documentation, everything. It used three or four tools on its own, making decisions about what to read, what to generate, and how to structure the output.

I used the Matrix analogy to explain what we'd just seen: "I don't know if some of you saw the movie The Matrix... 'I know Kung Fu,' right? Like he plugs himself into the skill and then he knows." Teaching Claude how to write MATLAB live scripts through structured documentation — that was the agentic part. Not just generating text, but autonomously learning and applying a format.

> "It's a spectacle. Like this just now. It turns into a performance. This thing is insane."

And the practical advice I gave right after: "Always read the code."

Ascension's arc during that meeting was the most rewarding part. She started skeptical — "Not yet, Yann. But I'm sure you will impress me." She ended engaged, seeing the potential for scaling educational content production on GitHub and File Exchange. The live demo did what slides never could.

## The Blog Post Feedback Loop

Around the same time, a parallel conversation was shaping how I thought about all of this.

A colleague had given me feedback on a blog post draft — the one about automating documentation with the MCP Core Server. Their response cut right to the core:

> "Based on the first paragraph, it sounded like you were going to show me how to take an existing script/analysis and get that ready for GitHub. But the workflow below starts from scratch and delegates the analysis to the LLM as well. I actually really liked the idea that you were going with in the first paragraph — do we have like an initial version of the script that we can start from? That way it's still the human doing the 'interesting' parts, and then you're using the AI to do the 'tedious' parts?"

I read that three times. It crystallized something I'd been circling around in every presentation, every demo, every late-night terminal session: **the human does the interesting parts, AI handles the tedious parts.** Not AI replacing humans. AI as amplifier.

This wasn't a new idea — but hearing it reflected back from a colleague who had independently arrived at the same framing made it feel like a thesis, not just a hunch. It connected the presentations back to the book's central question: what is this relationship, actually?

## The Book Club — Co-Intelligence as Mirror

If the presentations were about showing what Claude Code could do, the Marketing Book Club was about wrestling with what it all meant.

Kyle, Jacob, and I co-organized the club around Ethan Mollick's "Co-Intelligence." The choice was deliberate — Mollick's book explores practical frameworks for human-AI collaboration, and I'd re-listened to the audiobook recently. It was still remarkably relevant, even though it had been written before the agentic AI wave arrived.

The first session — Introduction and Chapter 1 — went places I didn't expect.

Mollick opens with the concept of "Sleepless Nights" — that first visceral encounter with AI that keeps you up. The participants shared their own moments. Kyle had perhaps the most striking one: someone had replicated six months of his market analysis work with a single deep research prompt in thirty minutes. "70% of the way there," he said. His reaction was immediate: "I was like, well, I'm out of a job."

Six months of work. Thirty minutes. Seventy percent. Those numbers tell a story that no slide deck can.

The discussion spiraled into territory that felt more like philosophy than a marketing meeting. Mollick's "Alien Mind" metaphor — AI as fundamentally non-human intelligence — resonated with what I'd been experiencing. AI doesn't think like us, but that's exactly what makes it a powerful thinking partner. If it thought like us, it would have the same blind spots.

I showed them ChatGPT's memory feature, which described me in absurdly flattering terms — "deep technical," endlessly praiseworthy. The room laughed. This was sycophancy in action, the AI telling you what you want to hear. I contrasted it with my experience of Claude pushing back on my strategic memo in the first week. The difference mattered.

Then I introduced Jean Claude — my personal AI agent on Telegram, complete with a French accent. I called the concept "productive anthropomorphism." Giving AI a persona and a name isn't a cognitive trap — it makes collaboration more intuitive, more natural. You don't anthropomorphize your spreadsheet, but maybe you should anthropomorphize your thinking partner.

The most substantive exchange came when I posed a question I'd been sitting with for weeks:

> "When AI starts writing good code, what is the part of your identity that you feel is threatened, if any?"

David Barnes paused. "I really like that articulation. About somewhat of the identity. It's not something I've thought about before."

I tried to frame it honestly: "If intelligence is just so cheap, so accessible, so scalable, what is me?... It's not necessarily insecurity, it's evolution."

I described my personal timeline as having three eras — the OpenAI days, "Google is so back," and now Claude Code. "There's BC — Before ChatGPT. Now there's also BCC — Before Claude Code."

The session ended with a message I'd been building toward across all the presentations and conversations: "If you can think of it as an AND — if we can do for humans AND for AI, then maybe we have something here." MathWorks should serve both humans and AI agents. Not zero-sum. AND.

## Resistance and Real Concerns

I'd be painting a dishonest picture if I suggested everyone walked out of these sessions converted. They didn't. And the pushback wasn't ignorance — it was legitimate concern.

Jeff raised the desktop-versus-cloud tension that ran through the company like a fault line. Agentic tools needed desktop software installed to be most effective, but the education market was demanding a cloud-first experience. That wasn't a problem I could demo my way out of.

The cost conversation came up in every session. "With great powers come great consumptions," I'd say, and it always got a knowing laugh. The $200-a-month subscription with daily limits was real. I was honest about it: "I have a 5-hour window where I start in the morning and then until 2... at some point I'm eating 100% and then I cannot work anymore. I need to go and chat with colleagues."

The governance gray areas were real too. What could AI-generated content be used for internally? Externally? Where was the line? "Very gray area" was the consensus, and nobody had a satisfying answer.

And then there was the elephant in the room that I tried to address head-on: "This is a controversial topic that puts pressure on our very business model." If AI could write MATLAB code, what happened to the value proposition of MATLAB itself? I didn't pretend to have the answer. But I did tell the room: "Don't shoot the messenger. I'm just sharing what's currently out there in the market."

These concerns weren't obstacles to adoption. They were evidence that the conversations mattered. Easy ideas don't generate pushback. Only ideas that challenge the status quo do.

The governance question, in particular, hung over every session like a fog that nobody could quite disperse. What could AI-generated content be used for internally? What about externally? If Claude drafted a competitive analysis, could it go into a customer-facing presentation? If it generated MATLAB code examples, who owned the intellectual property? "Very gray area" was the consensus, and nobody — not in marketing, not in legal, not anywhere I could find — had a satisfying answer yet. The honest response was that we were all figuring it out together, in real time, and pretending otherwise would have been the worst kind of evangelism.

> "The question you need to ask yourself now is not what it can do, but what it cannot do."

## From User to Advocate

Looking back at those six weeks, the trajectory surprises me. I walked into the pilot as someone who'd been asked to evaluate a tool. I walked out as something closer to an internal organizer.

At the end of the educator meeting, after Ascension's skepticism had turned to engagement and the team had seen Claude Code work in real time, I proposed creating a new Gen AI Tiger Team specifically for Educator Programs. Not a vague "let's keep talking about this" — a concrete team with scheduled sessions, a backlog of use cases to explore, and a commitment to document what worked and what didn't. The room said yes. We discussed follow-up sessions for the remaining use cases: community toolboxes, conference report-outs, and more.

That proposal was one piece of a larger pattern. I was also co-leading the Marketing Book Club on human-AI collaboration, creating onboarding materials for other MathWorkers, and sharing internal tools — the MATLAB Central MCP server, the skills repos, the philosopher-named tool chain.

The presentations evolved because I evolved. Week 2 was about workflows. Week 3 was about technical possibilities. Week 6 was about meeting people where they were. The book club was about grappling with what it all meant for who we are and what we do.

What started as "report back on your usage" became something more: an argument that we needed to surf on the wave, because otherwise we were going to get crushed.

But here's the thing I didn't expect. The most valuable part of having a thinking partnership wasn't what it did for me — it was what it taught me to share. Every demo, every slide, every uncomfortable question from a skeptical colleague pushed me to understand the technology more deeply, articulate its implications more clearly, and confront its limitations more honestly.

The evangelist doesn't just spread the word. The evangelist refines it.

And I was about to refine it further — in the most intense way possible. The hackathon was coming.

\newpage

# Chapter 8: What This Relationship Actually Is

Three months. That's how long this story spans — December 2025 to February 2026. In the arc of a career, it's nothing. In the arc of what happened, it's everything.

I started this book with a simple sentence: "GPTs were a thing, and then Claude Code arrived." Now I want to try to explain what that sentence actually means. Not the technology. Not the features. What it means for the person sitting at the keyboard.

## The Five Modes

Looking back across all those weeks — the late nights in the terminal, the presentations that kept evolving, the tools named after philosophers, the competitive intelligence that rewired how I thought about research — a pattern emerges. Claude wasn't one thing. It was five things, and the relationship shifted between them constantly.

**Claude as strategic thinking partner.** This was the first mode I discovered, and in some ways the most surprising. I didn't come to Claude expecting intellectual sparring. I came expecting a faster way to write memos. What I got was an entity that fact-checked my market claims, stress-tested my arguments, and told me where my strategic logic had gaps. The "agent calling toolboxes" positioning wasn't Claude's idea or mine — it was forged in dialogue, each of us pushing back on the other until the argument was sharp enough to present to a director.

**Claude as creative collaborator.** The philosopher-themed tools. The Zarathustra voiceover script. The rap battle concept. The recipe site content strategy. None of these emerged from a single prompt. They emerged from iterative conversation — one idea feeding the next, building creative momentum that neither of us could have generated alone. Claude didn't create for me. Claude created *with* me, and the distinction matters more than I expected.

**Claude as research engine.** The competitive intelligence work changed how I understood my own job. I brought context and strategic judgment. Claude brought breadth and synthesis. The PIDO analysis, the neuroscience toolbox landscape, the MATLAB community intelligence — each one followed the same pattern: Claude's breadth plus my judgment, iterated through dialogue until we arrived at something genuinely useful. 70% of the way there in thirty minutes, and then my expertise closing the remaining distance.

**Claude as writing partner.** My blog voice was analyzed, codified into a skill, and then used to draft posts that sounded like me — not a generic approximation, but specifically like me. My rhythms, my habit of acknowledging what I don't know, my tendency to open with a shared experience. A tool that makes you faster is useful. A tool that learns your voice and helps you sound more like yourself — that's something else.

**Claude as builder.** Presentations, documents, web apps, architecture designs, onboarding materials, CLI tools published to PyPI, MCP servers deployed and running. Actual artifacts. Not drafts or suggestions — working software and finished documents that existed in the world and did useful things.

Five modes. One relationship. And the most interesting part is that I rarely chose which mode to operate in. The conversation would shift naturally — from strategy to research to building to writing — and Claude would shift with it. There was no mode switch, no "now I'm your research assistant." The transitions were seamless because the underlying dynamic was consistent: two intelligences working on a shared problem, each contributing what the other couldn't.

## What Makes This Different

The introduction to this book established a before and after. Before Claude Code, there was the ChatGPT era — single interactions, disposable sessions, no continuity, no memory. Every conversation started from zero. Every project was an island.

The difference isn't just capability. It's the nature of the interaction.

**Continuity.** Each conversation built on the ones before it. When I mentioned the "agent calling toolboxes" strategy in Week 3, Claude knew what I meant because we'd developed it together in Week 1. When I referenced my blog voice, Claude could apply it because it had analyzed it weeks ago. The shared context grew with every exchange, and that accumulation made each new conversation more productive than the last.

**Honest feedback.** Claude didn't just agree with me. It pushed back. It identified weaknesses in my arguments. It suggested alternatives I hadn't considered. And it did all of this without the social dynamics that make human feedback complicated — no ego, no office politics, no tiptoeing around seniority. I could share a half-formed idea at midnight and get direct, substantive feedback without worrying about who I was imposing on.

**Mutual shaping.** My ideas shaped Claude's outputs, obviously — I was the one asking the questions and setting the direction. But Claude's responses shaped my thinking too. The competitive intelligence work didn't just produce reports — it changed how I understood market analysis. The philosopher tools didn't just automate presentation creation — they changed how I thought about the relationship between my values and my work. The influence ran both ways.

These three qualities — continuity, honest feedback, mutual shaping — are what separate a collaboration from a transaction. ChatGPT was transactional. You put a prompt in, you got an output, you moved on. Claude was collaborative. The relationship had history, direction, and cumulative depth.

## The Identity Question

There's a question that surfaced during the Marketing Book Club and that I've been carrying ever since:

> "When AI starts writing good code, what is the part of your identity that you feel is threatened, if any?"

David Barnes paused when I asked this. "I really like that articulation. About somewhat of the identity. It's not something I've thought about before."

I've thought about it a lot. Here's where I've landed.

If intelligence is cheap — so accessible, so scalable, so available at 2am — then what is *me*? What's the distinctly human contribution when the machine can research faster, write cleaner first drafts, and explore more options in parallel than I ever could?

The answer, I think, is judgment. Taste. Values. The ability to look at a technically correct output and say "that's not what matters here." The ability to name your tools after philosophers because you believe the things you build should carry meaning beyond their function. The ability to read a competitive analysis and know, from years of customer conversations, which insights are strategically relevant and which are noise.

Kyle's story haunts me in a productive way. Six months of market analysis replicated in thirty minutes. Seventy percent of the way there. But that remaining thirty percent — the part that requires lived experience, institutional knowledge, strategic intuition — that's not the leftover crumbs. That's the meal. The seventy percent is the plate it's served on.

This isn't insecurity. It's evolution. The product manager who spent 80% of their time gathering information and 20% thinking now spends 20% gathering and 80% thinking. The bottleneck has shifted. And the new bottleneck — judgment — is the part that's genuinely, stubbornly human.

## Productive Anthropomorphism

I gave my personal AI agent a name — Jean Claude — and a French accent. I named my tools after philosophers. I used the word "colleague" to describe an AI system. Some people would call this naive. Others would call it dangerous — projecting humanity onto a machine, setting yourself up for disappointment.

I call it productive anthropomorphism.

Here's the thing: the way we relate to our tools shapes how effectively we use them. Nobody anthropomorphizes their spreadsheet, and that's appropriate — a spreadsheet is a grid of cells that does math. But Claude isn't a spreadsheet. Claude is a system you have conversations with, that remembers context, that pushes back on your ideas, that generates creative output you didn't expect. The interface *is* conversational. Treating it as purely mechanical — "input prompt, receive output" — actually makes you worse at using it.

When I named a tool "ricoeur.cc," I wasn't pretending the tool was Paul Ricoeur. I was encoding a set of values — about interpretation, about the hermeneutic circle, about how we understand things — into the tool's identity. That encoding made the tool more meaningful to me, which made me more thoughtful about how I used it. The anthropomorphism wasn't a bug. It was a feature.

## The Honest Accounting

I want to end with honesty, because honesty is what set this relationship apart from the beginning.

**What Claude is good at:** Breadth. Speed. Consistency. First drafts. Research synthesis. Code generation. Pattern matching across large bodies of information. Tireless iteration. Availability at 2am without complaint.

**What Claude is not good at:** Insider knowledge. Political nuance. The kind of judgment that comes from sitting in a thousand customer meetings. Knowing when a technically correct answer is strategically wrong. Understanding that sometimes the right response to a colleague's question isn't the most accurate answer but the most diplomatic one. Taste — that ineffable sense of what matters and what doesn't.

**What surprised me:** The honest feedback. I expected helpfulness. I got intellectual partnership. The difference between Claude pushing back on my strategic memo and ChatGPT telling me it was great — that single contrast shaped everything that followed.

**What concerns me:** The speed of change. The governance gray areas that nobody has satisfying answers to yet. The pressure this puts on business models — including the one that pays my salary. The cost — "with great powers come great consumptions" — and the daily limits that remind you this technology is still in its infrastructure-constrained adolescence.

**What I believe:** That this is an AND, not an OR. We can build for humans AND for AI. The tools we build can carry our values AND be technically excellent. The relationship between human and AI can be productive AND honest about its limitations. The future belongs to people who can hold both sides of that AND without collapsing into either extreme.

## Where This Goes

I started this journey as a product manager evaluating a tool. I ended it as something I don't have a clean title for. An evangelist? An internal organizer? A person who builds tools named after philosophers and presents them to skeptical colleagues?

Maybe the simplest description is: a person who found a thinking partner and took the partnership seriously.

The pilot had a simple requirement — report back on your usage in Q1. What I'm reporting back is this: Claude Code isn't a productivity tool. It's not a coding assistant. It's not a chatbot with better features. It's the first AI system I've used where the word "collaboration" isn't a euphemism for "I type and it generates." The collaboration is real. The influence is mutual. The relationship has history and direction.

Three months is not a long time. But three months with a thinking partner who's available at 2am, who remembers where you left off, who pushes back when you're wrong, and who helps you build things that encode your values into working software — three months like that can change how you think about what's possible.

There's BC — Before ChatGPT. There's BCC — Before Claude Code. And now there's whatever comes next.

I don't know what to call this era yet. But I know the tools I'll build in it will carry the names of philosophers, and the conversations that shape them will be honest ones.

> "The tools we build shape the lives we lead."

Happy hacking, and let me know what you build! 👋🏻

The font size comes from Pandoc's default styling, not from the reference doc. Pandoc uses reference docs for style definitions (font family, colors, paragraph spacing) but applies its own defaults for some properties — and Pandoc's default Normal style uses 12pt.

Assistant2026-02-23T00:34:12.735Z


There's the answer. The reference doc's Normal style has no explicit font size (None) — it inherits from the theme/defaults. When Pandoc reads this, it falls back to its own default of 12pt.


The fix is to set the Normal style in the reference doc to 10pt explicitly. Let me create a patched copy.


Assistant2026-02-23T00:34:15.246Z




$ Bash


Create patched reference doc with 10pt Normal style
cd /c/Users/ydebray/Downloads/schopenhauer && python -c "
from docx import Document
from docx.shared import Pt
import shutil

# Copy the reference doc
src = 'C:/Users/ydebray/Downloads/claude-code-book/resources/Programming GPTs - Book - chap1.docx'
dst = 'templates/reference-docs/book.docx'
shutil.copy2(src, dst)

# Patch Normal style to 10pt
doc = Document(dst)
style = doc.styles['Normal']
style.font.size = Pt(10)
doc.save(dst)
print('Patched Normal style to 10pt in templates/reference-docs/book.docx')
" 2>&1
